3
Preliminaries

We use
[n]
to denote the set
\{1,2,\ldots n\}
and
[a,b]
(where
a<b
) to denote the set
\{a,a+1,\ldots b\}
. We use
{\cal H}{\cal W}(x)
to denote the Hamming weight of a binary string
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, and
\omega(x)
to denote its
relative
Hamming weight
{\cal H}{\cal W}(x)/|x|
. For two strings
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, we use
\Delta(x,y)=\omega(x\oplus y)
to denote the relative Hamming distance of
x,y
. For finite sets
X,Y
, let
F_{X\to Y}
be the set of functions with domain
X
and codomain
Y
. For a set
T\subseteq[n]
,
\{i\}_{i\in T}
is used to represent a set indexed by
T
.
Let
h_{b}(x)
denote the binary entropy function,
h_{b}(x)=-x\log_{2}(x)-(1-x)\log_{2}(1-x)
. We make use of the well-known fact that the number of strings of length
n
with relative Hamming weight at most
\delta
is
\leq 2^{h_{b}(\delta)n}
.

3.1
Quantum preliminaries

A register
{\cal X}
is a named Hilbert space
{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{n}}
. A pure quantum state on register
{\cal X}
is a unit vector
\ket{\psi}_{\cal X}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{n}}
, and we say that
\ket{\psi}_{\cal X}
consists of
n
qubits. A mixed state on register
{\cal X}
is described by a density matrix
\rho_{\cal X}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}
, which is a positive semi-definite Hermitian operator with trace 1.

A quantum operation
F
is a completely-positive trace-preserving (CPTP) map from a register
{\cal X}
to a register
{\cal Y}
, which in general may have different dimensions. That is, on input a density matrix
\rho_{\cal X}
, the operation
F
produces
F(\rho_{\cal X})=\tau_{\cal Y}
a mixed state on register
{\cal Y}
. A
unitary

U:{\cal X}\to{\cal X}
is a special case of a quantum operation that satisfies
U^{\dagger}U=UU^{\dagger}={\mathbb{I}}_{\cal X}
, where
{\mathbb{I}}_{\cal X}
is the identity matrix on register
{\cal X}
. A
projector

\Pi
is a Hermitian operator such that
\Pi^{2}=\Pi
, and a
projective measurement
is a collection of projectors
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i}
such that
\sum_{i}\Pi_{i}={\mathbb{I}}
.

Let
\mathsf{Tr}
denote the trace operator. For registers
{\cal X},{\cal Y}
, the
partial trace

\mathsf{Tr}_{\cal Y}
is the unique operation from
{\cal X},{\cal Y}
to
{\cal X}
such that for all
\rho_{\cal X},\tau_{\cal Y}
,
\mathsf{Tr}_{{\cal Y}}(\rho,\tau)=\mathsf{Tr}(\tau)\rho
. The
trace distance
between states
\rho,\tau
, denoted
\mathsf{TD}(\rho,\tau)
is defined as

\mathsf{TD}(\rho,\tau)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\tau\|_{1}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\sqrt{(\rho-\tau)^{\dagger}(\rho-\tau)}\right).

We will often use the fact that the trace distance between two states
\rho
and
\tau
is an upper bound on the probability that any algorithm can distinguish
\rho
and
\tau
.

Lemma 3.1

(Gentle measurement
[
Win99
]
)
.

Let
\rho_{\cal X}
be a quantum state and let
(\Pi,{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi)
be a projective measurement on
{\cal X}
such that
\Tr(\Pi\rho)\geq 1-\delta
. Let

\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\Pi\rho\Pi}{\Tr(\Pi\rho)}

be the state after applying
(\Pi,{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi)
to
\rho
and post-selecting on obtaining the first outcome. Then,
\mathsf{TD}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\leq 2\sqrt{\delta}
.

Finally, we will make use of the convention that
+
denotes the computational basis
\{\ket{0},\ket{1}\}
and
\times
denotes the Hadamard basis
\left\{\frac{\ket{0}+\ket{1}}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{\ket{0}-\ket{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\right\}
. For a bit
r\in\{0,1\}
, we write
\ket{r}_{+}
to denote
r
encoded in the computational basis, and
\ket{r}_{\times}
to denote
r
encoded in the Hadamard basis.

3.2
Quantum machines and protocols

Quantum interactive machines.

A quantum interactive machine (QIM) is a family of machines
\{M_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where each
M_{\lambda}
consists of a sequence of quantum operations
M_{\lambda,1},\dots,M_{\lambda,\ell(\lambda)}
, where
\ell(\lambda)
is the number of rounds in which
M_{\lambda}
operates. Usually, we drop the indexing by
\lambda
and refer to the machine
M=M_{1},\dots,M_{\ell}
. Each machine
M_{i}
may have a designated input and output register used to communicate with its environment.

Quantum oracle machines.

Let
X,Y
be finite sets and let
O:X\to Y
be an arbitrary function. We say that
A^{O}
is a
q
-query
quantum oracle machine
(QOM) if it can be written as
A_{q+1}U_{O}A_{q}U_{O}\dots U_{O}A_{2}U_{O}A_{1}
, where
A_{1},\dots,A_{q+1}
are arbitrary quantum operations, and
U[O]
is the unitary defined by

U[O]:\ket{x}_{\cal X}\ket{y}_{\cal Y}\to\ket{x}_{\cal X}\ket{y \oplus O(x)}_{\cal Y},

operating on a designated oracle input register
{\cal X}
and oracle output register
{\cal Y}
. We say that
A
is a
quantum interactive oracle machine
(QIOM) if
A=A_{1}^{O},\dots,A_{\ell}^{O}
is such that each
A_{i}^{O}
is a quantum oracle machine.

Sometimes, it will be convenient to consider
controlled
queries to an oracle
O
, which would be implemented by a unitary

U_{c}[O]:\ket{b}_{\cal B}\ket{x}_{\cal X}\ket{y}_{\cal Y}\to\ket{b}_{\cal B}\ket{x}_{\cal X}\ket{y \oplus b \cdot O(x)}_{\cal Y}.

However, it is easy to see that such a controlled query can be implemented with two standard queries, by introducing an extra register
{\cal Z}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\ket{b}_{\cal B}\ket{x}_{{\cal X}}\ket{y}_{\cal Y}\ket{0}_{\cal Z}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{U[O_{0}]_{{\cal X},{\cal Z}}}\ket{b}_{\cal B}\ket{x}_{{\cal X}}\ket{y}_{\cal Y}\ket{O(x)}_{\cal Z}\to\ket{b}_{\cal B}\ket{x}_{{\cal X}}\ket{y \oplus b \cdot O(x)}_{\cal Y}\ket{O(x)}_{\cal Z}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{U[O_{0}]_{{\cal X},{\cal Z}}}\ket{b}_{\cal B}\ket{x}_{{\cal X}}\ket{y \oplus b \cdot O(x)}_{\cal Y}\ket{0}_{\cal Z}.

It will also be convenient to consider algorithms
A^{O_{0},O_{1}}
with access to
multiple
oracles
O_{0}:X_{0}\to Y_{0},O_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{1}
, written as
A_{q+1}U_{O_{1}}A_{q}U_{O_{0}}\dots U_{O_{1}}A_{2}U_{O_{0}}A_{1}
. Defining
O(b,x)=O_{b}(x)
for
(b,x)\in(0,X_{0})\cup(1,X_{1})
, it is easy to see that any
A^{O_{0},O_{1}}
can be written as an oracle algorithm
B^{O}
. On the other hand, given a
q
-query oracle algorithm
A^{O}
where
O:X\to Y
, and a partition of
X
into
(0,X^{\prime})\cup(1,X^{\prime})
, we can write
A
as a
4q
-query algorithm
B^{O_{0},O_{1}}
, where
O_{b}:X^{\prime}\to Y
is such that
O_{b}(x^{\prime})=O(b,x^{\prime})
. This follows by answering each query to
O
using one controlled query to
O_{0}
and one controlled query to
O_{1}
. This can be extended to splitting up an oracle
O
into
k
oracles, with a multiplicative factor of
2k
in the number of queries made by the adversary. Thus, throughout this work, we often consider machines that have access to multiple (potentially independently sampled) oracles, while noting that this model is equivalent to considering machines with access to a single oracle, up to a difference in the number of oracle queries. In particular, any adversarial algorithm that has superposition access to a single oracle
O
with an input space that can be partitioned into
k
parts may be written as an adversarial algorithm wth access to
k
appropriately defined separate oracles
O_{1},\dots,O_{k}
.

Functionalities and protocols in the quantum random oracle model.

Let
{\cal F}
denote a
functionality
, which is a classical interactive machine specifying the instructions to realize a cryptographic task. A two-party protocol
8
8

8

One can also consider multi-party protocols, but we restrict to the two-party setting in this work.

\Pi
for
{\cal F}
consists of two QIMs
(A,B)
.
9
9

9

Technically,
A
and
B
are infinite families of interactive machines, parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda
.
A protocol
\Pi
in the
quantum random oracle model
(QROM) consists of two
QIOMs

(A^{H},B^{H})
that have quantum oracle access to a uniformly random function
H
sampled from
F_{X\to Y}
for some finite sets
X
and
Y
.

An adversary intending to attack the protocol along with a distinguisher can be described by a family
\{\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
is a QIOM that corrupts party
M\in\{A,B\}
,
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
is a QOM, and
x_{\lambda}
is the input of the honest party
P\in\{A,B\}
. Define the one-bit random variable
\Pi[\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}]
as follows.

•

H
is sampled uniformly at random.

•

\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}^{H}
interacts with
P^{H}(x_{\lambda})
during the execution of
\Pi
, and
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
outputs a quantum state
\rho
, while
P^{H}
outputs a classical string
y
.

•

\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{H}(\rho,y)
outputs a bit
b
.

An
ideal-world
protocol
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\cal F}
for functionality
{\cal F}
consists of two “dummy” parties
\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}
that have access to an additional “trusted” party that implements
{\cal F}
. That is,
\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}
each interact directly with
{\cal F}
, which eventually returns outputs to
\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}
. We consider the execution of ideal-world protocols in the presence of a simulator followed by a distinguisher, described by a family
\{\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
. Define the random variable
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\cal F}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}]
over one bit output a follows.

•

\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}
interacts with
\widetilde{P}(x_{\lambda})
during the execution of
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\cal F}
, and
\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}
outputs a quantum state
\rho
, while
\widetilde{P}
outputs a classical string
y
.

•

\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}}(\rho,y)
outputs a bit
b
.

In the above,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}
may be
stateful
, meaning that the part of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}
that interacts with
\widetilde{P}(x)
may pass an arbitrary state to the part of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}
that answers
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\prime}s
oracle queries.

Furthermore, we will only consider the notion of security with abort where every ideal functionality is slightly modified to (1) know the identities of corrupted parties and (2) be slightly reactive: after all parties have provided input, the functionality computes outputs and delivers the outputs to the corrupt parties only. Then the functionality awaits either a “deliver” or “abort” command from the corrupted parties. Upon receiving “deliver”, the functionality delivers the outputs to all the honest parties. Upon receiving “abort”, the functionality delivers an abort output (
\bot
) to all the honest parties.

Definition 3.2

(Securely Realizing Functionalities with Abort)
.

A protocol
\Pi

\mu
-
securely realizes
a functionality
{\cal F}
with abort in the quantum random oracle model if there exists a polynomial
s
such that for any function
q
and any
\{\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, there exists a simulator
\{\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that the run-time of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda}
is at most the run-time of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
plus
s(\lambda,q(\lambda))
, and for all
\{\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with the property that the combined number of oracle queries made by
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
is at most
q(\lambda)
, it holds that

\bigg|\Pr[\Pi[\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}]=1]-\Pr[\widetilde{\Pi}_{\cal F}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}]=1]\bigg|=\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda)).

Furthermore, we say that a protocol
\Pi

securely realizes
a functionality
{\cal F}
if it
\mu
-securely realizes
{\cal F}
where
\mu
is such that for any
q(\lambda)={\rm poly}(\lambda)
,
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))={\rm negl}(\lambda)
.

3.3
Oblivious transfer functionalities

We will consider various oblivious transfer functionalities in this work. Some of these will be used as stepping stones towards other constructions.

•

{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[k]}
: the
chosen-input string
OT functionality takes as input a bit
b
from the receiver and two strings
m_{0},m_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
from the sender. It delivers
m_{b}
to the receiver.

•

{\cal F}_{\mathsf{R}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
: the
random-receiver-input bit
OT functionality takes as input
\top
from the receiver and two bits
m_{0},m_{1}\in\{0,1\}
from the sender. It samples
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and delivers
(b,m_{b})
to the receiver.

•

{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[k]}
: the
random-sender-input (string)
OT functionality takes as input
\top
from the sender and
(b,m)
from the receiver for
b\in\{0,1\},m\in\{0,1\}^{k}
. It set
m_{b}=m
, samples
m_{1-b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{k}
and delivers
(m_{0},m_{1})
to the sender.

We will often refer to the following bit OT reversal theorem.

Imported Theorem 3.3

(
[
IKNP03
]
)
.

Any protocol that securely realizes the functionality
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
can be converted into a protocol that securely realizes the functionality
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{R}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
, without adding any messages.

For concreteness, we specify how the OT reversal works. Suppose that Alice and Bob have access to an ideal OT functionality
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
where Alice is the receiver and Bob is the sender. Their goal is to realize
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{R}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
with roles reversed, i.e. with Alice as sender and Bob as receiver. This is achieved as follows.

•

Alice has input
m_{0},m_{1}\in\{0,1\}
, and samples
c\leftarrow\{0,1\},r\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

•

Alice and Bob run the protocol for
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
where Alice inputs
(c,r)
as receiver to
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
, and Alice sends

\ell_{0}\coloneqq m_{0}\oplus r,\ell_{1}\coloneqq m_{1}\oplus r\oplus c

along with her OT message to Bob.

•

Bob obtains output
(r_{0},r_{1})
from the protocol for
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
. Then, he sets

b\coloneqq r_{0}\oplus r_{1},m_{b}\coloneqq\ell_{b}\oplus r_{0},

and outputs
(b,m_{b})
.

3.4
Quantum oracle results

We state here some results on quantum oracle machine from prior literature, which we use in our proofs.

Imported Theorem 3.4

(One-way to hiding
[
AHU19
]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be finite non-empty sets and let
(S,O_{1},O_{2},\ket{\psi})
be sampled from an arbitrary distribution such that
S\subseteq X
,
O_{1},O_{2}:X\rightarrow Y
are such that
\forall x\not\in S,O_{1}(x)=O_{2}(x)
, and
\ket{\psi}
is a quantum state on an arbitrary number of qubits. Let
A^{O}(\ket{\psi})
be a quantum oracle algorithm that makes at most
q
queries. Let
B^{O}(\ket{\psi})
be an oracle algorithm that does the following: pick
i\leftarrow[q]
, run
A^{O}(\ket{\psi})
until (just before) the
i^{th}
query, measure the query input register in the computational basis, and output the measurement outcome
x
. Let

•

P_{\text{left}}=\Pr[A^{O_{1}}(\ket{\psi})=1]
,

•

P_{\text{right}}=\Pr[A^{O_{2}}(\ket{\psi})=1]
,

•

and
P_{\text{guess}}=\Pr[x\in S:x\leftarrow B^{O_{1}}(\ket{\psi})]
.

Then it holds that

|P_{\text{left}}-P_{\text{right}}|\leq 2q\sqrt{P_{\text{guess}}}.

The above theorem is actually a generalization of the theorem stated in
[
AHU19
]
, in which the input
\ket{\psi}
is assumed to be a
classical
bit string
z
. However, the proof given in
[
AHU19
]
readily extends to considering quantum input. The proof is split up into
[
AHU19
, Lemma 8]
and
[
AHU19
, Lemma 9]
. In Lemma 8,
(S,O_{1},O_{2},z)
are fixed, and
z
is used to define
A
’s initial state
\ket{\Psi_0}
. Here, we can just define
A
’s initial state as
\ket{\psi}
.
In Lemma 9, an expectation is taken over
(S,O_{1},O_{2},z)
, and the same expectation can be taken over
(S,O_{1},O_{2},\ket{\psi})
.

Imported Theorem 3.5

(Measure-and-reprogram
[
DFMS19
,
DFM20
]
)
.
10
10

10

This theorem was stated more generally in
[
DFMS19
,
DFM20
]
to consider the drop in expectation for each specific
x^{*}\in X
.

Let
X,Y
be finite non-empty sets, and let
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\mathsf{Adv}
be a quantum oracle machine with initial state
\rho
that makes at most
q
queries to a uniformly random function
H:X\to Y
and that outputs an
x\in X
along with an arbitrary quantum state
\sigma
on register
{\cal A}
. There exists a quantum interactive machine
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}]
such that for any projection

\Pi[y]\coloneqq\sum_{x}\ket{x}\bra{x}\otimes\Pi^{x,y}_{\cal A},

where each
\Pi^{x,y}
is an arbitrary projection on register
{\cal A}
that is parameterized by strings
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, it holds that

\displaystyle\expectation_{H}\left[\Tr\left(\Pi[H(x)]\left(\ket{x}\bra{x}\otimes\sigma\right)\right):(x,\sigma)\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}^{H}(\rho)\right]

\displaystyle\leq(2q+1)^{2}\expectation\left[\Tr\left(\Pi[y]\left(\ket{x}\bra{x}\otimes\sigma\right)\right):\begin{array}[]{r}(x,\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}](\rho)\\
y\leftarrow Y\\
\sigma\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}](y,\mathsf{st})\end{array}\right].

Moreover,
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}]
runs
\mathsf{Adv}
except for the following differences: i) it introduces an intermediate measurement of one of the registers maintained by
\mathsf{Adv}
to obtain
x
, and ii) it simulates responses to
\mathsf{Adv}
’s oracle queries to
H
.

Finally, we will often make use of an “on-the-fly” method for simulating a quantum random oracle, due to
[
Zha19
]
. This method of simulation is
efficient
and does not depend on an a priori upper bound on the number of queries
q
to be made by the adversary. In fact, as shown by
[
DFMS21
]
, this simulation method may be augmented with an extraction interface that essentially allows to recovers a pre-image
x
given an image
y=H(x)
.

Below, we define
independent queries
to an interface to be two consecutive queries that can in principle be performed in either order. More formally, two consecutive queries are independent if they can applied to disjoint registers, meaning that one query may be applied to input and output registers
{\cal X}
and
{\cal Y}
, while the other may be applied to disjoint input and output registers
{\cal X}^{\prime}
and
{\cal Y}^{\prime}
.

Furthermore, we say that two quantum operations
E
and
F

\alpha
-
almost
-
commute
if for any input state
\rho
,
\mathsf{TD}(E(F(\rho)),F(E(\rho))\leq\alpha
.
We say that two quantum operations
E
and
F

commute
if for any input state
\rho
,
\mathsf{TD}(E(F(\rho)),F(E(\rho))=0
.

Imported Theorem 3.6

(On-the-fly simulation with extraction
[
Zha19
,
DFMS21
]
)
.

Let
X
be a finite non-empty set and
Y=\{0,1\}^{n}
. There exists a simulator
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
that consists of an initialization step and an interface
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
that maintains an internal state.
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
, given registers
{\cal X}
and
{\cal Y}
, applies a quantum operation to these registers and its internal state.
11
11

11

We can also consider applying
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
to a classical input
x
and producing classical output
y
, which corresponds to applying the quantum operation on
\ket{x}_{\cal X}\ket{0}_{\cal Y}
and measuring the register
{\cal Y}
to produce the output.
The following properties hold for any oracle algorithm
A
.

1.

Indistinguishable simulation.

\Pr_{H}[1\leftarrow A^{H}]=\Pr[1\leftarrow A^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}].

2.

Efficiency.
Suppose that
A
makes
q
queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
. Then the total runtime of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
is
O(q^{2})
.

There also exists an interface
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
that upon input a classical value
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, outputs a classical value
\hat{x}\in X\cup\{\emptyset\}
. The following properties hold for any oracle algorithm
A
.

1.

Correctness of extraction.

Suppose that
A
makes
q
queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
and no queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
, and outputs
\mathbf{x}\in X^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{y}\in Y^{\ell}
. Then,

\Pr\left[\exists\,i\,:\,(\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{\hat{y}}_{i})\wedge(\mathbf{x}_{i}\neq\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{i})\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{r}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\leftarrow A^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}\\
\mathbf{\hat{y}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(\mathbf{x})\\
\mathbf{\hat{x}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}(\mathbf{y})\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{296(q+\ell+1)^{3}+2}{2^{n}}.

2.

Almost commutativity of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
and
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
.
Any two independent queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}

\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{2^{n/2}}
-almost-commute.

3.

Efficiency.

Suppose that
A
makes
q_{\mathsf{RO}}
queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
and
q_{\mathsf{E}}
queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
. Then the total runtime of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
is
O(q_{\mathsf{RO}}q_{\mathsf{E}}+q_{\mathsf{RO}}^{2})
.

3.5
Quantum entropy and leftover hashing

Quantum conditional min-entropy.

Let
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
denote a bipartite quantum state over registers
{\cal X},{\cal Y}
. Following
[
Ren08
,
KRS09
]
, the conditional min-entropy of
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
given
{\cal Y}
is then defined to be

\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}\mid{\cal Y})\coloneqq\sup_{\tau}\max\{h\in\mathbb{R}:2^{-h}\cdot{\mathbb{I}}_{\cal X}\otimes\tau_{\cal Y}-\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}\geq 0\}.

In this work, we will exclusively consider the case where the
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
is a joint distribution of the form
(R,\tau)
where
R
is a classical random variable. In other words,
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
can be written as

\sum_{x}\Pr[X=x]\ket{x}\bra{x}\otimes\tau_{x}.

We refer to such
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
as a classical-quantum state. In this case, quantum conditional min-entropy exactly corresponds to the maximum probability of guessing
x
given the state on register
{\cal Y}
.

Imported Theorem 3.7

(
[
KRS09
]
)
.

Let
\rho_{{\cal X},{\cal Y}}
be a classical-quantum state, and let
p_{\mathsf{guess}}(\rho_{{\cal X},{\cal Y}}|{\cal Y})
be the maximum probability that any quantum operation can output the
x
on register
{\cal X}
, given the state on register
{\cal Y}
. Then

p_{\mathsf{guess}}(\rho_{{\cal X},{\cal Y}}|{\cal Y})=2^{-\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\rho_{{\cal X},{\cal Y}}|{\cal Y})}.

Leftover hash lemma with quantum side information.

We now state a generalization of the leftover hash lemma to the setting of quantum side information.

Imported Theorem 3.8

(
[
RK05
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a family of universal hash functions from
X
to
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, i.e. for any
x\neq x^{\prime}
,
\Pr_{h\leftarrow\mathcal{H}}[h(x)=h(x^{\prime})]=2^{-\ell}
. Let
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
be any classical-quantum state. Let
{\cal R}
be a register that holds
h\leftarrow{\cal H}
, let
{\cal K}
be a register that holds
h(x)
, where
x
is on register
{\cal X}
, and define
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}{\cal R}{\cal K}}
to be the entire system. Then, it holds that

\left\|\rho_{{\cal Y}{\cal R}{\cal K}}-\rho_{{\cal Y}{\cal R}}\otimes\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\sum_{u}\ket{u}\bra{u}\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2^{1+\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}|{\cal Y})-\ell)}}.

Small superposition of terms.

We will also make use of the following lemma from
[
BF10
]
.

Imported Theorem 3.9
.

(
[
BF10
]
)
Let
{\cal X},{\cal Y}
be registers of arbitrary size, and let
\{\ket{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{\ket{w}\}_{w\in W}
be orthonormal bases of
{\cal X}
. Let
\ket{\psi}_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
and
\rho_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
be of the form

\ket{\psi}=\sum_{i\in J}\alpha_{i}\ket{i}_{\cal X}\ket{\psi_i}_{\cal Y}\text{ and }\rho=\sum_{i\in J}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\ket{i}\bra{i}_{\cal X}\otimes\ket{\psi_i}\bra{\psi_i}_{\cal Y}

for some subset
J\subseteq I
. Furthermore, let
\widehat{\rho}_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
and
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathsf{mix}}_{{\cal X}{\cal Y}}
be the classical-quantum states obtained by measuring register
{\cal X}
of
\ket{\psi}
and
\rho
, respectively, in basis
\{\ket{w}\}_{w\in W}
to observe outcome
w
. Then,

\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\widehat{\rho}_{{\cal X},{\cal Y}}|{\cal Y})\geq\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathsf{mix}}_{{\cal X},{\cal Y}}|{\cal Y})-\log|J|.

3.6
Sampling in a quantum population

In this section, we describe a generic framework presented in
[
BF10
]
for analyzing cut-and-choose strategies applied to quantum states.

Classical sampling stratiegies.

Let
A
be a set, and let
\mathbf{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})\in A^{n}
be a string of length
n
. We consider the problem of estimating the relative Hamming weight of a substring
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{t}})
by only looking at the substring
\mathbf{q}_{t}
of
\mathbf{q}
, for a subset
t\subset[n]
. We consider sampling strategies
\Psi=(P_{T},P_{S},f)
, where
P_{T}
is an (independently sampled) distribution over subsets
t\subseteq[n]
,
P_{S}
is a distribution over seeds
s\in S
, and
f:\{(t,\mathbf{v}):t\subset[n],\mathbf{v}\in A^{t}\}\times S\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a function that takes the subset
t
, the substring
\mathbf{v}
, and a seed
s
, and outputs an estimate for the relative Hamming weight of the remaining string. For a fixed subset
t
, seed
s
, and a parameter
\delta
, define
B^{\delta}_{t,s}(\Psi)\subseteq A^{n}
as

B^{\delta}_{t,s}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{b}\in A^{n}:|\omega(\mathbf{b}_{\overline{t}})-f(t,\mathbf{b}_{t},s)|<\delta\}.

Then we define the
classical error probability
of strategy
\Psi
as follows.

Definition 3.10

(Classical error probability)
.

The classical error probability of a sampling strategy
\Psi=(P_{T},P_{S},f)
is defined as the following value, paraterized by
0<\delta<1
:

\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}(\Psi)\coloneqq\max_{\mathbf{q}\in A^{n}}\Pr_{t\leftarrow P_{T},s\leftarrow P_{S}}\left[\mathbf{q}\notin B_{t,s}^{\delta}(\Psi)\right].

Quantum sampling strategies.

Now, let
A=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
be an
n
-partite quantum system on registers
{\cal A}={\cal A}_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
, where each system has dimension
d
. Let
\{\ket{a}\}_{a}
be a fixed orthonormal basis for each
{\cal A}_{i}
.
{\cal A}
may be entangled with another system
{\cal E}
, and we write the purified state on
{\cal A}
and
{\cal E}
as
\ket{\psi}_{{\cal A}{\cal E}}
. We consider the problem of testing whether the state on
{\cal A}
is close to the all-zero reference state
\ket{0}_{{\cal A}_{1}}\dots\ket{0}_{{\cal A}_{n}}
. There is a natural way to apply any sampling strategy
\Psi=(P_{T},P_{S},f)
to this setting: sample
t,s
according to
P_{T},P_{S}
, measure subsystems
{\cal A}_{i}
for
i\in[t]
in basis
\{\ket{a}\}_{a}
to observe
\mathbf{q}_{t}\in A^{|t|}
, and compute an estimate
f(t,\mathbf{q}_{t},s)
.

In order to analyze the effect of this strategy, we first consider the mixed state on registers
{\cal T}
(holding the subset
t
),
{\cal S}
(holding the seed
s
), and
{\cal A},{\cal E}
that results from sampling
t
and
s
according to
P_{TS}=P_{T}P_{S}

\rho_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}}=\sum_{t,s}P_{TS}(t,s)\ket{t,s}\bra{t,s}\otimes\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}.

Next, we compare this state to an
ideal
state, parameterized by
0<\delta<1
, of the form

\widetilde{\rho}_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}}=\sum_{t,s}P_{TS}(t,s)\ket{t,s}\bra{t,s}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\psi}^{ts}}\bra{\widetilde{\psi}^{ts}}\text{ with }\ket{\psi^{ts}}\in\mathsf{span}\left(B_{t,s}^{\delta}\right)\otimes{\cal E},

where

\mathsf{span}\left(B_{t,s}^{\delta}\right)\coloneqq\mathsf{span}\left(\{\ket{\bb}:\mathbf{b}\in B_{t,s}^{\delta}\}\right)=\mathsf{span}\left(\{\ket{\bb}:|\omega(\mathbf{b}_{\overline{t}})-f(t,\mathbf{b}_{t},s)|<\delta\}\right).

That is,
\widetilde{\rho}_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}}
is a state such that it holds
with certainty
that the state on registers
{\cal A}_{\overline{t}}{\cal E}
, after having measured
{\cal A}_{t}
and oberserving
\mathbf{q}_{t}
, is in a superposition of states with relative Hamming weight
\delta
-close to
f(t,\mathbf{q}_{t},s)
. This leads us to the definition of the
quantum error probability
of strategy
\Psi
.

Definition 3.11

(Quantum error probability)
.

The quantum error probability of a sampling strategy
\Psi=(P_{T},P_{S},f)
is defined as the following value, parameterized by
0<\delta<1
:

\epsilon_{\mathsf{quantum}}^{\delta}(\Psi)\coloneqq\max_{\cal E}\max_{\ket{\psi}_{{\cal A}{\cal E}}}\min_{\widetilde{\rho}_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}}}\mathsf{TD}\left(\rho_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}},\widetilde{\rho}_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}}\right),

where the first max is over all finite-dimensional registers
{\cal E}
, the second max is over all state
\ket{\psi}_{{\cal A}{\cal E}}
and the min is over all ideal state
\widetilde{\rho}_{{\cal T}{\cal S}{\cal A}{\cal E}}
of the form described above.

Finally, we relate the classical and quantum error probabilities.

Imported Theorem 3.12

(
[
BF10
]
)
.

For any sampling strategy
\Psi
and
\delta>0
,

\epsilon_{\mathsf{quantum}}^{\delta}(\Psi)\leq\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}(\Psi)}.

4
Seedless extraction from quantum sources

In this section, we consider the problem of seedless randomness extraction from a quantum source of entropy. The source of entropy we are interested in comes from applying a Hadamard basis measurement to a state that is in a “small” superposition of computational basis vectors. More concretely, consider an arbitrarily entangled system on registers
{\cal A},{\cal X}
, where
{\cal X}
is in a small superposition of computational basis vectors. Then, we want to specify an extractor
E
such that, if
x
is obtained by measuring register
{\cal X}
in the Hadamard basis, then
E(x)
looks uniformly random, even given the “side information” on register
{\cal A}
. Note that
seeded
randomness extraction in this setting has been well-studied (e.g.
[
RK05
,
DFL+09
,
BF10
]
).

Proofs of the following two theorems are given in
Appendix

A
.

4.1
The XOR extractor

First, we observe that if
E
just XORs all the bits of
x
together, then the resulting bit
E(x)
is
perfectly
uniform, as long as the original state on
{\cal X}
is only supported on vectors with relative Hamming weight < 1/2.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
{\cal X}
be an
n
-qubit register, and consider any state
\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
that can be written as

\ket{\gamma}=\sum_{u:{\cal H}{\cal W}(u)<n/2}\ket{\psi_u}_{{\cal A}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\cal X}.

Let
\rho_{{\cal A},{\cal P}}
be the mixed state that results from measuring
{\cal X}
in the Hadamard basis to produce
x
, and writing
\bigoplus_{i\in[n]}x_{i}
into the single qubit register
{\cal P}
. Then it holds that

\rho_{{\cal A},{\cal P}}=\Tr_{\cal X}(\ket{\gamma}\bra{\gamma})\otimes\left(\frac{1}{2}\ket{0}\bra{0}+\frac{1}{2}\ket{1}\bra{1}\right).

4.2
The RO extractor

Next, our goal is to extract multiple bits of randomness from
x
. To do this, we model
E
as a
random oracle
. We derive a bound on the advantage any adversary has in distinguishing
E(x)
from a uniformly random string, based on the number of qubits
k
in the register
{\cal X}
, the number of vectors
C
in the superposition on register
{\cal X}
, and the number of queries
q
made to the random oracle. In fact, to be as general as possible, we consider a random oracle with input length
n
, and allow
n-k
of the bits of the input to the random oracle to be (adaptively) determined by the adversary, while the remaining
k
bits are sampled by measuring a
k
-qubit register
{\cal X}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
H:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
be a uniformly random function, and let
q,C,k
be integers. Consider a two-stage oracle algorithm
(A_{1}^{H},A_{2}^{H})
that combined makes at most
q
queries to
H
. Suppose that
A_{1}^{H}
outputs classical strings
(T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T})
, and let
\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
be its left-over quantum state,
12
12

12

That is, consider sampling
H
, running a purified
A_{1}^{H}
, measuring at the end to obtain
(T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T})
, and then defining
\ket{\gamma}
to be the left-over state on
{\cal A}
’s remaining registers.
where
T\subset[n]
is a set of size
n-k
, each
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
,
{\cal A}
is a register of arbitary size, and
{\cal X}
is a register of
k
qubits. Suppose further that with probability 1 over the sampling of
H
and the execution of
A_{1}
, there exists a set
L\subset\{0,1\}^{k}
of size at most
C
such that
\ket{\gamma}
may be written as follows:

\ket{\gamma}=\sum_{u\in L}\ket{\psi_u}_{\cal A}\otimes\ket{u}_{\cal X}.

Now consider the following two games.

•

\mathsf{REAL}
:

–

A_{1}^{H}
outputs
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
.

–

{\cal X}
is measured in the Hadamard basis to produce a
k
-bit string which is parsed as
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
, and a left-over state
\ket{\gamma'}_{{\cal A}}
on register
{\cal A}
. Define
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

–

A_{2}^{H}
is given
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma'}_{{\cal A}},H(x)
, and outputs a bit.

•

\mathsf{IDEAL}
:

–

A_{1}^{H}
outputs
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
.

–

r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{m}
.

–

A_{2}^{H}
is given
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\Tr_{\cal X}(\ket{\gamma}\bra{\gamma}),r
, and outputs a bit.

Then,

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{REAL}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IDEAL}=1]\right|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{q}C+2q\sqrt{C}}{2^{k/2}}<\frac{4qC}{2^{k/2}}.

5
Non-interactive extractable and equivocal commitments

A non-interactive commitment scheme with partial opening in the quantum random oracle model consists of classical oracle algorithms
(\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Rec})
with the following syntax.

•

\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
: On input the security parameter
\lambda
and
n
messages
\{m_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{k}\}_{i\in[n]}
, output
n
commitments
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and a state
\mathsf{st}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}^{H}(\mathsf{st},T)
: On input a state
\mathsf{st}
and a set
T\subseteq[n]
, output messages
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in T}
and openings
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

•

\mathsf{Rec}^{H}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T})
: on input
n
commitments
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, a set
T
, and a set of message opening pairs
\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
, output either
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in T}
or
\bot
.

The commitment scheme is parameterized by
n=n(\lambda)
which is the number of messages to be committed in parallel, and
k=k(\lambda)
which is the number of bits per message.

5.1
Definitions

Definition 5.1

(Correctness)
.

A non-interactive commitment scheme with partial opening in the QROM is
correct
if for any
\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
T\subseteq[n]
,

\Pr\left[\mathsf{Rec}^{H}(\left\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\right\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T})=\{m_{i}\}_{i\in T}:\begin{array}[]{r}(\mathsf{st},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\\
\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}^{H}(\mathsf{st},T)\end{array}\right]=1.

Definition 5.2

(
\mu
-Hiding)
.

A non-interactive commitment scheme with partial opening in the QROM is
\mu
-hiding if for any adversary
\mathsf{Adv}
that makes at most
q(\lambda)
queries to the random oracle, and any two sets of messages
\{m_{i,0}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
\{m_{i,1}\}_{i\in[n]}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Bigg|

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Adv}^{H}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=1:\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{m_{i,0}\}_{i\in[n]})\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Adv}^{H}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=1:\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{m_{i,1}\}_{i\in[n]})\right]\Bigg|=\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda)).

Furthermore, we say that a commitment is
hiding
if it is
\mu
-hiding, where
\mu
is such that for any
q(\lambda)={\rm poly}(\lambda)
,
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))={\rm negl}(\lambda)
.

Definition 5.3

(
\mu
-Extractability)
.

A non-interactive commitment scheme with partial opening in the QROM is
\mu
-
extractable
if there exists a polynomial
s
and a simulator
\mathsf{SimExt}
consisting of an interface
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
and an algorithm
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
that may share a common state, such that for any family of quantum oracle algorithms
\{\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{D}_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that makes at most
q(\lambda)
queries to the random oracle, it holds that

\displaystyle\Bigg|

\displaystyle\Pr_{H}\left[\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})=1:\begin{array}[]{r}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{H}\\
(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},T,\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})\\
\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{H}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T})\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle-\Pr\left[\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})=1:\begin{array}[]{r}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}}\\
\{m_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\\
(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},T,\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})\\
\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T})\\
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\mathsf{FAIL}\text{ if }\mathsf{out}\notin\{\{m_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in T},\bot\}\end{array}\right]\Bigg|=\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda)),

where the state of
\mathsf{SimExt}
was kept implicit, and the total run-time of
\mathsf{SimExt}
on security parameter
1^{\lambda}
is at most
s(\lambda,q(\lambda))
. The interface
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
is invoked on each query to
H
made by
\mathsf{Adv}
and
\mathsf{Rec}
, while the algorithm
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
is invoked on the classical commitments output by
\mathsf{Adv}
.

Furthermore, we say that a commitment is
extractable
if it is
\mu
-extractable, where
\mu
is such that for any
q(\lambda)={\rm poly}(\lambda)
,
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))={\rm negl}(\lambda)
.

Finally, we say that the commitment scheme satisfies
extraction with a
\nu
-commuting simulator
if a call to
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}

\nu(\lambda)
-almost-commutes with the operation
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
when the input and output registers of
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
and
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
are disjoint.
13
13

13

Note that
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
and
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
can share a common state, so do not necesarily commute even when their inputs and output registers are disjoint.

Definition 5.4

(
\mu
-Equivocality)
.

A non-interactive commitment scheme with partial opening in the QROM is
\mu
-
equivocal
if there exists a polynomial
s
and a simulator
\mathsf{SimEqu}
that consists of an interface
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
and two algorithms
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}
that may all share a common state, such that for any family of quantum oracle algorithms
\{\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{D}_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that makes at most
q(\lambda)
queries to the random oracle, it holds that

\displaystyle\Bigg|

\displaystyle\Pr_{H}\left[\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{\mathsf{com}_{i},m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=1:\begin{array}[]{r}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}^{H}\\
(\mathsf{st},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\\
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\\
\{m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}^{H}(\mathsf{st},[n])\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle-\Pr\left[\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{\mathsf{com}_{i},m_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=1:\begin{array}[]{r}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}^{\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}\\
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}\\
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}^{\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\\
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}(\{m_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\end{array}\right]\Bigg|=\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda)),

where the state of
\mathsf{SimEqu}
was kept implicit, and the total run-time of
\mathsf{SimEqu}
on security parameter
1^{\lambda}
is at most
s(\lambda,q(\lambda))
. The interface
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
is invoked on each query to
H
made by
\mathsf{Adv}
, the algorithm
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
is invoked to produce commitments, and the algorithm
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}
is invoked on a set of messages to produce openings.

Furthermore, we say that a commitment is
equivocal
if it is
\mu
-equivocal, where
\mu
is such that for any
q(\lambda)={\rm poly}(\lambda)
,
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))={\rm negl}(\lambda)
.

It is easy to see that a
\mu
-equivocal commitment satisfies
2\mu
-hiding, since one can first move from committing to
\{m_{i,0}\}_{i\in[n]}
to a hybrid where the equivocality simulator is run, and then move to committing to
\{m_{i,1}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

We also note that all our definitions consider
classical commitments
, where the commitment string itself is purely classical. Furthermore, we assume that any potentially quantum state sent by a malicious committer is immediately measured by an honest receiver to produce a classical string – it is this classical string that serves as the commitment. This is similar to prior works that consider commitments in the QROM (eg.,
[
DFMS21
]
), and we refer the reader to
[
BB21
]
for additional discussions about enforcing classical (parts of) commitments via measurement.

5.2
Construction

Protocol
6

Parameters: security parameter
\lambda
, number of commitments
n=n(\lambda)

Random oracle:
H:\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}
.

•

\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{b_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
: For all
i\in[n]
, sample
r_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and set
\mathsf{com}_{i}=H(b_{i}||r_{i})
. Set
\mathsf{st}=\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and output
(\mathsf{st},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
.

•

\mathsf{Open}^{H}(\mathsf{st},T)
: Parse
\mathsf{st}
as
\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and output
\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

•

\mathsf{Rec}^{H}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})
: Output
\bot
if there exists
i\in T
s.t.
H(b_{i}||r_{i})\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
. Otherwise output
\{b_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

Figure 6:
Extractable and equivocal commitment scheme

We construct extractable and equivocal bit commitments in the QROM in
Fig.

6
. Without loss of generality, a committer can commit to strings of length
>1
by committing to each bit in the string one by one, and sending all commitments in parallel.

5.3
Extractability

In this section, we prove the following theorem by relying on

3.6
.
We remark that our proof of extraction uses ideas already present in
[
DFMS21
]
to establish that our construction satisfies
Definition

5.3
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Protocol
6
is a
\mu
-extractable non-interactive commitment scheme with partial opening in the QROM, with message length
1
(i.e.
k=1
), satisfying
Definition

5.3
, where
\mu(\lambda,q,n)=\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}+\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}
14
14

14

When
n=c\lambda
for some arbitrary fixed constant
c
, then we can define
\mu_{c}(\lambda,q)=\frac{2q(c\lambda)^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}
. In all our OT protocols, we will set
n
in this manner and will assume that
\mu
is a function of
\lambda,q
.
, and where the runtime of the simulator is bounded by
s(\lambda,q)=O(q^{2}+q\cdot n(\lambda))
. In addition, the protocol satisfies extraction with
\nu
-commuting simulator, where
\nu(\lambda)=\frac{8}{2^{\lambda/2}}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E})
be the on-the-fly random oracle simulator with extraction from
3.6
. The extractable commitment simulator
\mathsf{SimExt}=(\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext})
is defined as follows.

•

\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}=\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}

•

\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
runs
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
to obtain either a
\lambda+1
bit string
x^{*}
, or
\emptyset
. In the case of
x^{*}
, output the first bit of
x^{*}
. In the case of
\emptyset
, output 0.

We now prove that for any family of quantum oracle algorithms
\{\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda},\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, the two experiments in
Definition

5.3
are
\mu(\lambda,q)
close, where
\mu(\lambda,q)=\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}
. We consider the following sequence of hybrids (where parts in
blue
indicate difference from the previous hybrid):

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: This corresponds to the “real” experiment in
Definition

5.3
.

1.

Sample oracle
H\leftarrow F_{\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{H}

3.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

4.

\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{H}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})

5.

Output
b\leftarrow\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: This is the same as previous hybrid, except that all oracle calls to
H
are answered by
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
.

1.

Initialize the extractable random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}}}

3.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

4.

\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})

5.

Output
b\leftarrow\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: This is the same as the previous hybrid except for an additional query to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
that is performed at the end of the experiment, along with an event
\mathsf{BAD}
that we define. Notice also that we have opened up the description of the algorithm
\mathsf{Rec}
below. The hybrid outputs the following distribution.

1.

Initialize the extractable random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}

3.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

4.

\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})

–

If there exists

s.t.

For all

.

7.

If

and there exists

such that

, otherwise output

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}:
This is the same as the previous hybrid except that there is a query to

, and an extra condition in the

event. The hybrid outputs the following distribution:

1.

Initialize the extractable random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}

3.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

4.

\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})

–

If there exists
i\in T
s.t.
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\bot
, otherwise set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\{b_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

5.

Obtain bit

6.

For all

.

For all

, or if

such that

, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, otherwise output

b
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
: This hybrid is identical to the previous one except that

is called earlier on in the hybrid. The hybrid outputs the following distribution:

1.

Initialize the extractable random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}

3.

For all
i\in[n]
, set
x^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{com}_{i})
.

4.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

5.

\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})

–

If there exists
i\in T
s.t.
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\bot
, otherwise set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\{b_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

6.

b\leftarrow\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})

7.

For all
i\in[n]
, set

.

8.

If there exists
i\in T
such that

(x_{i}^{*}\neq(b_{i}||r_{i}))\wedge(y_{i}=\mathsf{com}_{i})
, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, or if
\mathsf{out}\neq\bot
and there exists
i\in T
such that
y_{i}\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, otherwise output

b
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
: This hybrid is identical to the previous hybrid except for altering the variable
\mathsf{out}
to sometimes take the value
\mathsf{FAIL}
. The hybrid outputs the following distribution:

1.

Initialize the extractable random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}

3.

For all
i\in[n]
, set
x^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{com}_{i})
.

For all
i\in[n]
, if
x_{i}^{*}=\emptyset
, set
b^{*}_{i}\coloneqq 0
, and otherwise set
b^{*}_{i}
equal to the first bit of
x_{i}^{*}
.

4.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

5.

Obtain
\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})
as follows:

–

If there exists
i\in T
s.t.
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\bot
, otherwise set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\{b_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

6.

If
\mathsf{out}\notin\{\{b_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in T},\bot\}
, set
\mathsf{out}=\mathsf{FAIL}
.

7.

b\leftarrow\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})

8.

For all
i\in[n]
, set
y_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})
.

9.

If there exists
i\in T
such that

(x_{i}^{*}\neq(b_{i}||r_{i}))\wedge(y_{i}=\mathsf{com}_{i})
, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, or if
\mathsf{out}\neq\bot
and there exists
i\in T
such that
y_{i}\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, otherwise output
b
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
: This hybrid is identical to the previous hybrid except for removing the final query to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
and the event
\mathsf{BAD}
.

1.

Initialize the extractable random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
.

2.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}

3.

For all
i\in[n]
, set
x^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{com}_{i})
.
For all
i\in[n]
, if
x_{i}^{*}=\emptyset
, set
b^{*}_{i}\coloneqq 0
, and otherwise set
b^{*}_{i}
equal to the first bit of
x_{i}^{*}
.

4.

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open},\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1})

5.

Obtain
\mathsf{out}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in T})
as follows:

–

If there exists
i\in T
s.t.
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\bot
, otherwise set
\mathsf{out}\coloneqq\{b_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

6.

If
\mathsf{out}\notin\{\{b_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in T},\bot\}
, set
\mathsf{out}=\mathsf{FAIL}
.

7.

b\leftarrow\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\mathsf{out})

8.

For all
i\in[n]
, set
y_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})
.

9.

If there exists
i\in T
such that

(x_{i}^{*}\neq(b_{i}||r_{i}))\wedge(y_{i}=\mathsf{com}_{i})
, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, or if
\mathsf{out}\neq\bot
and there exists
i\in T
such that
y_{i}\neq\mathsf{com}_{i}
, output
\mathsf{BAD}
, otherwise output
b
.

We note that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
is the simulated distribution. We prove indistinguishability between the hybrids below.

Claim 5.6
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]

Proof.

This follows from the indistinguishable simulation property of Imported Theorem
3.6
.
∎

Claim 5.7
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]

Proof.

First, adding the extra query to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
does not affect the output of the experiment since it is performed after
b
is computed. Next, the event
\mathsf{BAD}
only occurs if some classical query
(b_{i}||r_{i})
to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
returns different classical values at different points in the experiment. However, this can never occur due to the indistinguishable simulation property of Imported Theorem
3.6
, and because two classical queries to an oracle
H
always return the same value.

∎

Claim 5.8
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]|\leq\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}

Proof.

First, adding the query to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
does not affect the output of the experiment since it is performed after the information needed to determine the output is already computed.

Thus, to prove this claim, it suffices to show that

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\exists\,i\in T\,:\,(x_{i}^{*}\neq(b_{i}||r_{i}))\wedge(y_{i}=\mathsf{com}_{i})\right]\leq\frac{296(q+n+1)^{3}+2}{2^{\lambda+1}}.

Consider adversary
\mathsf{B}
that runs steps
2
through
5
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, and outputs
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in T},\allowbreak\{b_{i}||r_{i}\}_{i\in T}
. Note that
\mathsf{B}
does not make any queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
. Now consider the experiment where
\mathsf{B}
is run as above, followed by running
y_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})
for all
i\in T
and then
x_{i}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{com}_{i})
for all
i\in T
, and outputting
1
if
\exists\,i\in T\,:\,(x_{i}^{*}\neq(b_{i}||r_{i}))\wedge(\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(b_{i}||r_{i})=\mathsf{com}_{i})
. Applying the correctness of extraction property of
3.6
, and bounding
|T|
by
n
, we get the required claim.
∎

Claim 5.9
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]|\leq\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}

Proof.

This follows from the almost commutativity of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
property of
3.6
. Indeed, since
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
are classical strings output by the experiment after step 2, all subsequent queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
are independent of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}(\mathsf{com}_{i})
for any
i
, in the sense that they may operate on disjoint input and output registers. Thus, the statistical distance between the two experiments is at most
\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}
, since there are most
q
queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
, and
n
queries to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
.

∎

Claim 5.10
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]

Proof.

The only change in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
is that the variable
\mathsf{out}
is modified and set to
\mathsf{FAIL}
when
\mathsf{out}\not\in\{\{b^{*}_{i}\}_{i\in T},\bot\}
. We show that whenever
\mathsf{out}
is set of
\mathsf{FAIL}
, the event
\mathsf{BAD}
occurs, which means that the output of the experiment is anyway
\mathsf{BAD}
.

Indeed, in the case of
\mathsf{FAIL}
, we know that
\mathsf{out}
is not equal to
\bot
or
\{b_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in T}
. Since
\mathsf{out}\neq\bot
, this means that either
\mathsf{BAD}
occurs, or
y_{i}=\mathsf{com}_{i}
for all
i\in T
. Since
\mathsf{out}\neq\{b_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in T}
, there must there exist
i\in T
such that
x_{i}^{*}\neq(b_{i}||r_{i})
. But then if
y_{i}=\mathsf{com}_{i}
for all
i\in T
, the event
\mathsf{BAD}
also occurs.

∎

Claim 5.11
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]

Proof.

This follows by observing that the distribution
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
except that it never outputs
\mathsf{BAD}
, and therefore the probability that it outputs
1
cannot possibly reduce.
∎

Combining all claims, we have that

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]\leq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]+\Bigg(\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}\Bigg),

(1)

and by a similar argument

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=0]\leq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=0]+\Bigg(\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}\Bigg).

Because the output of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
is a single bit, the equation above implies that

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]\leq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]+\Bigg(\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}\Bigg)

(2)

Combining equations (
1
) and (
2
), we have

\Big|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]\Big|\leq\Bigg(\frac{148(q+n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{8qn}{2^{\lambda/2}}\Bigg),

(3)

In addition, by Property
3
in
3.6
, the runtime of
\mathsf{Sim}
is bounded by a polynomial
s(\lambda,q)=O(q^{2}+q\cdot n)
. Finally, by the almost commutativity of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
and
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{E}
property of
3.6
, it follows that the simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
is
\nu
-commuting, with
\nu(\lambda)=\frac{8}{2^{\lambda/2}}
.
∎

5.4
Equivocality

Theorem 5.12
.

Protocol
6
is a
\mu
-equivocal bit commitment scheme with partial opening in the QROM satisfying Definition
5.4
, where
\mu(\lambda,q,n)=\frac{2qn^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}

15
15

15

When
n=c\lambda
for some arbitrary fixed constant
c
, then we can define
\mu_{c}(\lambda,q)=\frac{2q(c\lambda)^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}
. In all our OT protocols, we will set
n
in this manner and will assume that
\mu
is a function of
\lambda,q
.
and where the runtime of the simulator is bounded by
s(\lambda,q)=O(q^{2}+{\rm poly}(\lambda))
.

Proof.

We construct a simulator
\mathsf{SimEqu}=(\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open})
as follows:

1.

Initialize the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator,
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
, from
3.6
. For all
i\in[n]
, sample
r_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda},R^{i}_{0},R^{i}_{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}
.

2.

Let
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
answer oracle queries of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}
and
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}
using the oracle
{H}^{\bot}
which is defined as follows:

{H}^{\bot}(x)=\begin{cases}R^{i}_{0}&\,\text{if }x=0||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
R^{i}_{1}&\,\text{if }x=1||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(x)&\,\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

In an abuse of notation, we have defined
H^{\bot}
using the quantum operation
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
.
H^{\bot}
will actually be implemented by issuing a
controlled
query to
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
(see discussion on controlled queries in
Section

3.2
), controlled on the
x
in input register
{\cal X}
not being in the set
\{b||r_{i}\}_{b\in\{0,1\},i\in[n]}
, and then, for each
i\in[n]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, implementing a controlled query to a unitary that maps
\ket{x,y}\to\ket{x,y \oplus R_{b}^i}
, controlled on the input
{\cal X}
register being
(b||r_{i})
.

3.

\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
: To output commitments, for all
i\in[n]
, sample
c_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}
, set
\mathsf{com}_{i}=c_{i}
and output
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

4.

\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}
: When given input
\{b_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, output
\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

5.

Let
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
answer oracle queries of
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
using the oracle
{H}^{\bot}_{R}
which is defined as follows:

{H}^{\bot}_{R}(x)=\begin{cases}c_{i}&\,\text{if }x=b_{i}||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}(x)&\,\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

Note that
{H}^{\bot}_{R}
can be implemented in a similar way as described above.

Consider then the following sequence of hybrids to prove that Protocol
6
is an equivocal bit commitment scheme in QROM satisfying Definition
5.4
(parts in
blue
are different from previous hybrid):

.

Consider the following indistinguishability claims between the hybrids:

Claim 5.13
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]|\leq\frac{2qn^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}

Proof.

The two hybrids differ in the way oracle queries of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
are answered. In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, queries of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}
and
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}
are answered using oracle
{H}^{\bot}
instead of
H
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
.
Assume then for sake of contradiction that there exists some
\mathsf{Adv}=\{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
for which
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]|>\frac{2q^{3/2}n^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}
.
Fix such
\mathsf{Adv}
.

We derive a contradiction by relying on the One-Way to Hiding lemma (Imported Theorem
3.4
).
We first define oracle algorithms
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C}
.
Our goal after defining these algorithms will be to show
\mathsf{C}
succeeds in a particular event with more probability than is allowed by the statement of the lemma, which gives us a contradiction.

\mathsf{A}^{O}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})

•

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{b_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}^{O}

•

(\mathsf{st},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}^{H}(1^{\lambda},\{b_{i}\}_{i\in[n]};\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})

•

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}^{O}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})

•

\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}^{H}(\mathsf{st},[n])

•

Output
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}^{H}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\{b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
.

\mathsf{B}^{O}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})

Fix
q\coloneqq q(\lambda)
non-uniformly as (an upper bound on) the number of oracle queries of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
, and thus also
\mathsf{A}^{O}
.
Pick
i\leftarrow[q]
, run
\mathsf{A}^{O}
until just before the
i^{th}
query, measure the query register and output the measurement outcome
x
.

\mathsf{C}^{O}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})

Run
x\leftarrow\mathsf{B}^{O}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
, parse
x
as
b||r
, where
|b|=1,|r|=\lambda
, and output
r
.

We begin by proving the following claim about
\mathsf{B}
.

SubClaim 5.14
.

Given oracle
H
and
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1})=\{r_{i},R^{i}_{0},R^{i}_{1}\}_{i\in[n]}
, define oracle
{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}
as

{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}(x)=\begin{cases}R^{i}_{0}&\,\text{if }x=0||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
R^{i}_{1}&\,\text{if }x=1||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
H(x)&\,\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then,

\Pr[x\in S_{\mathbf{r}}\ \Bigg|\ \begin{array}[]{r}H\leftarrow F_{\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}}\\
\forall i\in[n],r_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda},R^{i}_{0},R^{i}_{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\\
x\leftarrow\mathsf{B}^{{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\\
S_{\mathbf{r}}=\{(b||r_{i})\}_{b\in\{0,1\},i\in[n]}\end{array}]>\frac{n}{2^{\lambda}}

Proof.

Note that over the randomness of sampling
H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}
,
\mathsf{A}^{{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
is the experiment in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, while
\mathsf{A}^{H}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
is the experiment in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
.

For any oracle
H
, and any
\mathbf{r}\coloneqq\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},\mathbf{R}_{0}\coloneqq\{R^{i}_{0}\}_{i\in[n]},\mathbf{R}_{1}\coloneqq\{R^{i}_{1}\}_{i\in[n]}
,

\displaystyle P_{\text{left}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{A}^{{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=1],\quad P_{\text{right}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{A}^{H}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=1]

This implies that

\displaystyle\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\left[P_{\text{left}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\right]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1],\quad\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\left[P_{\text{right}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\right]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]

Therefore,

\displaystyle\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\Big|P_{\text{left}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}-P_{\text{right}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\Big|

\displaystyle\geq\Big|\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\left[P_{\text{left}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\right]-\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\left[P_{\text{right}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\right]\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]\Big|>\frac{2qn^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}

(4)

where the first inequality follows by Jensen’s inequality and linearity of expectation.
Also, letting
S_{\mathbf{r}}=\{(b||r_{i})\}_{b\in\{0,1\},i\in[n]}
, define

P_{\text{guess}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\coloneqq\Pr[x\in S_{\mathbf{r}}\ |\ x\leftarrow\mathsf{B}^{{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})].

Invoking the one-way to hiding lemma (Imported Theorem
3.4
), with
O_{1},O_{2}
set as
{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}},H
, and noting the oracle algorithm
B
in the lemma is exactly the same as
\mathsf{B}
in our claim, and that set
S_{\mathbf{r}}
is the set of points such that
\forall x\notin S_{\mathbf{r}},H(x)={H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}(x)
, we get

\displaystyle\forall{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}},\,P_{\text{guess}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Big|P_{\text{left}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}-P_{\text{right}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\Big|^{2}}{4q^{2}}

\displaystyle\implies\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}[P_{\text{guess}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}]

\displaystyle\geq\expectation_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\Bigg[\frac{\Big|P_{\text{left}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}-P_{\text{right}}^{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\Big|^{2}}{4q^{2}}\Bigg]>\frac{n}{2^{\lambda}}\quad\text{(using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:comow2heq1})}

Therefore,

\Pr_{H,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1},\mathsf{B}}[x\in S_{\mathbf{r}}\ |\ x\leftarrow\mathsf{B}^{{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})]>\frac{n}{2^{\lambda}}

as desired.
∎

SubClaim 5.15
.

Given oracle
H
and
(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1})=\{r_{i},R^{i}_{0},R^{i}_{1}\}_{i\in[n]}
, define oracle
{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}
as

{H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}(x)=\begin{cases}R^{i}_{0}&\,\text{if }x=0||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
R^{i}_{1}&\,\text{if }x=1||r_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[n]\\
H(x)&\,\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then,

\Pr\left[y\in\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\ \Bigg|\begin{array}[]{r}H\leftarrow F_{\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}}\\
\forall i\in[n],r_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda},R^{i}_{0},R^{i}_{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{C}^{{H}^{\bot}_{r,R_{0},R_{1}}}(H,\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\end{array}\right]>{\frac{n}{2^{\lambda}}}

Proof.

This follows from Subclaim
5.14
, and noting that for any
x\in S_{\mathbf{r}}
,
x
is of the form
b||r
, where
|b|=1,|r|=\lambda
and
r\in\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

∎

To complete the proof of Claim
5.13
, we note that by SubClaim
5.15
, it holds that

\Pr[y\in\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}]>\frac{n}{2^{\lambda}}

where
y
and
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
are sampled according to the process below:

•

Sample oracle
H\leftarrow F_{\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}}
.

•

For all
i\in[n]
, sample
r_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda},R^{i}_{0},R^{i}_{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}

•

Sample
\iota\leftarrow[q]
and execute the steps below until the adversary makes the
\iota^{th}
query.

–

(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{b_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}^{\ {H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}

–

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}^{\ {H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1},\{H(b_{i}||r_{i})\}_{i\in[n]})

•

Measure the adversary’s query register to obtain
x
, parse
x
as
b||y
where
|b|=1,|y|=\lambda
.

Note that the view of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}
and
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}
consists of
({H}^{\bot}_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{R}_{0},\mathbf{R}_{1}},\{H(b_{i}||r_{i})\}_{i\in[n]})\equiv(O,\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
for a oracle
O
and strings
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
that are sampled uniformly and
independently
of each other and independently of
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. This means that the adversary is required to guess one out of
n
uniform
\lambda
-bit strings
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
given uniform and independent auxiliary information.
Since this is impossible except with probability at most
\frac{n}{2^{\lambda}}
, we obtain a contradiction, proving our claim.

∎

Claim 5.16
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]

Proof.

Note that the distribution of
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
in either hybrid is a set of uniformly independently sampled random strings, sampled independently of the oracle that is accessed by
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}
. Therefore, the distribution of the output of
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}
in either hybrid is identical. Conditioned on this, note that the following two distributions representing the inputs/oracle
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
has access to, are identical:

•

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
,
(H,\{\mathsf{com}_{i},b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=(H,\{H(b_{i}||r_{i}),b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
.

•

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
,
({H}^{\bot}_{R},\{\mathsf{com}_{i},b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})=({H}^{\bot}_{R},\{c_{i},b_{i},r_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
, where for all
i\in[n],{H}^{\bot}_{R}(b_{i}||r_{i})=c_{i}
.

Since the distributions are identical, the claim then follows.
∎

Claim 5.17
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]

Proof.

Indistinguishability follows immediately from the indistinguishable simulation property of
3.6
.
∎

Combining all claims, we get
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]|\leq\frac{2qn^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda/2}}
. In addition, note that the runtime of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{Equ}}
is bounded by
s(\lambda,q)=O(q^{2}+{\rm poly}(\lambda))
, where the
O(q^{2})
terms comes from using
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
(
3.6
).
∎

6
The fixed basis framework: OT from entanglement

We first obtain non-interactive OT in the shared EPR model, and then show that the protocol remains secure even when one player does the EPR pair setup.

6.1
Non-interactive OT in the shared EPR pair model

Protocol
7

Ingredients and parameters.

•

Security parameter
\lambda
, and constants
A,B
. Let
n=(A+B)\lambda
and
k=A\lambda
.

•

A non-interactive extractable commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec})
, where commitments to 3 bits have size
\ell\coloneqq\ell(\lambda)
.

•

A random oracle
H_{FS}:\{0,1\}^{n\ell}\to\{0,1\}^{\lceil\log\binom{n}{k}\rceil}
.

•

An extractor
E
with domain
\{0,1\}^{n-k}
which is either

–

The XOR function, so
E(r_{1},\dots,r_{n-k})=\bigoplus_{i\in[n-k]}r_{i}
.

–

A random oracle
H_{Ext}:\{0,1\}^{n-k}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

Setup.

•

2n
EPR pairs on registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,b},{\cal S}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
, where the receiver has register
{\cal R}\coloneqq\{{\cal R}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
and the sender has register
{\cal S}\coloneqq\{{\cal S}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

Protocol.

•

Receiver message.

\mathsf{R}
, on input
b\in\{0,1\},m\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, does the following.

–

Measurement.
Sample
\theta_{1}\theta_{2}\ldots\theta_{n}\leftarrow\{+,\times\}^{n}
and for
i\in[n]
, measure registers
{\cal R}_{i,0,},{\cal R}_{i,1}
in basis
\theta_{i}
to obtain
r_{i,0},r_{i,1}
.

–

Measurement check.

*

Compute
\left(\mathsf{st},\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}\left(\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\right)
.

*

Compute
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}\|\dots\|c_{n})
, parse
T
as a subset of
[n]
of size
k
.

*

Compute
\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in[T]}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{st},T)
.

–

Reorientation.
Let
\overline{T}=[n]\setminus T
, and for all
i\in\overline{T}
, set
d_{i}=b\oplus\theta_{i}
(interpreting
+
as 0,
\times
as 1).

–

Sampling.
Set
x_{b}=E\left(\{r_{i,\theta_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}\right)\oplus m
, and sample
x_{1-b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

–

Message.
Send to
\mathsf{S}

(x_{0},x_{1}),\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in[T]},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}.

•

Sender computation.

\mathsf{S}
does the following.

–

Check Receiver Message.
Abort if any of the following fails.

*

Check that
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}\|\dots\|c_{n})
.

*

Check that
\mathsf{Rec}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T})\neq\bot
.

*

For every
i\in T
, measure the registers
{\cal S}_{i,0},{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
\theta_{i}
to obtain
r_{i,0}^{\prime},r_{i,1}^{\prime}
, and check that
r_{i,0}=r_{i,0}^{\prime}
and
r_{i,1}=r_{i,1}^{\prime}
.

–

Output.

For all
i\in\overline{T}
, measure the register
{\cal S}_{i,0}
in basis
+
and the register
{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
\times
to obtain
r_{i,0}^{\prime},r_{i,1}^{\prime}
. Output

m_{0}\coloneqq x_{0}\oplus E\left(\{r_{i,d_{i}}^{\prime}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}\right),m_{1}\coloneqq x_{1}\oplus E\left(\{r_{i,d_{i}\oplus 1}^{\prime}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}\right).

Figure 7:
Non-interactive random-sender-input OT in the shared EPR pair model.

Theorem 6.1
.

Instantiate Protocol
7
with any non-interactive commitment scheme that is
correct
(
Definition

5.1
),
hiding
(
Definition

5.2
), and
extractable
(
Definition

5.3
). Then the following hold.

•

When instantiated with the XOR extractor, there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
7
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
.

•

When instantiated with the ROM extractor, there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
7
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
.

Furthermore, letting
\lambda
be the security parameter,
q
be an upper bound on the total number of random oracle queries made by the adversary, and using the commitment scheme from
Section

5.2
with security parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
, the following hold.

•

When instantiatied with the XOR extractor and constants
A=50
,
B=100
, Protocol
7
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\left(\frac{8q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{3600\lambda q}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{148(450\lambda+q+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}\right),\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\left(\frac{85\lambda^{1/2}q}{2^{2\lambda}}\right).

This requires a total of
2(A+B)\lambda=300\lambda
EPR pairs.

•

When instantiated with the ROM extractor and constants
A=1050
,
B=2160
, Protocol
7
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\left(\frac{8q^{3/2}+4\lambda}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{77040\lambda q}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{148(9630\lambda+q+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}\right),\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\left(\frac{197\lambda^{1/2}q}{2^{2\lambda}}\right).

This requires a total of
2(A+B)\lambda=6420\lambda
EPR pairs.

Then, applying non-interactive bit OT reversal (
3.3
) to the protocol that realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
immediately gives the following corollary.

Corollary 6.2
.

Given a setup of
300\lambda
shared EPR pairs, there exists a one-message protocol in the QROM that
O\left(\frac{q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
-securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{R}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
.

In this section, we provide the proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

Proof.

We will prove the part of the theorem statement that considers instantiating Protocol
7
with the specific commitment from
Section

5.2
, and note that the more general part of the theorem statement follows along the same arguments.

Let
{H_{C}}
be the random oracle used by the commitment scheme. We treat
{H_{C}}
and
H_{FS}
(and
H_{Ext}
in the case of the ROM extractor) as separate oracles that the honest parties and adversaries query, which is without loss of generality (see
Section

3.2
).

Sender security.

First, we show security against a malicious receiver
\mathsf{R}^{*}
. Let
(\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO},\allowbreak\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext})
be the simulator for the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.3
) against a malicious committer. We describe a simulator for our OT protocol against a malicious receiver below.

\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
:

•

Prepare
2n
EPR pairs on registers
{\cal R}
and
{\cal S}
.

•

Initialize
\mathsf{R}^{*}
with the state on register
{\cal R}
. Answer
H_{FS}
(and
H_{Ext}
) queries using the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator (
3.6
), and answer
{H_{C}}
queries using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
.

•

Obtain
(x_{0},x_{1}),\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
from
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and run

\{(r_{i,0}^{*},r_{i,1}^{*},\theta_{i}^{*})\}_{i\in[n]}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}).

•

Run the “check receiver message” part of the honest sender strategy, except that
\{r^{*}_{i,0},r^{*}_{i,1}\}_{i\in T}
are used in place of
\{r_{i,0},r_{i,1}\}_{i\in T}
for the third check. If any check fails, send
\mathsf{abort}
to the ideal functionality, output
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s state, and continue to answering the distinguisher’s queries.

•

Let
b\coloneqq\mathsf{maj}\{\theta_{i}^{*}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
. For all
i\in\overline{T}
, measure the register
{\cal S}_{i,b\oplus d_{i}}
in basis
+
if
b\oplus d_{i}=0
or basis
\times
if
b\oplus d_{i}=1
to obtain
r_{i}^{\prime}
. Let
m_{b}\coloneqq x_{b}\oplus E(\{r_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})
.

•

Send
(b,m_{b})
to the ideal functionality, output
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s state, and continue to answering the distinguisher’s queries.

•

Answer the distinguisher’s
H_{FS}
(and
H_{Ext}
) queries with the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator and
{H_{C}}
queries with
