\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
.

Now, given a distinguisher
\mathsf{D}
such that
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}
make a total of at most
q
queries combined to
H_{FS}
and
{H_{C}}
(and
H_{Ext}
), consider the following sequence of hybrids.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The result of the real interaction between
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and
\mathsf{S}
. Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over
\{0,1\}
described by
\Pi[\mathsf{R}^{*},\mathsf{D},\top]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, except that all
{H_{C}}
queries of
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}
are answered via the
\mathsf{Sim}.\mathsf{RO}
interface, and
\{(r_{i,0}^{*},r_{i,1}^{*},\theta_{i}^{*})\}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
is run after
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message. The values
\{r^{*}_{i,0},r^{*}_{i,1}\}_{i\in T}
are used in place of
\{r_{i,0},r_{i,1}\}_{i\in T}
for the third sender check.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: The result of
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
interacting in
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}}
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}}
). Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over
\{0,1\}
described by
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}],\mathsf{D},\top]
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}],\mathsf{D},\top]
).

The proof of security against a malicious
\mathsf{R}^{*}
follows by combining the two claims below,
6.3
and
6.4
.

Claim 6.3
.

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]\right|\leq\frac{24(A+B)\lambda q}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{148(q+3(A+B)\lambda+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}.

Proof.

This follows by a direct reduction to extractability of the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.3
). Indeed, let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit}}
be the machine that runs
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
until
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message, which includes
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open}}
be the machine that takes as input the rest of the state of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, which includes
T
and the openings
\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in[T]}
, and outputs
T
and these openings. Let
\mathsf{D}
be the machine that runs the rest of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and outputs a bit.

Then, the bound follows from plugging in
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
and
n=3(A+B)\lambda
(the number of bits committed) to the bound from
Theorem

5.5
.

∎

Claim 6.4
.

For any
q\geq 4
, when
E
is the XOR extractor and
A=50
,
B=100
, or when
E
is the ROM extractor and
A=1050,B=2160
,

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]\right|\leq\frac{8q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}.

Proof.

First, note that the only difference between these hybrids is that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, the
m_{1-b}
received by
\mathsf{D}
as part of the sender’s output is sampled uniformly at random (by the ideal functionality), where
b
is defined as
\mathsf{maj}\{\theta_{i}^{*}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
. Now, we introduce some notation.

•

Let
\mathbf{c}\coloneqq(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
be the classical commitments.

•

Write the classical extracted values
\{(r_{i,0}^{*},r_{i,1}^{*},\theta^{*}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
as

\mathbf{R}^{*}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}r^{*}_{1,0}\ \dots\ r^{*}_{n,0}\\
r^{*}_{1,1}\ \dots\ r^{*}_{n,1}\end{bmatrix},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\theta^{*}_{1}\ \dots\ \theta^{*}_{n}\end{bmatrix}.

•

Given any
\mathbf{R},\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
, define
\ket{\bR_{\btheta}}
as a state on
n
two-qubit registers,
where register
i
contains the vector
\ket{\bR_{i,0},\bR_{i,1}}
prepared in the
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})
-basis.

•

Given
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
and a subset
T\subset[n]
, define
\mathbf{R}_{T}
to be the columns of
\mathbf{R}
indexed by
T
, and define
\Delta\left(\mathbf{R}_{T},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{T}\right)
as the fraction of columns
i\in T
such that
(\mathbf{R}_{i,0},\mathbf{R}_{i,1})\neq(\mathbf{R}^{*}_{i,0},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{i,1})
.

•

For
T\subset[n]
, let
\overline{T}\coloneqq[n]\setminus T
.

•

Given
\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
,
T\subseteq[n]
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
, define

\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,\delta}\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{R}:\mathbf{R}_{T}=\mathbf{R}^{*}_{T},\Delta\left(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{\overline{T}}\right)\geq\delta}\ket{\bR_{\btheta^*}}\bra{\bR_{\btheta^*}}.

Intuitively, this is a projection onto “bad” states as defined by
\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,\delta
, i.e., states that agree with
\mathbf{R}^{*}
on all registers
T
but are at least
\delta
-“far” from
\mathbf{R}^{*}
on registers
\overline{T}
.

Now, consider the following projection, which has hard-coded the description of
H_{FS}
:

\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}_{{\cal Z}}\otimes\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},H_{FS}(\mathbf{c}),\delta}_{{\cal S}},

where
{\cal C}
is the register holding the classical commitments,
{\cal Z}
is the register holding the output of
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
, and
{\cal S}
is the register holding the sender’s halves of EPR pairs.

SubClaim 6.5
.

Let

\tau\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}p^{(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}~\tau^{(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})},

where

\tau^{(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}=\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}_{\cal Z}\otimes\rho^{(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}

is the entire state of the system, including the sender’s halves of EPR pairs and the receiver’s entire state in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
(equivalently also
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
) at the point in the experiment that is right after
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message and
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
is run. Here, each
p^{(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}
is the probability that the registers
{\cal C},{\cal Z}
holds the classical string
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
,
{\cal S}
is the register holding the sender’s halves of EPR pairs, and
{\cal X}
is a register holding the remaining state of the system, which includes the rest of the receiver’s classical message and its private state. Then,

•

If
A=50,B=100
, then
\Tr(\Pi_\mathsf{bad}^{0.25}\tau)\leq\frac{64q^{3}}{2^{2\lambda}}.

•

If
A=1050,B=2160
, then
\Tr(\Pi_\mathsf{bad}^{0.054}\tau)\leq\frac{64q^{3}}{2^{2\lambda}}.

Proof.

Define
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}^{H_{FS}}
to be the oracle machine that runs
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
until
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs
\mathbf{c}
(and the rest of its message), then runs
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
to obtain
\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}
, and then outputs the remaining state
\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}
. Consider running the measure-and-reprogram simulator
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]
from
Footnote

10
, which simulates
H_{FS}
queries, measures and outputs
\mathbf{c}
, then receives a uniformly random subset
T\subset[n]
of size
k
, and then continues to run
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
until it outputs
\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\otimes\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}
. Letting

\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}[T]\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}_{{\cal Z}}\otimes\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,\delta}_{{\cal S}},

for
T\subset[n]
,
Footnote

10
implies that

\displaystyle\Tr

\displaystyle\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq(2q+1)^{2}\expectation\left[\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}[T]\sigma\right):\begin{array}[]{r}(\mathbf{c},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]\\
T\leftarrow S_{n,k}\\
(\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}](T,\mathsf{st})\end{array}\right],

where

\sigma=\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{\cal C}\otimes\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}_{\cal Z}\otimes\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}},

and
S_{n,k}
is the set of all subsets of
[n]
of size
k
.

Now, recall that the last thing that
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
does in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
is run
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
on
\mathbf{c}
to obtain
(\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
. Consider instead running
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
on
\mathbf{c}
immediately after
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]
outputs
\mathbf{c}
. Note that
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
only operates on the register holding
\mathbf{c}
and its own private state used for simulating
{H_{C}}
, so since
\mathsf{com}
has a
\frac{8}{2^{{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}}/2}}
-commuting simulator (
Definition

5.3
), we have that,

\displaystyle\Tr

\displaystyle\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq(2q+1)^{2}\left(\expectation\left[\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}[T]\sigma\right):\begin{array}[]{r}(\mathbf{c},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]\\
(\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathbf{c})\\
T\leftarrow S_{n,k}\\
\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}](T,\mathsf{st})\end{array}\right]+\frac{8q}{2^{2\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(2q+1)^{2}\epsilon+\frac{8q(2q+1)^{2}}{2^{2\lambda}},

where

\sigma=\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{\cal B}\otimes\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}_{\cal Z}\otimes\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}},

and where we denote the expectation inside the parantheses by
\epsilon
, and we plugged in
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
.

Towards bounding
\epsilon
, we now consider the following quantum sampling game.

•

Fix a state on register
{\cal S}
(and potentially other registers of arbitrary size), where
{\cal S}
is split into
n
registers
{\cal S}_{1},\dots,{\cal S}_{n}
of dimension 4, and fix
\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
.

•

Sample
T\subset[n]
as a uniformly random subset of size
k
.

•

For each
i\in T
, measure registers
{\cal S}_{i}
in the
(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{i})
-basis to obtain a matrix
\mathbf{R}_{T}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times|T|}
, and output
\Delta\left(\mathbf{R}_{T},\mathbf{R}_{T}^{*}\right)
.

Next, we argue that
\epsilon
is bounded by the quantum error probability
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}
(
Definition

3.11
) of the above game.
This corresponds to the trace distance between the initial state on register
{\cal S}
and an “ideal” state (as defined in
Definition

3.11
). This ideal state is supported on vectors
\ket{\bR_{\btheta^*}}
such that
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{\overline{T}})<\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{T},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{T})+\delta
. In particular, for any
\ket{\bR_{\btheta^*}}
with
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{T},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{T})=0
in the support of the ideal state, it holds that
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{\overline{T}})<\delta
. Thus, this ideal state is orthogonal to the subspace
\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,\delta}_{\cal S}
, and so it follows that
\epsilon
is bounded by
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}
.

Thus, by
3.12
,
\epsilon
is then bounded by
\sqrt{\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}}
, where
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}
is the
classical
error probability (
Definition

3.10
) of the following sampling game.

•

Let
\mathbf{R}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
be an arbitrary matrix.

•

Sample a uniformly random subset
T\subset[n]
of size
k
.

•

Let
\delta^{*}
be the fraction of columns
(\mathbf{R}_{i,0},\mathbf{R}_{i,1})
for
i\in T
that are non-zero, and output
\delta^{*}
.

The classical error of the above game is the probability that
\geq\delta^{*}+\delta
of the columns
(\mathbf{R}_{i,0},\mathbf{R}_{i,1})
for
i\in\overline{T}
are non-zero. Using the analysis in
Section

D.2
,
we can bound this probability by
2\exp(-2(1-k/n)^2\delta^2k)
.

•

For
\delta=0.25
, this probability is bounded by

2\exp(-2(0.25)^2(1-A/(A+B))^2A)<2^{-4\lambda-1},

for
A=50,B=100
.
Thus, we can bound
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}
by
2/2^{4\lambda}
and thus
\epsilon
by
\sqrt{2}/2^{2\lambda}
.

•

For
\delta=0.054
, this probability is bounded by

2\exp(-2(0.054)^2(1-A/(A+B))^2A)<2^{-4\lambda-1},

for
A=1050,B=2160
.
Thus, we can bound
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}
by
2/2^{4\lambda}
and thus
\epsilon
by
\sqrt{2}/2^{2\lambda}
.

Summarizing, we have that in either case,

\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\tau\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}(2q+1)^{2}+8q(2q+1)^{2}}{2^{2\lambda}}\leq\frac{64q^{3}}{2^{2\lambda}},

for
q\geq 4
.

∎

Thus, by gentle measurement (
Lemma

3.1
), the
\tau
defined in
6.5
is within
\frac{8q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}
trace distance of a state
\tau_{\mathsf{good}}
in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.25}
if
A=50,B=100
and in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.054}
if
A=1050,B=2160
.

For readability, we note that

{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}=\sum_{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{\cal C}\otimes\ket{\bR^*,\btheta^*}\bra{\bR^*,\btheta^*}_{\cal Z}\otimes\left({\mathbb{I}}-\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},H_{FS}(\mathbf{c}),\delta}\right)_{\cal S},

where for any
T
,

{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,\delta}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}:(\mathbf{R}_{T}\neq\mathbf{R}^{*}_{T})\vee(\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{\overline{T}})<\delta)}\ket{\bR_{\btheta^*}}\bra{\bR_{\btheta^*}}.

We require the following two sub-claims to complete the proof of
6.4
.

SubClaim 6.6
.

If
E
is the XOR extractor, then conditioned on
\tau
being in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.25}
, it holds that

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1].

Proof.

First note that if the
T
sent by
\mathsf{R}^{*}
to the sender is not equal to
H_{FS}(\mathbf{c})
, then the sender will abort, and the hybrids are perfectly indistinguishable. So it suffices to analyze the state
\tau
conditioned on the register that contains
T
being equal to
H_{FS}(\mathbf{c})
.

Now, if
\tau
is in
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.25}
, it must be the case that the register
{\cal S}
is in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,0.25}
, where
\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
were output by
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
. Recall that the sender aborts if the positions measured in
T
are not equal to
\mathbf{R}^{*}_{T}
, and in this case the hybrids would be perfectly indistinguishable. Thus, we can condition on the sender not aborting, which, by the definition of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi^{\mathbf{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},T,0.25}
implies that register
{\cal S}_{\overline{T}}
is supported on vectors
\ket{\left(\bR_{\overline{T}}\right)_{\btheta^*}}
such that
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{R}^{*}_{\overline{T}})<0.25
.

Now, to obtain
m_{1-b}
, the sender measures register
{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}
in basis
d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1
for each
i\in\overline{T}
to obtain a string
r^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n-k}
. Then,
m_{1-b}
is set to
E(r^{\prime})
. Since
b
is defined as
\mathsf{maj}\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{*}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, at least
(n-k)/2
of the bits
r^{\prime}_{i}
are obtained by measuring in
1\oplus\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{i}
. Let
M\subset\overline{T}
be this set of size at least
(n-k)/2
, and define
\mathbf{r}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{*}_{i}=\mathbf{R}^{*}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}
. We know from above that the register
{\cal S}_{M}
is supported on vectors
\ket{\left(\br_M\right)_{\btheta^*}}
for
\mathbf{r}_{M}
such that
\Delta(\mathbf{r}_{M},\mathbf{r}^{*}_{M})<0.5
. Thus, recalling that each of these states is measured in the basis
1\oplus\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{i}
, we can appeal to
Theorem

4.1
(with an appropriate change of basis) to show that
m_{1-b}
is perfectly uniformly random from
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s perspective, completing the proof.

∎

SubClaim 6.7
.

If
E
is the ROM extractor and
B\geq 326,q\geq 4
, then conditioned on
\tau
being in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.054}
, it holds that

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{4q}{2^{\lambda}}.

Proof.

This follows the same argument as the above sub-claim, until we see that there are
(n-k)/2
qubits of
{\cal S}
that are measured in basis
1\oplus\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{M}
, and that the state on these qubits is supported on vectors
\ket{\left(\br_M\right)_{\btheta^*}}
for
\mathbf{r}_{M}
such that
\Delta(\mathbf{r}_{M},\mathbf{r}^{*}_{M})<0.108
. We can then apply
Theorem

4.2
with random oracle input size
n-k
, register
{\cal X}
size
(n-k)/2
, and
|L|\leq 2^{h_{b}(0.108)(n-k)/2}
. Note that, when applying this theorem, we are fixing any outcome of the
(n-k)/2
bits of the random oracle input that are measured in
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
, and setting register
{\cal X}
to contain the
(n-k)/2
registers that are measured in basis
1\oplus\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
. This gives a bound of

\frac{4q2^{h_{b}(0.108)(n-k)/2}}{2^{(n-k)/4}}=\frac{4q}{2^{(n-k)(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2}h_{b}(0.108))}}=\frac{4q}{2^{B\lambda(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2}h_{b}(0.108))}}\leq\frac{4q}{2^{\lambda}},

for
B\geq 326
.

∎

This completes the proof of
6.4
.
∎

Receiver security.

Next, we show security against a malicious sender
\mathsf{S}^{*}
. During the proof, we will use an efficient quantum random oracle “wrapper” algorithm
W[(x,z)]
that provides an interface between any quantum random oracle simulator, such as the on-the-fly simulator (
3.6
), and the machine querying the random oracle. The wrapper will implement a controlled query to the actual random oracle simulator, controlled on the input
{\cal X}
register not being equal to
x
. Then, it will implement a controlled query to a unitary that maps
\ket{x,y}\to\ket{x,y \oplus z}
, controlled on the input
{\cal X}
register being equal to
x
. The effect of this wrapper is that the oracle presented to the machine is the oracle
H
simulated by the simulator, but with
H(x)
reprogrammed to
z
.

\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}]:

•

Query the ideal functionality with
\bot
and obtain
m_{0},m_{1}
.

•

Sample
T
as a uniformly random subset of
[n]
of size
k
, sample
d_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
for each
i\in\overline{T}
, and sample
\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{+,\times\}
for each
i\in T
.

•

For each
i\in[n]
, sample
r_{i,0},r_{i,1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and prepare BB84 states
\ket{\psi_{i,0}},\ket{\psi_{i,1}}
as follows.

–

If
i\in T
, set
\ket{\psi_{i,0}}=\ket{r_{i,0}}_{\theta_{i}},\ket{\psi_{i,1}}=\ket{r_{i,1}}_{\theta_{i}}
.

–

If
i\in\overline{T}
, set
\ket{\psi_{i,0}}=\ket{r_{i,0}}_{+},\ket{\psi_{i,1}}=\ket{r_{i,1}}_{\times}
.

•

For each
i\in T
, let
e_{i}\coloneqq(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i})
and for each
i\in\overline{T}
, let
e_{i}\coloneqq(0,0,0)
. Compute
(\mathsf{st},\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}(\{e_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
and
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in T}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{st},T)
.

•

Set
x_{0}\coloneqq E(\{r_{i,d_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})\oplus m_{0}
and
x_{1}\coloneqq E(\{r_{i,d_{i}\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})\oplus m_{1}
(where if
E
is the ROM extractor, this is accomplished via classical queries to an on-the-fly random oracle simulator for
H_{Ext}
).

•

Run
\mathsf{S}^{*}
on input
(x_{0},x_{1}),\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
,
\{\ket{\psi_{i,b}}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
. Answer
{H_{C}}
queries using the on-the-fly random oracle simulator, answer
H_{FS}
queries using the on-the-fly random oracle simulator wrapped with
W[\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T]
, and if
E
is the ROM extractor, answer
H_{Ext}
queries using the on-the-fly random oracle simulator. Output
\mathsf{S}^{*}
’s final state and continue to answering the distinguisher’s random oracle queries.

Now, given a receiver input
b\in\{0,1\}
, and distinguisher
\mathsf{D}
such that
\mathsf{S}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}
make a total of at most
q
queries combined to
H_{FS}
and
{H_{C}}
(and
H_{Ext}
), consider the following sequence of hybrids.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The result of the real interaction between
\mathsf{R}(b)
and
\mathsf{S}^{*}
. Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over
\{0,1\}
desrcibed by
\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*},\mathsf{D},b]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: This is the same as the previous hybrid except that
T
is sampled uniformly at random as in the simulator, and
H_{FS}
queries are answered with the wrapper
W[(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T)]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: This is the same as the previous hybrid except that the messages
\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
are replaced with
(0,0,0)
inside the commitent.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: The result of
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}]
interacting in
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}}
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]]}}
). Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over
\{0,1\}
described by
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}],\mathsf{D},b]
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}],\mathsf{D},b]
).

The proof of security against a malicious
\mathsf{S}^{*}
follows by combining the following three claims.

Claim 6.8
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1].

Proof.

These hybrids are identically distributed, since
H_{FS}
is a random oracle and
T
is uniformly random in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
.
∎

Claim 6.9
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{4q\sqrt{3(A+B)\lambda}}{2^{2\lambda}}.

Proof.

This follows directly from the hiding of the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.2
), which is implied by its equivocality (see
Section

5.1
). To derive the bound, we plug in
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
and
n=3(A+B)\lambda
to the bound from
Theorem

5.12
.
∎

Claim 6.10
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1].

Proof.

First, note that one difference in how the hybrids are specified is that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, the receiver samples
x_{1-b}
uniformly at random, while in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
,
x_{1-b}
is set to
E(\{r_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})\oplus m_{1-b}
. However, since
m_{1-b}
is sampled uniformly at random by the functionality, this is an equivalent distribution.

Thus, the only difference between these these hybrids is the basis in which the states on registers
\{{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
are prepared (which are the registers
\{{\cal S}_{i,\theta_{i}\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
). Indeed, note that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, the state on register
{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b_{i}\oplus 1}
is prepared by having the receiver measure their corresponding half of an EPR pair (register
{\cal R}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b_{i}\oplus 1}
) in basis
\theta_{i}=d_{i}\oplus b
, while in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, this state is prepared by sampling a uniformly random bit and encoding it in the basis
d_{i}\oplus b_{i}\oplus 1
. However, these sampling procedures both produce a maximally mixed state on register
{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}
, and thus these hybrids are equivalent.

∎

This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

6.2
Two-round OT without setup

In this section, we analyze a variant of the EPR-based protocol (
Fig.

7
) where we allow the sender to generate the EPR setup. That is, an honest sender will prepare
2n
EPR pairs between registers
{\cal R}
and
{\cal S}
, and send
{\cal R}
to the reciever, while a malicious sender may prepare and send an arbitary state.

Thus, the resulting protocol is a two-round protocol without setup. We show that it securely realizes the
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
OT ideal functionality, where the receiver can send chosen inputs
(b,m)
to the functionality and the functionality outputs to the sender random
(m_{0},m_{1})
such that
m_{b}=m
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Consider instantiating the two-round variant of Protocol
7
with any non-interactive commitment scheme that is
correct
(
Definition

5.1
),
equivocal
(
Definition

5.4
), and
extractable
(
Definition

5.3
). Then the following hold.

•

When instantiated with the XOR extractor, there exist constants
A,B
such that the two-round variant of Protocol
7
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
.

•

When instantiated with the ROM extractor, there exist constants
A,B
such that the two-round variant of Protocol
7
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
.

Letting
\lambda
be the security parameter,
q
be an upper bound on the total number of random oracle queries made by the adversary, and using the commitment scheme from
Section

5.2
with security parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
, the following hold.

•

When instantiatied with the XOR extractor and constants
A=50
,
B=100
, the two-round variant of Protocol
7
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
with
\mu_{{\cal R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{{\cal S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{{\cal R}^{*}}=\left(\frac{8q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{3600\lambda q}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{148(450\lambda+q+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}\right),\mu_{{\cal S}^{*}}=\left(\frac{85\lambda^{1/2}q}{2^{2\lambda}}\right).

This requires a total of
2(A+B)\lambda=300\lambda
EPR pairs.

•

When instantiated with the ROM extractor and constants
A=1050
,
B=2160
, the two-round variant of Protocol
7
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
with
\mu_{{\cal R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{{\cal S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{{\cal R}^{*}}=\left(\frac{8q^{3/2}+4\lambda}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{77040\lambda q}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{148(9630\lambda+q+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}\right),\mu_{{\cal S}^{*}}=\left(\frac{197\lambda^{1/2}q}{2^{2\lambda}}\right).

This requires a total of
2(A+B)\lambda=6420\lambda
EPR pairs.

Then, applying non-interactive bit OT reversal (
3.3
) to the protocol that realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
immediately gives the following corollary.

Corollary 6.12
.

Given a setup of
300\lambda
shared EPR pairs, there exists a one-message protocol in the QROM that
O\left(\frac{q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}\right)
-securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{R}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
.

Proof.

Security against a malicious receiver remains the same as
Theorem

6.1
, so we only show security against a malicious sender. Let
\mathsf{S}^{*}
be a malicious sender. Let
(\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open})
be the equivocal simulator for the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.4
).

\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}]:

•

Run
\mathsf{S}^{*}
. Answer
H_{FS}
(and
H_{Ext}
) queries using the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator, and answer
{H_{C}}
queries using
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
. Eventually,
\mathsf{S}^{*}
outputs a state on register
{\cal R}=({\cal R}_{1,0},{\cal R}_{1,1},\dots,{\cal R}_{n,0},{\cal R}_{n,1})
.

•

Query the ideal functionality with
\bot
and obtain
m_{0},m_{1}
.

•

Run the following strategy on behalf of the receiver.

–

Compute
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
.

–

Compute
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}\|\dots\|c_{n})
and parse
T
as a subset of
[n]
of size
k
.

–

For each
i\in T
, sample
\theta\leftarrow\{+,\times\}
and measure registers
{\cal R}_{i,0}
and
{\cal R}_{i,1}
in basis
\theta_{i}
to obtain
r_{i,0},r_{i,1}
.

–

Compute
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in T}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}(\{r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in T})
.

–

For each
i\in\overline{T}
, measure register
{\cal R}_{i,0}
in basis
+
and register
{\cal R}_{i,1}
in basis
\times
to obtain
r_{i,0},r_{i,1}
.

–

For each
i\in\overline{T}
, sample
d_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
. Compute
x_{0}\coloneqq E(\{r_{i,d_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})\oplus m_{0},x_{1}\coloneqq E(\{r_{i,d_{i}\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})\oplus m_{1}
.

•

Send
(x_{0},x_{1}),\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
to
\mathsf{S}^{*}
, and run
\mathsf{S}^{*}
until it outputs a final state, answering
H_{FS}
(and
H_{Ext}
) queries using the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator and
{H_{C}}
queries using
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
. Output
\mathsf{S}^{*}
’s final state.

•

Answer the distinguisher’s
H_{FS}
(and
H_{Ext}
) queries using the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator and
{H_{C}}
queries using
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
.

Now, given a distinguisher
\mathsf{D}
such that
\mathsf{S}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}
make a total of at most
q
queries combined to
H_{FS}
and
{H_{C}}
, and a receiver input
(b,m_{b})
, consider the following sequence of hybrids.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The result of the real interaction between
\mathsf{S}^{*}
and
\mathsf{R}
. Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over bits described by
\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*},\mathsf{D},(b,m_{b})]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: Answer all
{H_{C}}
queries of
\mathsf{S}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}
with
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
. Run the honest receiver strategy, except
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
, and
\{u_{i}\}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}(\{(r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in T})
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: The result of
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}]
interacting in
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}}
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]]}}
). Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over bits described by
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}],\mathsf{D},(b,m_{b})]
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}],\mathsf{D},(b,m_{b})]
).

Claim 6.13
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]|\leq\frac{2q\sqrt{3(A+B)\lambda}}{2^{2\lambda}}.

Proof.

This follows by a direct reduction to equivocality of the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.4
). Indeed, let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit}}
be the machine that runs
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
until
\mathsf{S}^{*}
outputs its message on register
{\cal R}
and
\mathsf{R}
runs the
\mathbf{Measurement}
portion of its honest strategy to produce
\{r_{i,0},r_{i,1},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen}}
be the machine computes
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}\|\dots\|c_{n})
. Let
\mathsf{D}
be the machine that runs the rest of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, from the
\mathbf{Reorientation}
portion of its honest receiver’s strategy to the final bit output by the distinguisher.

Then, plugging in
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
and
n=3(A+B)\lambda
to
Theorem

5.12
gives the bound in the claim.
∎

Claim 6.14
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1].

Proof.

First, note that one difference in how the hybrids are specified is that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, the receiver samples
x_{1-b}
uniformly at random, while in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
,
x_{1-b}
is set to
E(\{r_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}})\oplus m_{1-b}
. However, since
m_{1-b}
is sampled uniformly at random by the functionality, this is an equivalent distribution.

Then, the only difference between these these hybrids is the basis in which the states on registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
are measured (which are the registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,\theta_{i}\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
). Indeed, since the resulting bits
r_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}
are unused by the receiver in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, and masked by
m_{1-b}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, they are independent of the sender’s view. Thus, measuring them in different bases has no effect on the sender’s view, and so the hybrids are identical.
∎

This completes the proof of the claim, as desired.
∎

7
The fixed basis framework: OT without entanglement or setup

In
Fig.

8
, we formalize our 3 round chosen-input OT protocol that does not rely on entanglement or setup.

Protocol
8

Ingredients / parameters / notation.

•

Security parameter
\lambda
and constants
A,B
. Let
k=A\lambda,n=(A+B)\lambda
.

•

For classical bits
(x,\theta)
, let
\ket{x}_{\theta}
denote
\ket{x}
if
\theta=0
, and
(\ket{0}+(-1)^{x}\ket{1})/\sqrt{2}
if
\theta=1
.

•

A non-interactive extractable and equivocal commitment
(\mathsf{Com},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec})
, where commitments to 3 bits have size
\ell\coloneqq\ell(\lambda)
.

•

A random oracle
H_{FS}:\{0,1\}^{n\ell}\to\{0,1\}^{\lceil\log\binom{n}{k}\rceil}
, and
a universal hash function family
h:\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\times\{0,1\}^{\leq B\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

Sender Input:
Messages
m_{0},m_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.
Receiver Input:
Choice bit b.

1.

Sender Message.

\mathsf{S}
samples strings
r^{0}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n},r^{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, a random subset
U\subset[n]
of size
k
, and for
i\in U
, it samples
b_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
uniformly at random.
It computes state
\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi}_{1}\ldots\ket{\psi}_{n}
as follows, and sends it to
\mathsf{R}
: for
i\in U
,
\ket{\psi}_{i}=(\ket{r^0_i}_{b_{i}},\ket{r^1_i}_{b_{i}})
and for
i\in[n]\setminus U
,
\ket{\psi}_{i}=(\ket{r^0_i}_{0},\ket{r^1_i}_{1})
.

2.

Receiver Message.

\mathsf{R}
does the following.

•

Choose
\widehat{\theta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and measure the
i^{th}
pair of qubits in basis
\widehat{\theta}_{i}
to obtain
\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1}
.

•

Measurement Check Message.

–

Compute
\left(\mathsf{st},\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}\left(\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\right)
.

–

Compute
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}||\dots||c_{n})
and parse
T
as a subset of
[n]
of size
k
.

–

Compute
\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{st},T)
.

•

Reorientation.
Let
\overline{T}\coloneqq[n]\setminus T
, and for all
i\in\overline{T}
, set
d_{i}=b\oplus\widehat{\theta}_{i}
.

•

Message.
Send to
\mathsf{S}
the values
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
.

3.

Sender Message.

\mathsf{S}
does the following.

•

Check Receiver Message.

\mathsf{S}
aborts if any of these checks fail:

–

Check that
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}||\ldots||c_{n})
.

–

Check that
\mathsf{Rec}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in T},T,\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T})\neq\bot
.

–

For every
i\in T\cap U
such that
\widehat{\theta}_{i}=b_{i}
, check that
\widehat{r_{i}}^{0}=r_{i}^{0}
and
\widehat{r_{i}}^{1}=r_{i}^{1}
.

•

Message.
Sample
s\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}
, let
R_{\beta}
denote the concatenation of
\{r^{d_{i}\oplus\beta}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
and send to
\mathsf{R}
the values

\left(s,U,ct_{0}=m_{0}\oplus h(s,R_{0}),ct_{1}=m_{1}\oplus h(s,R_{1})\right)
.

4.

Receiver Output.

Output

m_{b}=ct_{b}\oplus h(s,R)

where
R
is the concatenation
\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
.

Figure 8:
Three-round chosen-input OT without entanglement

Theorem 7.1
.

Instantiate Protocol
8
with any non-interactive commitment scheme that is
extractable
(
Definition

5.3
) and
equivocal
(
Definition

5.4
). Then there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
8
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
.

Furthermore, letting
\lambda
be the security parameter,
q
be an upper bound on the total number of random oracle queries made by the adversary, and using the commitment scheme from
Section

5.2
with security parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
, for constants
A=11\ 700,B=30\ 400
, Protocol
8
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\frac{3\sqrt{10}q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{2^{5\lambda}}+\frac{148(q+126300\lambda+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}+\frac{1010400q\lambda}{2^{2\lambda}},\quad\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\left(\frac{712q\lambda^{1/2}}{2^{2\lambda}}\right).

This requires a total of
2(A+B)\lambda=84\ 200\lambda
BB84 states.

Proof.

We begin by proving security against malicious senders below.

Receiver security

We now describe a simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
that simulates the view of an arbitrary malicious sender
\mathsf{S}^{*}
.
\mathsf{Sim}
will answer random oracle queries to
H
using
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
, the random oracle simulator for the commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
. Additionally, the queries to
H_{FS}
will be simulated using an efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
as mentioned in Imported Theorem
3.6
.

The Simulator.

\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}]
does the following.

1.

Receive
\{\ket{\psi}\}_{i\in[n]}
from
\mathsf{S}^{*}
.

2.

Perform the following steps.

•

Measurement Check Message.

–

Compute
\left(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
.

–

Compute
T=H_{FS}(c_{1}||\dots||c_{n})
and parse
T
as a subset of
[n]
of size
k
.

–

Perform (delayed) measurements on
\{\ket{\psi}\}_{i\in[n]}
as follows:

*

Sample
\widehat{\theta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
.

*

For all
i\in T
, measure the
i^{th}
pair of qubits in basis
\widehat{\theta}_{i}
to obtain
\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1}
.

*

For all
i\in\overline{T}
, measure the first qubit of
\ket{\psi}_{i}
in the computational basis and the second qubit in the Hadamard basis to obtain
\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1}
respectively.

–

Compute
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}(\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]})
.

•

Reorientation.
Let
\overline{T}=[n]\setminus T
, and for all
i\in\overline{T}
, set
d_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{i}
.

•

Message.
Send to
\mathsf{S}

\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i},u_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}.

3.

Upon receiving
(s,U,ct_{0},ct_{1})
from
\mathsf{S}^{*}
,

•

Set
R_{0}
to be the concatenation
\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
and
R_{1}
to be the concatenation of
\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
.

•

Compute
\widehat{m_{0}}:=ct_{0}\oplus h(s,R_{0})
,
\widehat{m_{1}}:=ct_{1}\oplus h(s,R_{1})
,
and send
\widehat{m_{0}},\widehat{m_{1}}
to the ideal functionality.

Analysis.

Fix any adversary
\{\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda}
is a QIOM that corrupts the sender,
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
is a QOM, and
b_{\lambda}
is the input of the honest receiver.
For any receiver input
b_{\lambda}\in\{0,1\}
consider the random variables
\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}]
and

\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}]

according to Definition
3.2
for the protocol in Figure
8
.
Let
q(\cdot)
denote an upper bound on the combined number of queries of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
.
We will show that :

\bigg|\Pr[\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}]=1]-\Pr[\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}]=1]\bigg|=\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda)).

This is done via a sequence of hybrids, as follows:

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The output of this hybrid is the
real
distribution
\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: The output of this hybrid is the same as the previous hybrid except that the challenger uses switches
H_{FS}
with an efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
as mentioned in Imported Theorem
3.6
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: The output of this hybrid is the same as the previous hybrid except that instead of running
(\mathsf{Com},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open})
, the challenger uses
(\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO},\allowbreak\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\allowbreak\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open})
to prepare their commitments. It answers any random oracle queries to
{H_{C}}
by calling
\mathsf{Sim}\mathsf{Equ}.\mathsf{RO}
instead.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: The output of this hybrid is the same as the previous hybrid except that the measurement of
\{\ket{\psi}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
on behalf of
\mathsf{R}
is done after computing set
T
and before invoking
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}
on the measured values.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
: The output of this hybrid is the same as the previous hybrid except the following modification on behalf of
\mathsf{R}
, for all
i\in\overline{T}
:

–

Sample
\widehat{\theta_{i}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}

–

Measure the first qubit of
\ket{\psi}_{i}
in the computational basis and the second qubit in the Hadamard basis. Let the outcomes be
\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1}
respectively.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
: The output of this hybrid is the same as the previous hybrid except the following modification.

–

For
i\in\overline{T}
, set reorientation bit
d_{i}:=\widehat{\theta_{i}}
.

–

After receiving the last sender message.

*

Set
R_{0}
to be the concatenation
\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
and
R_{1}
to be the concatenation of
\{\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
.

*

Compute
\widehat{m_{0}}:=ct_{0}\oplus h(s,R_{0})
,
\widehat{m_{1}}:=ct_{1}\oplus h(s,R_{1})
,
and send
\widehat{m_{0}},\widehat{m_{1}}
to the ideal functionality.

The output of this last hybrid is identical to the
ideal
distribution
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},b_{\lambda}]
.

We show that
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]|\leq\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
, where
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
is a
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
-equivocal bit commitment scheme, where
\mu(\lambda,q,n_{\mathsf{com}})=\frac{2qn_{\mathsf{com}}^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}
for the specific commitment scheme that we construct in Section
5.2
, where
n_{\mathsf{com}}
is the number of bit commitments and
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}
is the security parameter for the commitment scheme. Later, we will set
n_{\mathsf{com}}=c_{1}\lambda
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=c_{2}\lambda
for some fixed constants
c_{1},c_{2}
. Thus
\mu
will indeed be a function of
\lambda
and
q
.
We now procced with the proof by arguing indistinguishability of each pair of consecutive hybrids in the sequence above.

Claim 7.2
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]
.

Proof.

This follows from the indistinguishable simulation property of
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
as mention in the Imported Theorem
3.6
.
∎

Claim 7.3
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
.

Proof.

Suppose there exists an adversary
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
corrupting
\mathsf{S}
, a distinguisher
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
,
and a bit
b
such that,

\bigg|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]\bigg|>\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))

We will build a reduction adversary
\{\mathsf{Adv}^{*}_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{D}^{*}_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that makes at most
q(\lambda)
queries to the random oracle, and contradicts the
\mu
-equivocality of the commitment
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
as defined in Definition
5.4
. In the following reduction, all random oracle queries to
{H_{C}}
will be answered by the equivocal commitment challenger whereas calls to
H_{FS}
will be simulated by
\mathsf{Adv}^{*}_{\lambda}
by internally running
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
.

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}
:

•

Initalize the OT protocol with between honest receiver
\mathsf{R}
and
\mathsf{Adv}
corrupting
\mathsf{S}
.

•

Output intermediate state
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,1}
representing the joint state of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
along with the measurement information
\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
computed by
\mathsf{R}
.

The measurement information
\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
is sent as messages to the reduction challenger which then returns a set of commitments
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}(\rho^{*}_{\lambda,1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
: Use
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,1}
to initialize the joint state of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
, and output the new joint state
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,2}
after
\mathsf{R}
has computed
T
.

The challenger returns
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
which is then fed to the following distinguisher (along with the information
\{\mathsf{com}_{i},(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
from the aforementioned execution).

\mathsf{D}^{*}_{\lambda}(\rho^{*}_{\lambda,2},\{\mathsf{com}_{i},(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}):

•

Use
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,2}
to initialize the joint state of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
. Run it until completion using
\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
as openings of
\mathsf{R}
in the measurement check proof

•

Let
\tau_{\lambda}^{*}
be the final state of
\mathsf{Adv}
and
y^{*}
be the output of
\mathsf{R}
. Run
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}(\tau^{*}_{\lambda},y^{*})
and output the bit
b
returned by the distinguisher.

By construction, when the challenger executes
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}})
, the reduction will generate a distribution identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Similarly, when the challenger executes
(\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open})
, the reduction will generate a distribution identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. Therefore, the reduction
\{\mathsf{Adv}^{*}_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{D}^{*}_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
directly contradicts the
\mu
-equivocality of the underlying commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
as in Definition
5.4
.
∎

Claim 7.4
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]

Proof.

The only difference in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
is that we commute the measurement of
\{\ket{\psi}\}_{i\in[n]}
past the invocation of
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
and the computation of
T
. Since these two operators are applied to disjoint subsystems, this can be done without affecting the hybrid distribution.
∎

Claim 7.5
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]

Proof.

The only difference in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
is the following. For all
i\in\overline{T}
: If
\widehat{\theta_{i}}=0
, we measure the second qubit of
\ket{\psi_i}
in the Hadamard basis (instead of the computational basis as defined in the previous hybrid). If
\widehat{\theta_{i}}=1
, we measure the first qubit of
\ket{\psi_i}
in computational basis (instead of the Hadamard basis as defined in the previous hybrid). But this doesn’t affect the hybrid distribution because the values on these registers are not used anywhere in the hybrid and are eventually traced out.
∎

Claim 7.6
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]

Proof.

The only difference between these experiments is the way in which we define the output of honest receiver. Assuming the correctness of
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
, the two hybrids are identical. In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
, the receiver’s output is computed by the challenger as
\widehat{m_{b}}=ct_{b}\oplus h(s,||_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}})
(where
||_{i\in G}x_{i}
denotes the concatenation of
x_{i}
for
i\in G
, in increasing order of
i
). In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
, the receiver’s output is derived via the OT ideal functionality which receives sender’s input strings
\widehat{m_{0}}:=ct_{0}\oplus h(s,||_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}})
and
\widehat{m_{1}}:=ct_{1}\oplus h(s,||_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}\oplus 1})
from the challenger and receiver choice bit
b
. The OT ideal functionality sends
\widehat{m_{b}}=ct_{b}\oplus h(s,||_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}\widehat{r_{i}}^{\widehat{\theta}_{i}\oplus b})
to the ideal receiver which it then outputs. Therefore for any fixing of the adversary’s state and receiver choice bit, the two hybrids result in identical
\widehat{m_{b}}
.
∎

Combining all the claims, we get that
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]|\leq\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
. Using Theorem
5.12
where we derived
\mu(\lambda,q,n_{\mathsf{com}})=\frac{2qn_{\mathsf{com}}^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}
and plugging
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
,
n_{\mathsf{com}}=3n
(as we are committing to 3 bits at a time) where
n=42\ 100\lambda
(this setting of
n
is the same as that needed in the sender security part of the proof), we get
\frac{712q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{2\lambda}}
security against a malicious sender.
∎

Sender security

Let
\mathsf{SimExt}=(\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext})
be the simulator for the extractable commitment scheme from
Section

5
. Let
+
refer to the computational basis and
\times
to the hadamard basis. Below we describe the simulator
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
against a malicious receiver
\mathsf{R}^{*}
for Protocol
8
.

\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
:

•

Initialize the on-the-fly random oracle simulator
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
from
3.6
. Run
\mathsf{R}^{*}
answering its oracle queries to
H_{FS}
using
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
and queries to
{H_{C}}
using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
.

•

Sample
2n
EPR pairs on registers
\{({\cal S}_{i,b},{\cal R}_{i,b})\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
(where each
{\cal S}_{i,b},{\cal R}_{i,b}
is a 2-dimensional register). Send registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathsf{R}^{*}
.

•

When
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]},T,\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
, run
\{(\widetilde{r_{i}}^{0},\widetilde{r_{i}}^{1},\widetilde{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
.

•

Run the “check receiver message" part of the honest sender strategy, except do the following in place of the third check. If any of the checks fail, send
\mathsf{abort}
to the ideal functionality, output
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s state and continue answering distinguisher’s queries.

–

Sample subset
U\subset[n]
of size
k
and for each
i\in[n]
, sample bit
b_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.

–

For each
i\in[n]
, do the following:

*

If
i\in U
, measure both registers
{\cal S}_{i,0}
and
{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
+
when
b_{i}=0
, and both in basis
\times
when
b_{i}=1
. Denote measurement outcomes from
{\cal S}_{i,0}
and
{\cal S}_{i,1}
by
r_{i}^{0}
and
r_{i}^{1}
respectively.

*

If
i\notin U
, then measure
{\cal S}_{i,0}
in basis
+
and
{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
\times
and denote outcomes by outcomes
r_{i}^{0},r_{i}^{1}
respectively.

–

For each
i\in T\cap U
such that
\widetilde{\theta}_{i}=b_{i}
, check that
\widetilde{r_{i}}^{0}=r_{i}^{0}
and
\widetilde{r_{i}}^{1}=r_{i}^{1}
.

•

Set
b\coloneqq\mathsf{maj}\{\widetilde{\theta}_{i}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
and send
b
to
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
to obtain
m_{b}
.

•

Compute the last message using the honest sender strategy except for using
m_{1-b}\coloneqq 0^{\lambda}
.

•

Send
\mathsf{R}^{*}
this last message, output the final state of
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and terminate.

•

Answer any queries of distinguisher to
H_{FS}
and
{H_{C}}
using
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}
and
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
respectively.

Fix any distinguisher
\mathsf{D}
and let
q
denote the total queries that
\mathsf{R}^{*},\mathsf{D}
make to
H_{FS}
and
{H_{C}}
. Consider the following sequence of hybrids:

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: This is the real world interaction between
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and
\mathsf{S}
. Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over
\{0,1\}
denoted by
\Pi[\mathsf{R}^{*},\mathsf{D},(m_{0},m_{1})]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: This is the same as the previous hybrid, except the following are run instead to generate the first sender message: (1) Sample
2n
EPR pairs on registers
\{({\cal S}_{i,b},{\cal R}_{i,b})\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
. (2) Run the following algorithm:

Algorithm Measure-EPR
:

–

Sample subset
U\subset[n]
of size
k
and for each
i\in[n]
, sample bit
b_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.

–

For each
i\in[n]
, do the following:

*

If
i\in U
, measure registers
{\cal S}_{i,0}
and
{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
+
when
b_{i}=0
, and in basis
\times
when
b_{i}=1
, to get outcomes
r_{i}^{0}
and
r_{i}^{1}
respectively.

*

If
i\notin U
, then measure
{\cal S}_{i,0}
in basis
+
and
{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
\times
to get outcomes
r_{i}^{0},r_{i}^{1}
respectively.

Thereafter, registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
are sent over to
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and the rest of the experiment works as the previous hybrid.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: This is the same previous hybrid, except that the sender does not perform any measurements before sending registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathsf{R}^{*}
, and
delays running the algorithm Measure-EPR to just before executing the third check in “check receiver message" part of the honest sender strategy.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: This is the same as the previous hybrid, except for the following changes: queries of
\mathsf{R}^{*}
to
{H_{C}}
are now answered using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
. Once
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its second message, run
\{(\widetilde{r_{i}}^{0},\widetilde{r_{i}}^{1},\widetilde{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
. Thereafter,
\{(\widetilde{r_{i}}^{0},\widetilde{r_{i}}^{1},\widetilde{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in T}
are used for the third check in the “check receiver part" of the honest sender strategy (instead of using
\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in T}
).

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
: This is the result of the interaction between
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
,
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
and honest sender
\mathsf{S}
. Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is denoted by
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}],\mathsf{D},(m_{0},m_{1})]
.

We prove the indistinguishability between the hybrids using the following claims:

Claim 7.7
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]

Proof.

The only difference between the two hybrids is in how
\mathsf{S}
samples the state on the registers that it sends to
\mathsf{R}^{*}
. Denote the registers that
\mathsf{S}
sends to
\mathsf{R}^{*}
in either hybrid by
\{{\cal R}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
.
In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, each pair
({\cal R}_{i,0},{\cal R}_{i,1})
contains state
(\ket{r_i^0}_{b_{i}},\ket{r_i^1}_{b_{i}})
, for
i\in U
and
b_{i}
chosen uniformly from
\{+,\times\}
, and
(\ket{r_i^0}_{0},\ket{r_i^1}_{1})
for
i\notin U
, and for independently uniformly sampled bits
r_{i}^{0},r_{i}^{1}
.
In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, the challenger prepares
2n
EPR pairs on registers
\{({\cal S}_{i,b},{\cal R}_{i,b})\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
, then for every
i\in U
measures the pair
{\cal S}_{i,0},{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
b_{i}
that is uniformly sampled from
\{+,\times\}
, and for
i\notin U
measures
{\cal S}_{i,0},{\cal S}_{i,1}
in basis
0,1
respectively.
By elementary properties of EPR pairs, each register
{\cal R}_{i,b}
is in a state
\ket{r}
for a uniformly independently sampled bit
r
and in a basis that is chosen from the same distribution in both experiments.
∎

Claim 7.8
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]

Proof.

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
,
\mathsf{S}
measures the registers
\{{\cal S}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
first, after which
\mathsf{R}^{*}
operates on registers
\{{\cal R}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[n],b\in\{0,1\}}
. In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
,
\mathsf{S}
performs the same measurements, but after receiving the second round message from
\mathsf{R}^{*}
. Indistinguishability follows because measurements on disjoint sub-systems commute.
∎

Claim 7.9
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]|\leq\frac{148(q+3n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}+\frac{24qn}{2^{2\lambda}}.

Proof.

This follows by a direct reduction to extractability of the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.3
). Indeed, let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit}}
be the machine that runs
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
until
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message, which includes
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open}}
be the machine that takes as input the rest of the state of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, which includes
T
and the openings
\{(\widehat{r_{i}}^{0},\widehat{r_{i}}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
, and outputs
T
and these openings. Let
\mathsf{D}
be the machine that runs the rest of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and outputs a bit.

Then, plugging in
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
,
Definition

5.3
when applied to
(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit}},\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open}},\mathsf{D})
implies that the hybrids cannot be distinguished except with probability

\frac{148(q+3n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}+\frac{24qn}{2^{2\lambda}},

since we are committing to a total of
3n
bits.
∎

Claim 7.10
.

For
A=11\ 700,B=30\ 400
, and
q\geq 5
,

\displaystyle|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]|\leq\frac{3\sqrt{10}q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{2^{5\lambda}}

Proof.

The only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
is that
m_{1-b}=0^{\lambda}
, where
b=\mathsf{maj}\{\widetilde{\theta}_{i}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
. In what follows, we show that
m_{1-b}
is masked with a string that is (statistically close to) uniformly random from even given the view of
\mathsf{R}^{*}
in either hybrid, which implies the given claim.

Notation:

We setup some notation before proceeding.

•

Let
\mathbf{c}\coloneqq(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
be the classical commitments and
\mathbf{b}\coloneqq(b_{1},\dots b_{n})
be the bits sampled by the sender while executing its checks.

•

Write the classical extracted values
\{(\widetilde{r_{i}}^{0},\widetilde{r_{i}}^{1},\widetilde{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
as matrices

\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{r_{1}}^{0}\ \dots\ \widetilde{r_{n}}^{0}\\
\widetilde{r_{1}}^{1}\ \dots\ \widetilde{r_{n}}^{1}\end{bmatrix},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\theta}_{1}\ \dots\ \widetilde{\theta}_{n}\end{bmatrix}.

•

Given any
\mathbf{R}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, define
\ket{\bR_{\btheta}}
as a state on
n

4
-dimensional registers, where register
i
contains the state
\ket{\bR_{i,0},\bR_{i,1}}
prepared in the
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})
-basis.

•

Given
\mathbf{R},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
and a subset
T\subseteq[n]
, define
\mathbf{R}_{T}
be the columns of
\mathbf{R}
indexed by
T
, and define
\Delta\left(\mathbf{R}_{T},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{T}\right)
as the fraction of columns
i\in T
such that
(\mathbf{R}_{i,0},\mathbf{R}_{i,1})\neq(\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{i,0},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{i,1})
.

•

For
T\subset[n]
, let
\overline{T}\coloneqq[n]\setminus T
.

•

Given
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
T\subseteq[n]
,
U\subseteq[n]
,
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
, define

\Pi^{\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},T,U,\mathbf{b},\delta}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R}\,:\,\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}},\Delta\left(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}\setminus U},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T}\setminus U}\right)\geq\delta\\
\text{where }S^{\prime}=\{j\,|\,j\in T\cap U\,\wedge\,\mathbf{b}_{j}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}\}\end{subarray}}\ket{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}\bra{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}.

Now, consider the following projection, which has hard-coded the description of
H_{FS}
:

\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\mathbf{b},\\
U\subseteq[n],|U|=k\end{subarray}}\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\Pi^{\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},H_{FS}(\mathbf{c}),U,\mathbf{b},\delta}_{{\cal S}},

where
{\cal C}
is the register holding the classical commitments,
{\cal Z}_{1}
is the register holding the output of
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
,
{\cal Z}_{2}
is the register holding the subset
U
and bits
\mathbf{b}
sampled by sender, and
{\cal S}
denotes all the registers holding the sender’s halves of EPR pairs.

SubClaim 7.11
.

Let

\tau\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b}}p^{(\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b})}\tau^{(\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b})},

where

\tau^{(\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b})}=\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\rho^{(\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b})}_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}

is the entire state of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
(equivalently also
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
) immediately after
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message (which includes
\mathbf{c}
),
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
is run to get
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, and sender samples the set
U\subseteq[n]
of size
d
and bits
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Here, each
p^{(\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b})}
is the probability that the string
\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b}
is contained in the registers
{\cal C},{\cal Z}_{1},{\cal Z}_{2}
. Also,
{\cal S}
is the register holding the sender’s halves of EPR pairs and
{\cal X}
is a register holding remaining state of the system, which includes the rest of the receiver’s classical message and its private state. Then, for
A=11\ 700,B=30\ 400
and for
q\geq 5
,

\Tr(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{11/200} \tau)\leq\frac{45q^{3}}{2^{2\lambda}}

Proof.

Define
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}^{H_{FS}}
to be the oracle machine that runs
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
until
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs
\mathbf{c}
(and the rest of its message), then runs
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
to obtain
\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}
, followed by sampling the set
U\subseteq[n]
of size
d
, and bits
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
in the register
{\cal Z}_{2}
, and finally outputting the remaining state
\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}
. Consider running the measure-and-reprogram simulator
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]
from
Footnote

10
, which simulates
H_{FS}
queries, measures and outputs
\mathbf{c}
, then receives a uniformly random subset
T\subset[n]
of size
k
, and then continues to run
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
until it outputs
\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}
. Letting

\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}[T]\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\mathbf{b}\\
U\subseteq[n],|U|=d\end{subarray}}\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\Pi^{\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},T,U,\mathbf{b},\delta}_{{\cal S}},

for
T\subset[n]
,
Footnote

10
implies that
\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\tau\right)\leq(2q+1)^{2}\gamma
, where

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\expectation\left[\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}[T]\left(\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{\cal C}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}\right)\right)\right]

with expectation defined over the following experiment:

•

(\mathbf{c},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]
,

•

T\leftarrow S_{n,k}
, the set of all subsets of
[n]
of size
k
,

•

\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\\
\mathbf{b},\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}](T,\mathsf{st})
.

Now, recall that one of the last things that
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
does in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
is run
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
on
\mathbf{c}
to obtain
(\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
. Consider instead running
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
on
\mathbf{c}
immediately after
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]
outputs
\mathbf{c}
. Note that
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
only operates on the register holding
\mathbf{c}
and its own private state used for simulating
{H_{C}}
, so since
\mathsf{Com}^{{H_{C}}}
has a
\frac{8}{2^{{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}}/2}}
-commuting simulator (
Definition

5.3
), we have that,

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq(2q+1)^{2}\left(\epsilon+\frac{8q}{2^{{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}}/2}}\right)

(5)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon\coloneqq\expectation\left[\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}[T]\left(\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{\cal C}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}\right)\right)\right]

(6)

over the randomness of the following experiment:

•

(\mathbf{c},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}]
,

•

(\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\mathbf{c})
,

•

T\leftarrow S_{n,k}
,

•

\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},U,\mathbf{b},\rho_{{\cal S},{\cal X}}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}](T,\mathsf{st})
.

The sampling of
T,U\subseteq[n]
each of size
k
uniformly and independently at random in the experiment above is equivalent to the following sampling strategy.
First, sample the size of their intersection, i.e. sample and fix
s=|T\cap U|
, this fixes the size of
T\cup U
to be
2k-s
, since
|T|=|U|=s
.
Next sample and fix a set
T^{\prime}
of size
2k-s
(that will eventually represent the union
T\cup U
). Finally, sample a subset
S\subset T^{\prime}
of size
s
(which will eventually represent the intersection
T\cap U
), and then obtain
T
and
U
by paritioning
T^{\prime}\setminus S
into two random subsets each of size
k-s
, and then computing the union of each set with
S
.
This is described formally below.

•

Fix a state on register
{\cal S}
(and potentially other registers of arbitrary size), where
{\cal S}
is split into
n
registers
{\cal S}_{1},\dots,{\cal S}_{n}
of dimension 4, and fix
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

Sample two independent and uniform subsets of
[n]
each of size
k
.
Let
s
denote the size of their intersection.
Fix
s
, and discard the subsets themselves.

•

Sample a random subset
T^{\prime}
of
[n]
, of size
2k-s
.

•

Sample subsets
T,U,S^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime}
as follows:

–

Sample and fix a random subset set of size
s
of
T^{\prime}
, call this subset
S
.

–

Partition
T^{\prime}\setminus S
(note: this has size
2k-2s
) into two equal sets
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
of size
k-s
.

This can be done by first sampling a set
W_{1}
of size
k-s
uniformly at random from
T^{\prime}\setminus S
and setting
W_{2}=(T^{\prime}\setminus S)\setminus W_{1}
.

–

Let
T=W_{1}\cup S
and
U=W_{2}\cup S
.

–

Sample bits
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and set
S^{\prime}=\{j\,|\,j\in S\,\wedge\,\mathbf{b}_{j}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}\}
.

•

For each
i\in S^{\prime}
, measure the register
{\cal S}_{i}
in basis
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}
to get
\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times|S^{\prime}|}
. Output
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}})
.

The quantum error probability
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}
(
Definition

3.11
) of the above game corresponds to the trace distance between the initial state on register
{\cal S}
and an “ideal” state (as defined in
Definition

3.11
). This ideal state is supported on vectors
\ket{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}
such that
|\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}})-\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}})|<\delta
. In particular, for any
\ket{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}
with
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}})=0
in the support of the ideal state, it holds that
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}})<\delta
, or
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}\setminus U},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T}\setminus U})<\delta
(since
\overline{T^{\prime}}=\overline{T}\setminus U
in the sampling game above). Thus, this ideal state is orthogonal to the subspace
\Pi^{\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},T,U,\mathbf{b},\delta}_{\cal S}
, and so it follows that
\epsilon
is bounded by
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}
.

Thus, by
3.12
,
\epsilon
is then bounded by
\sqrt{\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}}
, where
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}
is the
classical
error probability (
Definition

3.10
) in the corresponding classical sampling game, defined as follows:

•

Let
\mathbf{R},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\in\{0,1\}^{2\times n}
s.t.
\mathbf{R}
is the matrix on which we are running the sampling and
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
is an arbitrary matrix.

•

Sample two independent and uniform subsets of
[n]
each of size
k
.
Let
s
denote the size of their intersection.
Fix
s
, and discard the subsets themselves.
Sample a random subset
T^{\prime}
of
[n]
, of size
2k-s
.

•

Sample subset
S^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime}
as follows:

–

Sample
S
as a random subset of
T^{\prime}
of size
s
.

–

Sample bits
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and set
S^{\prime}=\{j\,|\,j\in S\,\wedge\,\mathbf{b}_{j}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}\}
.

•

Output
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}})
.

We provide an analysis of this classical sampling game in
Section

D.3
.
Using
Lemma

D.4
from the same appendix, we get that for
0<\epsilon,\beta,\delta<1
and
0<\gamma<\delta
,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(\frac{(n-k)^{2}-3\epsilon k^{2}}{(n-k)^{2}+(1-2\epsilon)k^{2}}\right)^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\epsilon)\frac{k^{2}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\gamma)^{2}(1-\beta)(1-\epsilon)\frac{k^{2}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}(1-\epsilon)k^{2}}{2n}\right)+2\exp\left(-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}k^{3}}{n^{2}}\right)

Setting
\delta=11/200,\epsilon=0.03917,\beta=0.04213,\gamma=0.02456,k=A\lambda,n=(A+B)\lambda,A=11\ 700,B=30\ 400
, we get each of the exp terms above is
\leq\frac{1}{2^{4\lambda}}
. Thus,
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}\leq\frac{7}{2^{4\lambda}}
, giving us,
\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}\leq\sqrt{\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{7}}{2^{2\lambda}}
.

This gives using
Eq.

5
that:

\Tr(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{11/200} \tau)\leq(2q+1)^{2}\left[\frac{\sqrt{7}}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{8q}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}\right]

For
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
and
q\geq 5
,

\Tr(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{11/200} \tau)\leq(2q+1)^{2}\left[\frac{\sqrt{7}}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{8q}{2^{2\lambda}}\right]\leq\frac{(2q+1)^{2}(8q+\sqrt{7})}{2^{2\lambda}}\leq\frac{5q^{2}\cdot 9q}{2^{2\lambda}}\leq\frac{45q^{3}}{2^{2\lambda}}

∎

Thus, by gentle measurement (
Lemma

3.1
), the
\tau
defined in
7.11
is within trace distance
\frac{3\sqrt{10}q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}
of a state
\tau_{\mathsf{good}}
in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{11/200}
.The following sub-claim completes the proof of
7.10
.

SubClaim 7.12
.

If
h:\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{\leq A\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
is a universal family of hash functions, then conditioned on
\tau
(defined in
6.5
) being the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{11/200}
, and
A=11\ 700,B=30\ 400
, it holds that

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]|\leq\frac{1}{2^{5\lambda}}

where
h_{b}
is the binary entropy function.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{\delta}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\mathbf{b},\\
U\subseteq[n],|U|=d\end{subarray}}\ket{\bc}\bra{\bc}_{{\cal C}}\otimes\ket{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}\bra{\widetilde{\bR},\widetilde{\btheta}}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\otimes\ket{U,\bb}\bra{U,\bb}_{{\cal Z}_{2}}\otimes\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R}\,:\,\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}}\neq\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}}\text{ or }\Delta\left(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}\setminus U},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T}\setminus U}\right)<\delta\\
\text{where }T=H_{FS}(\mathbf{c}),\\
S^{\prime}=\{j\,|\,j\in T\cap U\,\wedge\,\mathbf{b}_{j}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}\}\end{subarray}}\ket{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}\bra{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}_{{\cal S}}\right)

(7)

Since
\tau
is in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{11/200}
, by definition the state on register
{\cal S}
is in a superposition of states as in the summation above. However, note that if
T\neq H_{FS}(\mathbf{c})
or if
\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}}\neq\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}}
(where
S^{\prime}=\{j\,|\,j\in T\cap U\,\wedge\,\mathbf{b}_{j}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}\}
), then the sender side check will fail and the two hybrids are perfectly indistinguishable. So, it suffices to analyze states
\tau
where the register containing
T
equals
H_{FS}(\mathbf{c})
and where
\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}}
.
Thus, conditioned on the sender not aborting,
the above equation implies that the register
{\cal S}
is in superposition of states
\ket{\bR_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}
s.t.
\mathbf{R}_{S^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{S^{\prime}},\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}\setminus U},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T}\setminus U})<11/200
, for
S^{\prime}
as defined above.

Recall that
\tau
is the state of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
(equivalently also
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
) immediately after
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message (which includes
\mathbf{c}
),
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
is run to get
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, and sender samples the set
U\subseteq[n]
of size
d
and bits
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}

next the sender measures register
{\cal S}
.
Since measurements on different subsystems commute, we may assume that the sender measures the registers
{\cal S}_{{T}\cup U}
first (recall that we are trying to argue that the remaining registers have entropy).
Then, by the argument in the previous paragraph, this leaves the remaining registers
{\cal S}_{\overline{T}\setminus{U}}
in a superposition of states
\ket{\left(\bR_{\overline{T}\setminus U}\right)_{\widetilde{\btheta}}}
for
\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}\setminus U}
s.t.
\Delta(\mathbf{R}_{\overline{T}\setminus U},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{\overline{T}\setminus U})<\frac{11}{200}
.

Next, to obtain
ct_{c}
for
c\in\{0,1\}
, the sender measures registers
{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus c}
in basis
d_{i}\oplus c
to obtain a string
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{c}\in\{0,1\}^{|\overline{T}\setminus U|}
. Then,
ct_{c}
is set as
m_{c}\oplus h(s,\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{c})
, where
s
is uniformly sampled seed for a universal hash function
h
.
Recall, in addition, in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
the sender defines
b
as
\mathsf{maj}\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
. We now prove a lower bound on the quantum min-entropy of
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{b\oplus 1}
, which by the Leftover Hash lemma (
3.8
) would imply our claim.

Consider the subset
W\subseteq\{0,1\}\times\overline{T}\setminus U
defined as
W=\{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
. Consider again by the commuting property of measurements on different systems that registers
{\cal S}_{\overline{T}\setminus(U\cup W)}=\{{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
are measured first, leaving the registers
{\cal S}_{W}=\{{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
in a superposition of states
\ket{\br_{\widetilde{\btheta}_{W[1]}}}
, where
\Delta\left(\mathbf{r},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{W}\right)<11/200
, and where
W[1]
denotes the projection of
W
on the second set, i.e.
W[1]=\{d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
. Hence, since
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{b\oplus 1}
is obtained by measuring registers
{\cal S}_{W}=\{{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
in basis
d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1
, majority of the bits of
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{b\oplus 1}
are obtained by measuring
{\cal S}_{W}
in basis
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1
(since
b
was defined as
\mathsf{maj}\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus d_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
, this means in the majority of the places in
\overline{T}\setminus U
, the following holds:
b=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus d_{i}\iff d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1
).

Therefore, registers
{\cal S}_{W}
are in a superposition of states
\ket{\br_{\widetilde{\btheta}_{W[1]}}}
, where
\Delta\left(\mathbf{r},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{W}\right)<11/200
. Recall that
{\cal S}_{W}=\{{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
, then the paragraph above implies that for a majority of
i\in\overline{T}\setminus U
, register
{\cal S}_{i,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1}
is measured in basis
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1
. Using
3.9
, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{b\oplus 1}\,|\,{\cal C},{\cal Z}_{1},{\cal Z}_{2},{\cal X})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|\overline{T}\setminus U|}{2}-h_{b}\left(\frac{11}{200}\right)|\overline{T}\setminus U|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n-2k}{2}-h_{b}\left(\frac{11}{200}\right)(n-k)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n-2k}{2}-0.3073(n-k)

For
n=(A+B)\lambda,k=A\lambda,A=11\ 700,B=30\ 400
, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{b\oplus 1}\,|\,{\cal C},{\cal Z}_{1},{\cal Z}_{2},{\cal X})

\displaystyle\geq 9\lambda.

where
h_{b}
is the binary entropy function, and we bound the number of strings of length
n
with relative hamming weight at most
\delta
by
h_{b}(\delta)n
. Hence, using the leftover hash lemma (
3.8
),
(s,h(s,\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{b\oplus 1}))
is
\frac{1}{2^{5\lambda}}
.
∎

This completes the proof of the claim, as desired.
∎

Appendix A
Security of the seedless extractors

In this section, we show the security of the XOR and ROM extractors.

A.1
XOR extractor

Theorem A.1
.

Let
{\cal X}
be an
n
-qubit register, and consider any state
\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
that can be written as
