\ket{\gamma}=\sum_{u:{\cal H}{\cal W}(u)<n/2}\ket{\psi_u}_{{\cal A}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\cal X}.

Let
\rho_{{\cal A},{\cal P}}
be the mixed state that results from measuring
{\cal X}
in the Hadamard basis to produce
x
, and writing
\bigoplus_{i\in[n]}x_{i}
into the single qubit register
{\cal P}
. Then it holds that

\rho_{{\cal A},{\cal P}}=\Tr_{\cal X}(\ket{\gamma}\bra{\gamma})\otimes\left(\frac{1}{2}\ket{0}\bra{0}+\frac{1}{2}\ket{1}\bra{1}\right).

Proof.

First, write the state on
({\cal A},{\cal X},{\cal P})
that results from applying Hadamard to
{\cal X}
and writing the parity, denoted by
p(x)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in[n]}x_{i}
, to
{\cal P}
:

\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left(\sum_{u:{\cal H}{\cal W}(u)<n/2}(-1)^{u\cdot x}\ket{\psi_u}\right)\ket{x}\ket{p(x)}.

Then we have that

\displaystyle\rho_{{\cal A},{\cal P}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{x:p(x)=0}\left(\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}}(-1)^{(u_{1}\oplus u_{2})\cdot x}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{x:p(x)=1}\left(\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}}(-1)^{(u_{1}\oplus u_{2})\cdot x}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{1}\bra{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\otimes\left(\sum_{x:p(x)=0}(-1)^{(u_{1}\oplus u_{2})\cdot x}\ket{0}\bra{0}+\sum_{x:p(x)=1}(-1)^{(u_{1}\oplus u_{2})\cdot x}\ket{1}\bra{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}}2^{n/2}\delta_{u_{1}=u_{2}}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\otimes\left(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u:{\cal H}{\cal W}<n/2}\ket{\psi_u}\bra{\psi_u}\otimes\left(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr_{\cal X}(\ket{\gamma}\bra{\gamma})\otimes\left(\frac{1}{2}\ket{0}\bra{0}+\frac{1}{2}\ket{1}\bra{1}\right),

where the 3rd equality is due to the following claim, plus the observation that
u_{1}\oplus u_{2}\neq 1^{n}
for any
u_{1},u_{2}
such that
{\cal H}{\cal W}(u_{1}),{\cal H}{\cal W}(u_{2})<n/2
.

Claim A.2
.

For any
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
u\notin\{0^{n},1^{n}\}
, it holds that

\sum_{x:p(x)=0}(-1)^{u\cdot x}=\sum_{x:p(x)=1}(-1)^{u\cdot x}=0.

Proof.

For any such
u\notin\{0^{n},1^{n}\}
, define
S_{0}=\{i:u_{i}=0\}
and
S_{1}=\{i:u_{i}=1\}
. Then, for any
y_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{|S_{0}|}
and
y_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{|S_{1}|}
, define
x_{y_{0},y_{1}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to be the
n
-bit string that is equal to
y_{0}
when restricted to indices in
S_{0}
and equal to
y_{1}
when restricted to indices in
S_{1}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{x:p(x)=0}(-1)^{u\cdot x}=\sum_{y_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{|S_{1}|}}\sum_{y_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{|S_{0}|}:p(x_{y_{0},y_{1}})=0}(-1)^{u\cdot x_{y_{0},y_{1}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{|S_{1}|}}2^{|S_{0}|-1}(-1)^{1^{|S_{1}|}\cdot y_{1}}=2^{|S_{0}|-1}\sum_{y_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{|S_{1}|}}(-1)^{p(y_{1})}=0,

and the same sequence of equalities can be seen to hold for
x:p(x)=1
.
∎

∎

A.2
RO extractor

Theorem A.3
.

Let
H:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
be a uniformly random function, and let
q,C,k
be integers. Consider a two-stage oracle algorithm
(A_{1}^{H},A_{2}^{H})
that combined makes at most
q
queries to
H
. Suppose that
A_{1}^{H}
outputs classical strings
(T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T})
, and let
\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
be its left-over quantum state,
16
16

16

That is, consider sampling
H
, running a purified
A_{1}^{H}
, measuring at the end to obtain
(T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T})
, and then defining
\ket{\gamma}
to be the left-over state on
{\cal A}
’s remaining registers.
where
T\subset[n]
is a set of size
n-k
, each
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
,
{\cal A}
is a register of arbitary size, and
{\cal X}
is a register of
k
qubits. Suppose further that with probability 1 over the sampling of
H
and the execution of
A_{1}
, there exists a set
L\subset\{0,1\}^{k}
of size at most
C
such that
\ket{\gamma}
may be written as follows:

\ket{\gamma}=\sum_{u\in L}\ket{\psi_u}_{\cal A}\otimes\ket{u}_{\cal X}.

Now consider the following two games.

•

\mathsf{REAL}
:

–

A_{1}^{H}
outputs
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
.

–

{\cal X}
is measured in the Hadamard basis to produce a
k
-bit string which is parsed as
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
, and a left-over state
\ket{\gamma'}_{{\cal A}}
on register
{\cal A}
. Define
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

–

A_{2}^{H}
is given
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma'}_{{\cal A}},H(x)
, and outputs a bit.

•

\mathsf{IDEAL}
:

–

A_{1}^{H}
outputs
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
.

–

r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{m}
.

–

A_{2}^{H}
is given
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\Tr_{\cal X}(\ket{\gamma}\bra{\gamma}),r
, and outputs a bit.

Then,

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{REAL}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IDEAL}=1]\right|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{q}C+2q\sqrt{C}}{2^{k/2}}<\frac{4qC}{2^{k/2}}.

Proof.

The proof follows via two steps. First, we define a HYBRID distribution where we re-program the random oracle at input
x
to a uniformly random string
r
, and argue that the adversary cannot notice, even given
x
. Intuitively, this is establishing that
H(x)
must have been quite close to uniformly random from the adversary’s perspective at the point that
x
is measured (on average over
x
). This requires a new “adaptive re-programming” lemma for the QROM, where the point
x
that is adaptively re-programmed may be sampled from a
quantum
source of entropy. As mentioned in the introduction, all previous adaptive re-programming lemmas have only handled classical entropy sources. Second, we “undo” the re-programming of
H(x)
, but still output (uniformly random)
r
as the extracted string. Indistinguishability of these two games, on the other hand, can be established via a one-way-to-hiding lemma, since in the final game, the adversary is given no information at all about the measured string
x
. In particular, it suffices to use the fact that
x
has high quantum min-entropy conditioned on the adversary’s state to argue that the adversary cannot guess
x
and thus cannot notice whether or not
H(x)
was re-programmed.

Now we formalize this strategy. Consider the following hybrid game.

•

\mathsf{HYBRID}
:

–

A_{1}^{H}
outputs
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
.

–

{\cal X}
is measured in the Hadamard basis to produce a
k
-bit string which is parsed as
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
, and a left-over state
\ket{\gamma'}_{{\cal A}}
on register
{\cal A}
. Define
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
. Sample
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{m}
, and re-program
H(x)
to
r
.

–

A_{2}^{H}
is given
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma'}_{{\cal A}},r
, and outputs a bit.

The theorem follows by combining the two following claims.

Claim A.4
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{REAL}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{HYBRID}=1]|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{q}C}{2^{k/2}}.

Proof.

Consider purifying the random oracle
H
on register
{\cal R}
, and let
\ket{\widehat{\gamma}}_{{\cal R},{\cal A},{\cal X}}
be the left-over state of
A_{1}
and the random oracle in
\mathsf{REAL}
or
\mathsf{HYBRID}
after
A_{1}
outputs
(T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T})
. By
A.6
, the state
\ket{\widehat{\gamma}}_{{\cal R},{\cal A},{\cal X}}
satisfies the premise of
Lemma

A.7
below, where
{\cal F}
is the set of
2^{k}
sub-registers of
{\cal R}
corresponding to each
x^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}
for all
i\in T
.

Now, consider a reduction that receives the state
\rho^{\mathsf{REAL}}
or
\rho^{\mathsf{REPROG}}
from
Lemma

A.7
, measures
{\cal F}_{x}
in the computational basis to obtain
H(x)
, and then continues to run
A_{2}
on input
H(x)
(along with
A_{1}
’s state on
{\cal A}
and
T
,
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T}
). In the case of
\rho^{\mathsf{REAL}}
, this exactly matches the
\mathsf{REAL}
game and in the case of
\rho^{\mathsf{REPROG}}
, this exactly matches the
\mathsf{HYBRID}
game, using the fact that
\ket{\phi_0}
(as defined in
A.6
) is the uniform superposition state.

∎

Claim A.5
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{HYBRID}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{IDEAL}=1]|\leq\frac{2q\sqrt{C}}{2^{k/2}}.

Proof.

This follows from an invocation of
3.4
. Consider the distribution over
(S,O_{1},O_{2},\ket{\psi})
that results from the following.

•

Sample
O_{1}
as a random oracle
H
,

•

run
A_{1}^{H}
to obtain
T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal A},{\cal X}}
,

•

measure to obtain
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
as in the
\mathsf{HYBRID}
game, define
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, and define
S=\{x\}
,

•

sample
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{m}
, and let
O_{2}
be the same as
O_{1}
, except that
O_{2}(x)=r
,

•

let
\ket{\psi}
be the resulting state on register
{\cal A}
along with the classical information
(T,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in T},r)
.

Then,
\Pr[\mathsf{IDEAL}=1]=P_{\text{left}}
and
\Pr[\mathsf{HYBRID}=1]=P_{\text{right}}
, so it suffices to bound
P_{\text{guess}}
. By
3.7
,
P_{\text{guess}}
is upper bounded by
1/2^{\ell}
, where
\ell
is the quantum conditional min-entropy of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
given register
{\cal A}
. By
3.9
, and the fact that measuring an unentangled
k
-bit standard basis vector in the Hadamard basis gives
k
bits of quantum conditional min-entropy,
P_{\text{guess}}
is upper bounded by
\frac{C}{2^{k}}
. Thus,
3.4
gives the final bound of
\frac{2q\sqrt{C}}{2^{k/2}}
.

∎

∎

A.3
The superposition oracle

Following
[
Zha19
,
GHHM21
]
, we will use the fact that a quantum accesible random oracle
H:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
can be implemented as follows.

•

Let
{\cal F}
be a
(m\cdot 2^{n})
-qubit register split into
2^{n}
subregisters
\{{\cal F}_{x}\}_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}
of size
m
. Let
\ket{\phi_0}
be the uniform superposition state. Prepare an initial state

\ket{\Psi}_{\cal F}=\bigotimes_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{\phi_0}_{{{\cal F}_{x}}}.

•

A query on registers
{\cal X},{\cal Y}
is answered with a unitary
O_{{\cal X},{\cal Y},{\cal F}}
such that

O_{{\cal X},{\cal Y},{\cal F}}\ket{x}\bra{x}_{\cal X}=\ket{x}\bra{x}_{\cal X}\otimes(\mathsf{CNOT}^{\otimes m})_{{\cal F}_{x}:{\cal Y}}.

•

Register
{\cal F}
is measured to obtain a random function
H
.

Imported Theorem A.6

(
[
AMRS20
,
GHHM21
]
)
.

Let
\ket{\psi_q}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}}
be the joint adversary-oracle state state after an adversary has made
q
queries to the superposition oracle on register
{\cal F}
. Then this state can be written as

\ket{\psi_q}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}}=\sum_{S\subset\{0,1\}^{n},|S|\leq q}\ket{\psi_{q,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{\otimes(2^{n}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}},

where
\ket{\psi_{q,S}}
are such that
\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\ket{\psi_{q,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}=0
for all
x\in S
.

A.4
Re-programming

In this section, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\ket{\phi_0}
be an
m
-qubit unit vector, and let
{\cal F}
be a
(m\cdot 2^{k})
-qubit register split into
2^{k}
sub-registers
\{{\cal F}_{x}\}_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}
of
m
qubits. Let
{\cal A}
be an arbitrary register and
{\cal X}
be an
k
-qubit register. Consider any state
\ket{\gamma}_{{\cal F},{\cal A},{\cal X}}
, set
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{k}
, and integer
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
\ket{\gamma}
can be written as

\ket{\gamma}=\sum_{u\in L}\ket{\widehat{\psi}_u}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\cal X},

where each

\frac{\ket{\widehat{\psi}_u}}{\|\ket{\widehat{\psi}_u}\|}=\sum_{S\subset\{0,1\}^{n}:|S|\leq q}\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}},

and each
\ket{\psi_{u,S}}
is orthogonal to
\ket{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}
for all
x\in S
. Let

•

\rho^{\mathsf{REAL}}_{{\cal F},{\cal A},{\cal X}}
be the mixed state that results from measuring
{\cal X}
in the Hadamard basis to produce
x\in\{0,1\}^{k}
and a left-over state
\ket{\gamma_x}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}
, and outputting
\ket{\gamma_x}\bra{\gamma_x}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}
, and

•

\rho^{\mathsf{REPROG}}_{{\cal F},{\cal A},{\cal X}}
be the mixed state that results from measuring
{\cal X}
in the Hadamard basis to produce
x\in\{0,1\}^{k}
and a left-over state
\ket{\gamma_x}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}
, and outputting
\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\ket{\gamma_x}\bra{\gamma_x}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}
.

Then,

\mathsf{TD}\left(\rho^{\mathsf{REAL}},\rho^{\mathsf{REPROG}}\right)\leq\frac{2\sqrt{q}|L|}{2^{k/2}}.

Proof.

For each
u\in L
, let
a_{u}\coloneqq\|\ket{\widehat{\psi}_u}\|
, and
\ket{\psi_u}\coloneqq\ket{\widehat{\psi}_u}/a_{u}
. Consider applying the Hadamard transform to register
{\cal X}
of
\ket{\gamma}
, producing

\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k},u\in L}\frac{(-1)^{u\cdot x}a_{u}}{2^{k/2}}\ket{\psi_u}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}\otimes\ket{x}_{{\cal X}}\coloneqq\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\ket{\gamma_x}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}\otimes\ket{x}_{{\cal X}}

and then measuring in the computational basis to produce
x
and left-over state
\ket{\gamma_x}\bra{\gamma_x}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}.

The lemma asks to bound the following quantity.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\ket{\gamma_x}\bra{\gamma_x}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}-\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\ket{\gamma_x}\bra{\gamma_x}_{{\cal F},{\cal A}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}\Bigg\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Bigg\|\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}(-1)^{(u_{1}\oplus u_{2})\cdot x}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}(-1)^{(u_{1}\oplus u_{2})\cdot x}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}\Bigg\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\left\|\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\left(\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}\right)-\left(\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\otimes\ket{x}\bra{x}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left\|\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}-\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\right\|_{1},

where the inequalities follow from the triangle inequality. Now, following the proof of
[
GHHM21
, Theorem 6]
, for any
u_{1},u_{2},
and
x
, we can write

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}=

\displaystyle\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\ket{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}+\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})

\displaystyle+({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}

and

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}=

\displaystyle\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\ket{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}

\displaystyle+\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}

so

\displaystyle\left\|\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}-\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\right\|_{1}\leq\|\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\|_{1}

\displaystyle+\|({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\|_{1}+\|\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\|_{1}.

Now, for each
x,u
, define
\alpha_{x,u}=\|\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\ket{\psi_{u}}\|
. The first term above simplifies as

\displaystyle\|\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\|_{1}=\|\bra{\psi_{u_2}}({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\|=\sqrt{1-\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}\ket{\psi_{u_2}}}=\sqrt{1-\alpha_{x,u_{2},}^{2}}.

The second term above simplifies as

\displaystyle\|({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\|_{1}\leq\|({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\|=\sqrt{1-\bra{\psi_{u_1}}\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}\ket{\psi_{u_1}}}=\sqrt{1-\alpha_{x,u_{1}}^{2}},

where the inequality is Holder’s inequality. The third term simplifies as

\displaystyle\left\|\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\otimes\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\right\|_{1}=\left\|\Tr_{{\cal F}_{x}}\left(({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|({\mathbb{I}}-\ket{\phi_0}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}})\ket{\psi_{u_1}}\bra{\psi_{u_2}}\right\|_{1}=\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{1}}},

where the inequality is the following fact from
[
Hol19
]
: for any bounded operator
T
on
{\cal A}\otimes{\cal B}
,
\|\Tr_{\cal B}(T)\|_{1}\leq\|T\|_{1}
.

Thus, the distinguishing advantage can be bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}2\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{1}}}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\left(\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{1}}}+\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\left(\sqrt{2^{k}\left(2^{k}-\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{1}}\right)}+\sqrt{2^{k}\left(2^{k}-\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\alpha^{2}_{x,u_{2}}\right)}\right),

where the second inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwartz.

Now, for any
u
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\alpha^{2}_{x,u}=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\|\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\ket{\psi_{u}}\|^{2}\|

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\bigg\|\sum_{S\subset\{0,1\}^{k}:|S|\leq q}\bra{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}}\bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\bigg\|\sum_{S\not\owns x}\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}}\bigg\|^{2},

where the last equality follows since
\ket{\phi_0}_{{\cal F}_{x}}
is orthogonal to
\ket{\psi_{u,S}}
for all
x\in S
, and
\ket{\phi_0}
is normalized. Now, since each of the summands in the inner summation are pairwise orthogonal (so that we can move the summation outside of the norm), we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\bigg\|\sum_{S\not\owns x}\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}}\bigg\|^{2}=\sum_{S}\sum_{x\notin S}\bigg\|\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}}\bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(2^{k}-q)\sum_{S}\bigg\|\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}}\bigg\|^{2}=(2^{k}-q)\bigg\|\sum_{S}\ket{\psi_{u,S}}_{{\cal A},{\cal F}_{S}}\otimes\left(\ket{\phi_0}^{(2^{k}-|S|)}\right)_{{\cal F}_{\overline{S}}}\bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(2^{k}-q)\|\ket{\psi_{u}}\|^{2}=2^{k}-q.

Thus, the distinguishing advantage can be bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{2}{2^{k}}\sqrt{2^{k}\cdot q}\sum_{u_{1},u_{2}\in L}a_{u_{1}}a_{u_{2}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{q}|L|}{2^{k/2}},

since, by Cauchy-Schwartz and the fact that
\sum_{u\in L}a_{u}^{2}=1
, we can bound
\sum_{u\in L}a_{u}\leq\sqrt{|L|}
.

∎

Appendix B
The random basis framework

In this section, we obtain three round OT realizing
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}}
, and we provide a modification that yields four round chosen input
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
. The constructions make use of standard BB84 states, therefore we refer to this as the random basis framework.

Theorem B.1

(Three round random-sender-input OT.)
.

Instantiate Protocol
9
with any non-interactive commitment scheme that is
extractable
(
Definition

5.3
) and
equivocal
(
Definition

5.4
). Then the following hold.

•

When instantiated with the XOR extractor, there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
9
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
.

•

When instantiated with the ROM extractor, there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
9
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
.

Furthermore, letting
\lambda
be the security parameter,
q
be an upper bound on the total number of random oracle queries made by the adversary, and using the commitment scheme from
Section

5.2
with security parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=2\lambda
, the following hold.

•

When instantiatied with the XOR extractor and constants
A=1100
,
B=500
, Protocol
9
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\frac{\sqrt{5}+1}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{148(q+3200\lambda+1)^{3}+1}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{25600q\lambda}{2^{\lambda}},\quad\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\frac{114q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{\lambda}}.

This requires a total of
(A+B)\lambda=1600\lambda
BB84 states.

•

When instantiated with the ROM extractor and constants
A=11\ 000,B=12\ 000
, Protocol
9
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{4q}{2^{18\lambda}}+\frac{148(q+46000\lambda+1)^{3}+1}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{368000q\lambda}{2^{\lambda}},\quad\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\frac{430q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{\lambda}}.

This requires a total of
(A+B)\lambda=23\ 000\lambda
BB84 states.

Theorem B.2

(Four Round chosen input string OT.)
.

Instantiate Protocol
10
with any non-interactive commitment scheme that is
extractable
(
Definition

5.3
) and
equivocal
(
Definition

5.4
). Then there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
10
securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
.

Furthermore, letting
\lambda
be the security parameter,
q
be an upper bound on the total number of random oracle queries made by the adversary, and using the commitment scheme from
Section

5.2
with security parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=2\lambda
, for constants
A=5300,B=5000
, Protocol
10
securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{2^{9\lambda}}+\frac{148(q+2n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{16qn}{2^{\lambda}},\quad\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\frac{288q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{\lambda}}.

This requires a total of
(A+B)\lambda=10\ 300\lambda
BB84 states.

Protocol
9

Ingredients, parameters and notation.

•

Security parameter
\lambda
and constants
A,B
. Let
n=(A+B)\lambda
and
k=A\lambda
.

•

For classical bits
(x,\theta)
, let
\ket{x}_{\theta}
denote
\ket{x}
if
\theta=0
, and
(\ket{0}+(-1)^{x}\ket{1})/\sqrt{2}
if
\theta=1
.

•

A non-interactive extractable and equivocal commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec})
, where commitments to 2 bits have size
\ell\coloneqq\ell(\lambda)
.

•

An extractor
E
with domain
\{0,1\}^{n-k}
which is either

–

The XOR function, so
E(r_{1},\dots,r_{n-k})=\bigoplus_{i\in[n-k]}r_{i}
.

–

A random oracle
H_{Ext}:\{0,1\}^{n-k}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

Receiver input
:
b\in\{0,1\},m\in\{0,1\}^{z}
, where
z
is the output length of the extractor.

1.

Receiver message.

\mathsf{R}
performs the following steps.

(a)

Choose
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\theta\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and prepare the states
\{\ket{x_i}_{\theta_{i}}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

(b)

Compute
\left(\mathsf{st},\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}\left(\{(x_{i},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\right)
, and send
\{\ket{x_i}_{\theta_{i}},c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
\mathsf{S}
.

2.

Sender message.

\mathsf{S}
performs the following steps.

(a)

Choose
\widehat{\theta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
. For all
i\in[n]
, measure
\ket{x_i}_{\theta_{i}}
in basis
\widehat{\theta_{i}}
to get outcome
\widehat{x_{i}}
.

(b)

Sample a random subset
T
of
[n]
of size
k
.
Send
T,\{\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
to
\mathsf{R}
, where
\overline{T}\coloneqq[n]\setminus T
.

3.

Receiver message.

\mathsf{R}
performs the following steps.

(a)

Divide
\overline{T}
into 2 disjoint subsets
S_{0},S_{1}
as follows. Set
I_{b}=S_{0}
and
I_{1-b}=S_{1}
, where
{S_{0}=\{i\ |\ i\in\overline{T}\wedge\theta_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}},{S_{1}=\{i\ |\ i\in\overline{T}\wedge\theta_{i}\neq\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}}
.

(b)

Compute
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{st},T)
.

(c)

Compute
X
as the concatenation of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I_{b}}
, set
r_{b}:=E(X)\oplus m
, and sample
r_{1-b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{z}
, where
z
is the output length of extractor.

(d)

Send
I_{0},I_{1},\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T},(r_{0},r_{1})
to
\mathsf{S}
.

4.

Output computation

\mathsf{S}
does the following:

(a)

Abort if
\mathsf{Rec}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T})=\bot
or if
\exists i\in T
s.t.
\theta_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{i}
but
x_{i}\neq\widehat{x}_{i}
.

(b)

Compute
\widehat{X}_{0},\widehat{X}_{1}
as the concatenation of
\{\widehat{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{0}},\{\widehat{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{1}}
respectively. Output
m_{0}:=E(\widehat{X}_{0})\oplus r_{0}
and
m_{1}:=E(\widehat{X}_{1})\oplus r_{1}
.

Three-round OT protocol realizing
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
.

Protocol
10

Ingredients, parameters and notation.

•

Security parameter
\lambda
and constants
A,B
. Let
n=(A+B)\lambda
and
k=A\lambda
.

•

For classical bits
(x,\theta)
, let
\ket{x}_{\theta}
denote
\ket{x}
if

, and

if

.

(\mathsf{Com},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec})
, where commitments to 2 bits have size

.

•

A universal hash function
h:\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\times\{0,1\}^{\leq B\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

:

,
Receiver input

.

Choose

Sample a random subset

of

of size

. Send

to
\mathsf{R}
, where

.

performs the following steps.

(a)

Divide
\overline{T}
into 2 disjoint subsets

as follows. Set
I_{b}=S_{0}
and
I_{1-b}=S_{1}
, where
{S_{0}=\{i\ |\ i\in\overline{T}\wedge\theta_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}},{S_{1}=\{i\ |\ i\in\overline{T}\wedge\theta_{i}\neq\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}}
.

(b)

Compute
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{st},T)
.

(c)

Compute
X
as the concatenation of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I_{b}}
, set

, and

sample

, where

is the output length of extractor.

Send
I_{0},I_{1},\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
,

to

.

Sender message.

\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
do the following:

•

does the following:

–

Abort if

or if
\exists i\in T
s.t.
\theta_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{i}
but
x_{i}\neq\widehat{x}_{i}
.

\widehat{X}_{0},\widehat{X}_{1}
as the concatenation of

respectively.

–

, send

to

.

•

computes

as the concatenation of

.

Figure 10:
Four-round OT protocol realizing
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
. Parts in
blue
are different than the 3-round OT protocol realizing
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}
in Protocol
9
.

B.1
Three-round random-input OT

In this section, we prove
Theorem

B.1
.

Sender Security

Let
\mathsf{SimExt}=(\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext})
be the simulator for the extractable commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{{H_{C}}},\mathsf{Open}^{{H_{C}}},\mathsf{Rec}^{{H_{C}}})
(according to
Definition

5.3
). Let
\epsilon=0.053
(for XOR extractor) or
0.010748
(for ROM extractor) be a constant. We describe the simulator
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
against a malicious receiver
\mathsf{R}^{*}
.

\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
:

•

Initialize
\mathsf{R}^{*}
and answer its oracle queries to
{H_{C}}
using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
and in case of ROM extractor queries to
H_{Ext}
using the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator (
3.6
). Wait to receive
n
qubits and commitments
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from
\mathsf{R}^{*}
.

•

\{({x}^{*}_{i},{\theta}^{*}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
.

•

Choose
\widehat{\theta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and measure all the received qubits in bases
\widehat{\theta}
to get measurement outcomes
\widehat{x}
. Also, sample a random subset
T\leftarrow[n]
s.t.
|T|=k
and send
T,\{\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
to
\mathsf{R}^{*}
, where
\overline{T}\coloneqq[n]\setminus T
.

•

Wait to receive sets
I_{0},I_{1}
, where
I_{0}\subseteq\overline{T}
,
I_{1}=\overline{T}\setminus I_{0}
and openings
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
.

•

Check if
\mathsf{Rec}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in T},\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T})=\bot
or if there exists
i\in T
s.t.
\widehat{\theta}_{i}={\theta}^{*}_{i}
but
\widehat{x}_{i}\neq{x}^{*}_{i}
. If any of the checks fail, send
\mathsf{abort}
to the ideal functionality, output
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s state and continue answering distinguisher’s queries.

•

Compute the set
Q=\{i\ |\ i\in I_{0}\wedge\theta_{i}^{*}\neq\widehat{\theta}_{i}\}
. If
|Q|\geq\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{4}
, then set
b=1
, else set
b=0
. Compute
m_{b}=\{\widehat{x}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{b}}
, send
(b,m_{b})
to the ideal functionality and output
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s state.

•

Continue answering distinguisher’s queries to
{H_{C}}
(and
H_{Ext}
) using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
(and the efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator).

Consider a distinguisher
(\mathsf{D},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
such that
\mathsf{R}^{*},\mathsf{D}
make a total of
q
queries combined to
{H_{C}}
(and
H_{Ext}
). Consider the following sequence of hybrids:

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: This is the real world interaction between
\mathsf{R}^{*},\mathsf{S}
. Using the notation of
Definition

3.2
, this is a distribution over
\{0,1\}
denoted by
\Pi[\mathsf{R}^{*},\mathsf{D},\top]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: This is identical to the previous hybrid, except that queries to
{H_{C}}
are answered using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
, and
\{({x}^{*}_{i},{\theta}^{*}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}\leftarrow\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
is run after
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its first message. After
\mathsf{R}^{*}
sends its openings in the third round, the sender performs the following checks: check if
\mathsf{Rec}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in T},\allowbreak\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T})=\bot
or if there exists
i\in T
s.t.
\theta^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{i}
but
x^{*}_{i}\neq\widehat{x}_{i}
(note that it uses
x_{i}^{*},\theta_{i}^{*}
for its second check, rather than
{x}_{i},{\theta}_{i}
as in the honest sender strategy). It then continues with the rest of the protocol as in the honest sender strategy.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: This is the result of
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
interacting in
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[1]}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}],\mathsf{D},\top]
(or
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}[\lambda]}[\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}],\mathsf{D},\top]
).

Claim B.3
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]|\leq\frac{148(q+2n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{16qn}{2^{\lambda}}
.

Proof.

This follows by a direct reduction to extractability of the commitment scheme (
Definition

5.3
). Indeed, let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit}}
be the machine that runs
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
until
\mathsf{R}^{*}
outputs its message, which includes
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open}}
be the machine that takes as input the rest of the state of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, and runs it till the third round to get set
T
and openings
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
, and outputs
T
and these openings. Let
\mathsf{D}
be the machine that runs the rest of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and outputs a bit.

Then, plugging in
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=2\lambda
,
Definition

5.3
when applied to
(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Commit}},\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{Open}},\mathsf{D})
implies that the hybrids cannot be distinguished except with probability

\frac{148(q+2n+1)^{3}+1}{2^{2\lambda}}+\frac{16qn}{2^{\lambda}},

since we are committing to a total of
2n
bits.
∎

Claim B.4
.

When instantiated with the XOR extractor and constants
A=1100,B=500
, we have,

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}+1}{2^{\lambda}}.

And when instantiated with the ROM extractor and constants
A=11\ 000,B=12\ 000
, we have,

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{4q}{2^{18\lambda}}.

Proof.

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, while both
R_{0},R_{1}
were set according to the honest sender strategy, in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, for
b
as defined by the simulator,
R_{1-b}
(output by the honest sender) is set as a uniformly random string. In the following, we show that in either hybrid
R_{1-b}
is statistically close to a uniformly random string given
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s view which would imply this claim. We setup some notation before proceeding:

•

Let
{\cal X}=\{{\cal X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
denote the
n
registers, each holding a single qubit, sent by
\mathsf{R}^{*}
in its first round.

•

For a vector
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots x_{n})
, and a set
S\subseteq[n]
, let
\mathbf{x}_{S}
denotes the values of
\mathbf{x}
indexed by
S
.

•

For vectors
\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
\ket{\bx_{\btheta}}
denote the state on
n
single-qubit registers, where register
i
contains the state
\ket{\bx_i}
prepared in the
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}
basis.

•

For a set
T\subseteq[n]
, let
\overline{T}\coloneqq[n]\setminus T
.

•

Using notation as defined in
Section

3
, for a subset
S\subseteq[n]
and two vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{y}_{S})
denotes the fraction of values
\mathbf{x}_{i},i\in S
s.t.
\mathbf{x}_{i}\neq\mathbf{y}_{i}
.

Consider the following quantum sampling game (defined as in
Section

3.6
):

•

Fix some state on register
{\cal X}
and some strings
\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

Sample
T\subseteq[n]
as a uniform random subset of size
k
.

•

Sample
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, and let
S=\{i\,|\,i\in T\wedge\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{*}\}
. For each
i\in S
, measure register
{\cal X}_{i}
in basis
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{*}
to get outcome
x_{i}
.

•

Let
\mathbf{x}_{S}
be the concatenation of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in S}
. Output
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{x}_{S}^{*})
.

This quantum sampling game corresponds to the execution in either hybrid, where register
{\cal X}
is the register sent by
\mathsf{R}^{*}
in its first message,
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
represent the values extracted by running
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{Ext}
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
respresents the bases sampled by the sender.
By
Definition

3.11
, the quantum error probability
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}
of the above game corresponds to the trace distance between the initial state on the register
{\cal X}
and an “ideal” state, where it holds with certainty that register
{\cal X}
is in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\btheta^*}}
for
\mathbf{x}
s.t.
|\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{\overline{T}})-\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S})|<\delta
. In the following we find a bound
\epsilon_{\mathsf{quantum}}^{\delta}
and show that given the state on register
{\cal X}
is in the ideal state described above, the two hybrids are statistically indistinguishable.

SubClaim B.5
.

\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}
when instantiated with the XOR extractor (with
\delta=0.1183
) or with the ROM extractor (with
\delta=0.0267
).

Proof.

Using
3.12
,
\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{quantum}}
can be bound by the square root of the classical error probability,
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}
, of the corresponding classical sampling game, described as follows:

•

Given a string
\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, sample
T\subseteq[n]
as a uniform random subset of size
k
.

•

Sample a subset
S\subseteq T
as follows: sample bits
\mathbf{b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let set
S=\{i\,|\,i\in T\wedge\mathbf{b}_{i}=1\}
.

•

Output
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})
.

Since setting
S
as above is equivalent to choosing a random subset of
T
(chosen uniformly among all possible subsets of
T
), using the analysis in
Section

D.2
, we get, for
0<\beta<1
and
0<\eta<\delta
,

\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{2}\eta^{2}k\right)+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\eta)^{2}(1-\beta){k}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right).

For the case of XOR extractor, for
\delta=0.1183,\beta=0.051,\eta=0.081
, we have each of the expressions inside the exp terms above bounded by
\frac{1}{2^{2\lambda}}
, giving us
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}\leq\frac{5}{2^{2\lambda}}
, which means
\epsilon_{\mathsf{quantum}}^{\delta}\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}
.

And for the case of ROM extractor, for
\delta=0.0267,\beta=0.01588,\eta=0.01538
, we achieve the same bounds and get
\epsilon_{\mathsf{quantum}}^{\delta}\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}
.
∎

SubClaim B.6
.

Given the state on register
{\cal X}
is in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\btheta^*}}
s.t.
|\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{\overline{T}})-\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S})|<\delta
, where
S=\{i\,|\,i\in T\ \wedge\ \widehat{\theta}_{i}=\theta_{i}^{*}\}
, the following holds:

•

When instantiated with the XOR extractor and
\delta=0.1183,A=1100,B=500
,

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}

•

When instantiated with ROM extractor and
\delta=0.0267,A=11\ 000,B=12\ 000
,

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{4q}{2^{18\lambda}}

Proof.

Note that the checks performed by the sender once
\mathsf{R}^{*}
sends the openings in the third round correspond to checking if
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S})=0
. If this check fails, the sender aborts and the hybrids are perfectly indistinguishable. So it suffices to analyze the states on
{\cal X}
that are in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\btheta^*}}
s.t.
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S})=0
and
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{\overline{T}})<\delta
.

Recall that in either hybrid, for
i\in\overline{T}
, the sender chooses bit
\widehat{\theta}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and measures register
{\cal X}_{i}
in basis
\widehat{\theta}_{i}
. Using Hoeffding’s inequality (stated in
Appendix

D
), the number of positions
i\in\overline{T}
s.t.
\widehat{\theta}_{i}\neq\theta_{i}^{*}
is at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{2}
except with probability
\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}(n-k)}{2}\right)
. Hence, given any partition
(I_{0},I_{1})
of
\overline{T}
that
\mathsf{R}^{*}
provides in the third round, it holds that there exists a bit
b
and partition
I_{1-b}
s.t. there are at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{4}
positions
i
with
\widehat{\theta}_{i}\neq\theta_{i}^{*}
except with probability
\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}(n-k)}{2}\right)
. Call this subset of positions in
I_{1-b}
as
M
. Also, note that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, the bit
b
used by
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
is the same bit used above.

•

XOR extractor
: For the case of XOR extractor, for
\epsilon=0.053,n-k=B\lambda,B=500
, this probability above
\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}(n-k)}{2}\right)<\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}
.

Combining the two parts above, we have that register
{\cal X}_{M}=\{{\cal X}_{i}\}_{i\in M}
is in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\btheta^*_M}}
s.t.
\Delta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{M})<\frac{\delta(n-k)}{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)/4}=\frac{4\delta}{1-\epsilon}\approx 0.49968<\frac{1}{2}
(for
\delta=0.1183,\epsilon=0.053
). Using
Theorem

4.1
, it then follows that
m_{1-b}
is uniformly random string, hence proving the given claim.

•

ROM extractor
: For the case of ROM extractor, for
\epsilon=0.01013,n-k=B\lambda,B=13\,500
, this probability above
\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}(n-k)}{2}\right)<\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}
.

In a similar way as above, we have that register
{\cal X}_{M}=\{{\cal X}_{i}\}_{i\in M}
is in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\btheta^*_M}}
s.t.
\Delta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{M})<\frac{\delta(n-k)}{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)/4}=\frac{4\delta}{1-\epsilon}<0.10796
(for
\delta=0.0267,\epsilon=0.010748
).
We now apply
Theorem

4.2
with random oracle input size
n-k
, register
{\cal X}
of size
|M|
, and
|L|\leq 2^{h_{b}(0.10796)(1-\epsilon)(n-k)/4}
. Note that, when applying this theorem, we are fixing the outcome of the
n-k-|M|
bits of the random oracle input that are measured in basis
{\theta}^{*}_{i}
, and setting
{\cal X}
to contain the
|M|
registers are measured in basis
\widehat{\theta}_{i}=\theta^{*}_{i}\oplus 1
. This gives a bound of

\displaystyle\frac{4\cdot q\cdot 2^{h_{b}(0.10796)(1-\epsilon)(n-k)/4}}{2^{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)/8}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4q}{2^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-h_{b}(0.10796)\right)(1-\epsilon)(n-k)/4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4q}{2^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-h_{b}(0.10796)\right)(1-\epsilon)B\lambda/4}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4q}{2^{18\lambda}}

for
B=12\ 000,\epsilon=0.010748
.

∎

∎

Receiver Security.

Let
\mathsf{SimEqu}=(\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open})
be the simulator for the equivocal commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{{H_{C}}},\mathsf{Open}^{{H_{C}}},\mathsf{Rec}^{{H_{C}}})
(according to
Definition

5.3
). We describe the simulator
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{S}^{*}]
against a malicious receiver
\mathsf{S}^{*}
.
\mathsf{Sim}
will answer random oracle queries to
{H_{C}}
using
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
.
Additionally, if randomness extractor
E
in the protocol is
H_{Ext}
, then its simulation is accomplished via queries to an on-the-fly random oracle simulator
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
as mentioned in Imported Theorem
3.6
.

The Simulator.

1.

Prepare
n
EPR pairs on registers
\{{\cal S}_{i},{\cal R}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}

2.

Compute the commitments strings
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
by calling
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
for the underlying commitment scheme.

3.

Send
\{{\cal S}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
to
S^{*}
.

4.

Receive
T,\{\widehat{\theta_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
from
S^{*}
.

5.

For all
i\in T
, sample
\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{+,\times\}
and measure
{\cal R}_{i}
in the basis
\theta_{i}
to obtain outcome
x_{i}
. For all
i\in\overline{T}
, measure
{\cal R}_{i}
in the basis
\widehat{\theta_{i}}
to obtain outcome
\widetilde{x_{i}}
.

6.

Call
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}(\{x_{i},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in[T]})
of the underlying commitment scheme to obtain openings
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in[T]}
.

7.

Generate a partition
I_{0},I_{1}
of
\overline{T}
as follows: for every
i\in\overline{T}
, flip a random bit
b_{i}
and place
i\in I_{b_{i}}
.

8.

Receives
m_{0},m_{1}
from
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}}
functionality.

9.

Set
r_{0}=E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{0}})\oplus m_{0}
,
r_{1}=E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{1}})\oplus m_{1}
.

10.

Send
I_{0},I_{1},\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T},(r_{0},r_{1})
to
S^{*}
.

Analysis.

Fix any adversary
\{\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda}
is a QIOM that corrupts the sender,
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
is a QOM, and
(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})
is the input of the honest receiver.
For any receiver input
b_{\lambda}\in\{0,1\},m_{\lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{v}
, where
v
is the output lenght of extractor, consider the random variables
\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})]
and

\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}^{\mathsf{OT}}}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})]

according to Definition
3.2
for the protocol in Figure
9
.
Let
q(\cdot)
denote an upper bound on the number of queries of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
.
We will show that :

\bigg|\Pr[\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})]=1]-\Pr[\widetilde{\Pi}_{\cal F}[\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})]=1]\bigg|=\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))

where the term on the right corresponds to the security error in the equivocal commitment.

This is done via a sequence of hybrids, as follows
17
17

17

If randomness extractor
E
in the protocol is
H_{Ext}
, then there will be an additonal hybrid between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
where we switch from using
H_{Ext}
to simulating it using an efficient on-the-fly random oracle simulator
\mathsf{Sim}_{\mathsf{RO}}.\mathsf{RO}
as mentioned in Imported Theorem
3.6
. This hybrid’s (perfect) indistinguishability will follow directly from the indistinguishable simulation property as mentioned in Imported Theorem
3.6
.
:

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}:
The output of this hybrid is the
real
distribution
\Pi[\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})]
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}:

This is the same as the previous hybrid except that instead of running the
\mathsf{Com}
and
\mathsf{Open}
algorithm, as in Figure
9
, the challenger now answers random oracle queries to
{H_{C}}
using
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{RO}
, the random oracle simulator for the commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
. Additionally, it performs the following modified steps on behalf of
\mathsf{R}
:

–

Prepare the commitments by calling
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com}
for the underlying commitment protocol.

–

Prepare the opening by calling
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}(\{x_{i},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
, where
\{x_{i},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
are as defined in the previous hybrid.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}:

This is the same as the previous hybrid except the following change: in protocol round 1, the challenger calls
Algorithm EPR-to-BB84
(i)
for every
i\in[n]
to obtain
\ket{x_i}_{\theta_{i}}
.

Algorithm EPR-to-BB84
(i)
:

1.

Sample EPR pair on registers
{\cal S}_{i},{\cal R}_{i}
.

2.

Randomly sample a basis
\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{+,\times\}

3.

Measure
{\cal R}_{i}
in the basis
\theta_{i}
and let the outcome be
x_{i}

4.

Use
{\cal S}_{i}
as a BB84 state
\ket{x_i}_{\theta_{i}}

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:
This is the same as the previous hybrid, except that in protocol round 1, the challenger sends halves of
n
EPR pairs
\{{\cal S}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, prepared by executing Step 1 of the algorithm EPR-to-BB84, to
\mathsf{S}^{*}
while retaining
\{{\cal R}\}_{i\in[n]}
with itself.
After round 2, the challenger runs Steps 2 and 3 of the Algorithm EPR-to-BB84 for every
i\in[n]
to obtain
\{x_{i},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. The resulting values
\{x_{i},\theta_{i}\}_{i\in T}
are used as inputs to
\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open}
to prepare openings in round 3. Step 4 of the Algorithm EPR-to-BB84
(i)
is not relevant in this hybrid.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:
This is the same as the previous hybrid, except the following changes. After round 2, the challenger runs the Steps 2-3 of algorithm EPR-to-BB84 for every
i\in[T]
, leaving
\{{\cal R}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
unmeasured.
It generates a partition
I_{0},I_{1}
of
\overline{T}
as follows: for every
i\in\overline{T}
, flip a random bit
b_{i}
and place
i\in I_{b_{i}}
.

For all
i\in\overline{T}
, the challenger measures
\{{\cal R}_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
in the basis
\{\widehat{\theta_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
where
\{\widehat{\theta_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
was obtained from
\mathsf{S}^{*}
in round
2
. Denote measurement outcomes by
\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in\overline{T}}
.
Using the resulting outcomes, the challenger sets
r_{b}:=E(\{\widetilde{x}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{b}})\oplus m
.

•

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}:
This is the same as the previous hybrid, except that in Round 3, the challenger sets
r_{0}=E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{0}})\oplus m_{0}
,
r_{1}=E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{1}})\oplus m_{1}
where
m_{0},m_{1}
are received from
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}}

The output of this experiment is identical to the
ideal
distribution
\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}}}[\allowbreak\mathsf{Sim}_{\lambda},\mathsf{D}_{\lambda},(b_{\lambda},m_{\lambda})]
.

We show
that
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]|\leq\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
, where
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
is a
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
-equivocal bit commitment scheme, where
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
is a
\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
-equivocal bit commitment scheme, where
\mu(\lambda,q,n_{\mathsf{com}})=\frac{2qn_{\mathsf{com}}^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}
for the specific commitment scheme that we construct in Section
5.2
, where
n_{\mathsf{com}}
is the number of bit commitments and
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}
is the security parameter for the commitment scheme. Later, we will set
n_{\mathsf{com}}=c_{1}\lambda
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=c_{2}\lambda
for some fixed constants
c_{1},c_{2}
. Thus
\mu
will indeed be a function of
\lambda
and
q
. We now procced with the proof by arguing the computational indistinguishability of each pair of consecutive hybrids in the above sequence.

Claim B.7
.

|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]|\leq\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
.

Proof.

Suppose there exists an adversary
\mathsf{Adv}_{\lambda}
corrupting
\mathsf{S}
, a distinguisher
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}
, quantuam states
\rho_{\lambda},\sigma_{\lambda}
and a bit
b
such that,

\bigg|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]\bigg|>\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))

We will construct a reduction
\{\mathsf{Adv}^{*}_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda},\allowbreak\mathsf{D}^{*}_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that makes at most
q(\lambda)
queries to the random oracle, and contradicts the
\mu
-equivocality of the underlying commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
as defined in Definition
5.4
. In the following reduction, all random oracle queries to
{H_{C}}
will be answered by the equivocal commitment challenger.

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{RCommit},\lambda}(\rho_{\lambda})
:

•

Initalize the OT protocol with between honest receiver
\mathsf{R}
and
\mathsf{Adv}(\rho_{\lambda})
corrupting
\mathsf{S}
.

•

After
\mathsf{R}
samples
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
, output the intermediate state
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,1}
representing the joint state of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
along with
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
.

The commitment challenger obtains
\{(x_{i},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
and returns a set of commitments
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ROpen},\lambda}(\rho^{*}_{\lambda,1},\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]})
: Use
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,1}
to initialize the joint state of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
, and
\{\mathsf{com}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
as commitments of
\mathsf{R}
in the protocol. Output the new joint state
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,2}
after
\mathsf{R}
has computed
I_{0}
and
I_{1}
.

The challenger returns
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
which is then fed to the following distinguisher (along with the information
\{\mathsf{com}_{i},(x_{i},\theta_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
from the aforementioned execution).

\mathsf{D}^{*}_{\lambda}(\rho^{*}_{\lambda,2},\{\mathsf{com}_{i},(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}):

•

Use
\rho^{*}_{\lambda,2}
to initialize the joint state of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{R}
. Run it until completion using
\{(x_{i},u_{i})\}_{i\in T}
as openings of
\mathsf{R}
.

•

Let
\tau_{\lambda}^{*}
be the final state of
\mathsf{Adv}
and
y^{*}
be the output of
\mathsf{R}
. Run
\mathsf{D}_{\lambda}(\sigma_{\lambda},\tau^{*}_{\lambda},y^{*})
and output the bit
b
returned by it.

By construction, when the challenger executes
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}})
, the reduction will generate a distribution identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
. Similarly, when the challenger executes
(\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Com},\mathsf{SimEqu}.\mathsf{Open})
algorithms, the reduction will generate a distribution identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Therefore, the reduction directly contradicts the
\mu
-equivocality of the underlying commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Com}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Open}^{H_{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Rec}^{H_{C}})
according to Definition
5.4
, as desired.

∎

Claim B.8
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]

Proof.

The only difference between the two hybrids is a syntactic change in the way BB84 states are sampled in round
1
. The distribution
(x_{i},\theta_{i},\ket{x_i}_{\theta_{i}})_{i\in[16\lambda]}
resulting from these syntactically different sampling strategies is identical in both hybrids.
∎

Claim B.9
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]

Proof.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
constitutes a purification of the receiver’s strategy in round
1
and since actions on disjoint subsystems commute, this does not affect the joint distribution of the sender’s view and receiver output.
∎

Claim B.10
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]

Proof.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
constitutes a purification of the receiver’s strategy in round
3
and since actions on disjoint subsystems commute, this does not affect the joint distribution of the sender’s view and receiver output.
∎

Claim B.11
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]

Proof.

Assuming correctness of
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}}
, the two hybrids are identical.Suppose ideal world receiver’s input is
(b=0,m)
. In this case,
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{ROT}}
sends
m_{0}=m,m_{1}
to the challenger where
m_{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{v}
. In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
, this would lead to
r_{0}=E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{0}})\oplus m
(which is same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
) and
r_{1}=E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{1}})\oplus m_{1}
(which is uniformly random as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
). Moreover, the output on sender side in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
is
(E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{0}})\oplus r_{0},E(\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{1}})\oplus r_{1})
=
(m,m_{1})
as desired.
The case when ideal world receiver bit
b
is
1
can be proved in a similar way. Therefore for any fixing of the adversary’s state and receiver’s input, the two hybrids result in identical distributions.
∎

Combining all the claims, we get that
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]|\leq\mu(\lambda,q(\lambda))
. In Theorem
5.12
, we derived
\mu(\lambda,q,n_{\mathsf{com}})=\frac{2qn_{\mathsf{com}}^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}
. We will now state the parameters for
n_{\mathsf{com}}
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}
.

•

XOR extractor
: Plugging
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=2\lambda
,
n_{\mathsf{com}}=2n
(as we are committing to 2 bits at a time) where
n=1600\lambda
(this setting of
n
is the same as that needed in the sender security part of the proof), we get
\frac{114q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{\lambda}}
security against a malicious sender.

•

ROM extractor
: Plugging
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=2\lambda
,
n_{\mathsf{com}}=2n
(as we are committing to 2 bits at a time) where
n=23\ 000\lambda
(this setting of
n
is the same as that needed in the sender security part of the proof), we get
\frac{430q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{\lambda}}
security against a malicious sender.

B.2
Four-round chosen-input OT

In this section, we prove
Theorem

B.2
.

Sender Security

The proof of this follows along a similar line as the proof of security against a malicious receiver for the 3-round protocol described before. We only describe the changes to the corresponding proof from before over here.

The only change to the simulator (compared to
\mathsf{Sim}[\mathsf{R}^{*}]
for the 3-round protocol described earlier) is that after computing
b
at the end of third round, it sends
b
to
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[\lambda]}
to receive back
m_{b}
, sets
m_{1-b}\coloneqq 0^{\lambda}
, and thereafter completes the protocol as in the honest sender strategy. Once it outputs
\mathsf{R}^{*}
’s state, it continues answering distinguisher’s queries using
\mathsf{SimExt}.\mathsf{RO}
.

The hybrids (
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
) also remain same as in the proof before, and the indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
proceeds as before. The indistiguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
follows using a slightly modified analysis of
B.5
and
B.6
. Specifically, for the proof of
B.5
, we use
\delta=0.04,\beta=0.023,\eta=0.0236,A=5300,B=5000
, and obtain the same result of
\epsilon_{\mathsf{quantum}}^{\delta}\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}
.

For the proof of
B.5
, we use a different analysis as follows: set
\epsilon=0.017
. By assumption of the subclaim and using a similar analysis as the proof of
B.5
, the state on
{\cal X}
is in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\btheta^*}}
s.t.
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{S},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{S})=0
and
\Delta(\mathbf{x}_{\overline{T}},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{\overline{T}})<\delta
. Using Hoeffding’s inequality, the number of positions
i\in\overline{T}
s.t.
\widehat{\theta}_{i}\neq\theta_{i}^{*}
is at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{2}
except with probability
\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}(n-k)}{2}\right)
. For
\epsilon=0.053,n-k=B\lambda,B=5000
, this probability is
<\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}
. Next, as before, given any partition
(I_{0},I_{1})
of
\overline{T}
that
\mathsf{R}^{*}
sends in the third round, it holds that there exists a bit
b
and partition
I_{1-b}
s.t. there are at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{4}
positions
i
with
\widehat{\theta}_{i}\neq\theta_{i}^{*}
.

Hence,
{\cal X}_{I_{1-b}}
is in a superposition of states
\ket{\bx_{\left(\btheta^*_{I_{1-b}}\right)}}
s.t.
\Delta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{I_{1-b}})<\delta
and at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{4}
positions of it are measured in basis
\widehat{\theta}_{i}\neq\theta_{i}^{*}
. Let
\widehat{X}_{1-b}
be the string obtained by concatenating the measurement outcomes of
I_{1-b}
. Also, let
{\cal C}
denote the register for the complete system (including the private state of
\mathsf{R}^{*}
), but excluding register
{\cal X}
. Using
3.9
, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\widehat{X}_{1-b}\,|\,{\cal X}_{I_{b}},{\cal C})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{4}-h_{b}(\delta)|I_{1-b}|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-\epsilon)(n-k)}{4}-h_{b}(\delta)(n-k)

For
\epsilon=0.017,\delta=0.04,n-k=B\lambda,B=5000
, we get,
\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\widehat{X}_{1-b}\,|\,{\cal X}_{I_{b}},{\cal C})\geq 17\lambda
, and also, that
\mathbf{H}_{\infty}(\widehat{X}_{1-b}\,|\,\widehat{X}_{I_{b}},{\cal C})\geq 17\lambda
. Using
3.8
, we then get that
(s,h(s,\widehat{X}_{I_{1-b}}))
is
\frac{1}{2^{9\lambda}}
statistically close to uniformly random string.
Hence,
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{2^{9\lambda}}
.

Receiver Security

The proof of this is similar to the proof of receiver security for the 3 round random basis protocol described before. We only describe the changes here. The only change to the simulator is the following: Instead of executing Steps 8-10, it computes
\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}}
as the concatenation of
\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{0}},\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{1}}
respectively. It sends
I_{0},I_{1},\{(x_{i},\theta_{i}),u_{i}\}_{i\in T}
to
\mathsf{S}
. On receiving
s,ct_{0},ct_{1}
from
\mathsf{S}
in Round 4, it extracts
m_{0}:=ct_{0}\oplus h(s,\widetilde{X_{0}})
,
m_{1}:=ct_{1}\oplus h(s,\widetilde{X_{1}})
, and sends
m_{0},m_{1}
to
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}}
.

The hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{2},\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
remain same as before. In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
, instead of setting
r_{b}
, the challenger just outputs
m_{b}:=ct_{b}\oplus h(s,\{\widetilde{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I_{b}})
after Round 4. In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
, instead of setting
r_{0},r_{1}
, the challenger extracts
m_{0}:=ct_{0}\oplus h(s,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I_{0}})
,
m_{1}:=ct_{1}\oplus h(s,\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I_{1}})
after Round 4, and sends
m_{0},m_{1}
to
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}}
. The proof of indistinguishability between each pair of hybrids is similar to the prior proof.

The only security loss in the proof is between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
(when we invoke the equivocality of the underlying commitment scheme). Using Theorem
5.12
where we derived
\mu(\lambda,q,n_{\mathsf{com}})=\frac{2qn_{\mathsf{com}}^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}
and plugging
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=2\lambda
,
n_{\mathsf{com}}=2n
(as we are committing to 2 bits at a time) where
n=10\ 300\lambda
(this setting of
n
is the same as that needed in the sender security part of the proof), we get
\frac{288q\sqrt{\lambda}}{2^{\lambda}}
security against a malicious sender.

Appendix C
Three round chosen input bit OT via the XOR extractor

In this section, we derive parameters required when using a seedless XOR extractor in place of a universal hash function, in Protocol
8
.

Theorem C.1

(Three round chosen input bit OT.)
.

Consider Protocol
8
and modify it to use the XOR extractor in place of the universal hash function. In addition, instantiate the protocol with any non-interactive commitment scheme that is
extractable
(
Definition

5.3
) and
equivocal
(
Definition

5.4
). Then there exist constants
A,B
such that Protocol
8
(modified to use XOR extractor) securely realizes (
Definition

3.2
)
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[1]}
.

Furthermore, letting
\lambda
be the security parameter,
q
be an upper bound on the total number of random oracle queries made by the adversary, and using the commitment scheme from
Section

5.2
with security parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
, for constants
A=800,B=800
, Protocol
8
(modified to use XOR extractor) securely realizes
{\cal F}_{\mathsf{OT}[1]}
with
\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}
-security against a malicious receiver and
\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}
-security against a malicious sender, where

\mu_{\mathsf{R}^{*}}=\frac{3\sqrt{10}q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}+\frac{148(q+4800\lambda+1)^{3}+1}{2^{4\lambda}}+\frac{38400q\lambda}{2^{2\lambda}},\quad\mu_{\mathsf{S}^{*}}=\frac{80\sqrt{3}q\lambda}{2^{2\lambda}}.

This requires a total of
2(A+B)\lambda=3200\lambda
BB84 states.

Proof.

The proof of this proceeds along the same line as that of Protocol
8
. We only describe the changes here.

Sender security

We define the same hybrids as used in the proof of sender security of Protocol
8
, and the proof of the indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{2},\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
proceeds along the same way. For the proof of indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
as well, the proof proceeds similarly except that the proof of some subclaims change. Specifically,
7.11
now proves that for
A=800,B=800,q\geq 5
,
\Tr\left(\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.245}\tau\right)\leq\frac{45q^{3}}{2^{2\lambda}}
. In particular, in the proof of
7.11
we get
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}\leq\frac{7}{2^{\lambda}}
assuming
\delta=0.245,\epsilon=0.08326,\beta=0.123,\gamma=0.152,k=A\lambda,n=(A+B)\lambda,A=800,B=800
.

As in that proof then, by gentle measurement (
Lemma

3.1
), the
\tau
defined in
7.11
is within trace distance
\frac{3\sqrt{10}q^{3/2}}{2^{\lambda}}
of a state
\tau_{\mathsf{good}}
in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.245}
. And now conditioned on
\tau
being in the image of
{\mathbb{I}}-\Pi_{\mathsf{bad}}^{0.245}
, and
A=800,B=800
, we show that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]
.

To prove this, as in the proof of
7.12
, we have registers
{\cal S}_{W}
are in a superposition of states
\ket{\br_{\widetilde{\btheta}_{W[1]}}}
, where
\Delta\left(\mathbf{r},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{W}\right)<0.245
. Recalling that
{\cal S}_{W}=\{{\cal S}_{i,d_{i}\oplus b\oplus 1}\}_{i\in\overline{T}\setminus U}
, we have, for a majority of
i\in\overline{T}\setminus U
, register
{\cal S}_{i,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1}
is measured in basis
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1
. Call these set of registers that are measured in basis
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\oplus 1
as
M
. We then have that registers
{\cal S}_{M}
are in superposition of states
\ket{\br_{\widetilde{\btheta}_{M}}}
, where
\Delta\left(\mathbf{r},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{M}\right)\leq\frac{0.245\cdot|\overline{T}\setminus U|}{|M|}\leq\frac{0.245\cdot|\overline{T}\setminus U|}{|\overline{T}\setminus U|/2}=2\cdot 0.245=0.49<\frac{1}{2}
.

Hence, using
Theorem

4.1
it then follows that the measured bit is a uniformly random bit.

Receiver Security

This proceeds in the same way as the proof of receiver security. The only difference is the security loss incurred during in the indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. As before,
|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq\frac{2qn_{\mathsf{com}}^{1/2}}{2^{\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}/2}}
. Plugging
\lambda_{\mathsf{com}}=4\lambda
,
n_{\mathsf{com}}=3n
(as we are committing to 3 bits at a time) where
n=1600\lambda
(this setting of
n
is the same as that needed in the sender security part of the proof), we get
\frac{80\sqrt{3}q\lambda}{2^{2\lambda}}
security against a malicious sender.
∎

Appendix D
Classical sampling strategies

We analyze some common sampling strategies to find their classical error probability,
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}
in this section.
Before doing so, we recall Hoeffding’s inequality, which we make extensive use of below.

Hoeffding’s inequality

Let
X_{1},\dots X_{n}
be independent bounded random variables with
X_{i}\in[a,b]
for all
i
, where
-\infty<a\leq b<\infty
. Let
X=\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{i}
. Then, for
\epsilon>0
,

\Pr[X\geq\expectation[X]+\epsilon]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{n(b-a)^{2}}\right),\,\Pr[X\leq\expectation[X]-\epsilon]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{n(b-a)^{2}}\right)

D.1
Random subset without replacement

This corresponds to sampling
T\subseteq[n]
of size
k
uniformly at random without replacement and outputting
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})
. Then, for
0<\delta<1
,
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{2}\delta^{2}k\right)

[
BF10
, Appendix B.1]
.

D.2
Random subset without replacement, using only part of the sample

This corresponds to sampling a set
T\subseteq[n]
of size
k
without replacement, then sampling
S\subseteq T
uniformly at random among all possible subsets of
T
and outputting
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})
. We provide a tighter analysis of this compared to
[
BF10
, Appendix B.4]
.

Lemma D.1
.

For
0<\delta,\beta<1
and
0<\eta<\delta
,

\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{2}\eta^{2}k\right)+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\eta)^{2}(1-\beta){k}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{T,S}[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T}})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})|\geq\delta]

We have using the sampling strategy above, for
0<\eta<1
,

\displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{T}[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T}})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})|\geq\eta]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{2}\eta^{2}k\right)

(8)

In the following, given a string
\mathbf{q}
, we find a bound on
\Pr_{S}[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})|\geq\gamma]
. Relating this to the above, we will get the final bound. Conditioning on the size of
S
being
s
, the sampling of
S
corresponds to sampling a uniform subset of size
s
.
We have the following subclaim:

SubClaim D.2
.

\Pr_{S}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})|\geq\gamma\,\big|\,|S|=s\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-2\gamma^{2}s\right)

Proof.

We find a bound using Hoeffding’s inequality applied to sampling
S
with replacement (sampling
S
without replacement will only be tighter). For each
i\in[s]
, let
Y_{i}=1
if the
i^{th}
drawn element of
S
is
1
. Let
Y=\sum_{i\in[s]}Y_{i},\overline{Y}=Y/s
. Then, using Hoeffding’s inequality, since
Y_{i}
are independent bounded random variables, for
\gamma^{\prime}>0
,
\Pr[|Y-\expectation[Y]|\geq\gamma^{\prime}]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2\gamma^{\prime 2}}{s}\right)
or
\Pr[|\overline{Y}-\expectation[\overline{Y}]|\geq\gamma]\leq 2\exp\left(-{2\gamma^{2}s}\right)
for
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}/s
. Since,
\expectation[\overline{Y}]=\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})
, we have for
0<\gamma<1
,
\Pr\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})|\geq\gamma\,|\,|S|=s\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-{2\gamma^{2}s}\right)
.
∎

Using the distribution of
|S|
, we have,

SubClaim D.3
.

For
0<\gamma,\beta<1
,

\Pr_{S}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})|\geq\gamma\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-2\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)\frac{k}{2}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right)
.

Proof.

To find the distribution of
|S|
, note that sampling
S
corresponds to choosing each element of
T
at random with probability
1/2
. For
i\in[k]
, let
X_{i}=1
if
i^{th}
element is chosen to be part of the set, and let
X=\sum_{i\in[k]}X_{i}
. Then,
\Pr[X_{i}=1]=1/2
and each
X_{i}
is an independent bounded random variable. Using Hoeffding’s inequality, for
\beta^{\prime}>0
,
\Pr[X\leq\expectation[X]-\beta^{\prime}]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2\beta^{\prime 2}}{k}\right)
. Setting
\beta^{\prime}=\expectation[X]\beta
, we get,
\Pr[X\leq(1-\beta)\expectation[X]]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2\beta^{2}(\expectation[X])^{2}}{k}\right)
. Since
\beta^{\prime}>0
, we have
\beta>0
. In particular, for
0<\beta<1
, we have,
\Pr_{S}[|S|\leq(1-\beta)\frac{k}{2}]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right)
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})|\geq\gamma\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\leq(1-\beta)k/2}\Pr\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})|\geq\gamma\,\big|\,|S|=s\right]\Pr[|S|=s]

\displaystyle+\sum_{s>(1-\beta)k/2}\Pr\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{T})|\geq\gamma\,\big|\,|S|=s\right]\Pr[|S|=s]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right)+\sum_{s>(1-\beta)k/2}2\exp\left(-2\gamma^{2}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right)+2\exp\left(-2\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)\frac{k}{2}\right)

∎

Combining the above with
Eq.

8
, we get, for any string
\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T}})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})|\geq\eta+\gamma\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{2}\eta^{2}k\right)

\displaystyle+2\exp\left(-2\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)\frac{k}{2}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right).

For
\delta=\eta+\gamma
, we get, for
0<\delta<1,0<\eta<\delta
and
0<\beta<1
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T}})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S})|\geq\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{2}\eta^{2}k\right)

\displaystyle+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\eta)^{2}(1-\beta){k}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}k}{2}\right).

∎

D.3
Intersection of two uniform subsets and then using part of the sample

This corresponds to sampling two independent uniform subsets
T,U\subseteq[n]
, each of size
k
, setting
S=T\cap U
, and then taking a random subset of
S
(among all possible subsets of
S
). This is the strategy followed in our 3 round chosen input OT protocol (
Section

7
). In terms of the sampling strategy definition in
Section

3.6
, the above strategy can be thought of as the following sampling strategy
\Psi
:

•

P_{T^{\prime}}
: Sample two independent and uniform subsets of
[n]
each of size
k
. Let
s
denote their intersection size. Fix
s
, and discard the subsets themselves.
Sample a random subset
T^{\prime}
of
[n]
, of size
2k-s
, and output
T^{\prime}
.

•

P_{S^{\prime}}
: Given
T^{\prime}
, reverse calculate
s
as
2k-|T^{\prime}|
. Sample a uniformly random subset
S
of
T^{\prime}
, of size
s
. Sample a uniformly random subset
S^{\prime}
of
S
(among all possible subsets of
S
). Output
S^{\prime}
.

•

f(T^{\prime},\mathbf{q}_{T^{\prime}},S^{\prime})
: Output
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})
.

We prove then the following:

Lemma D.4
.

For
0<\epsilon,\beta,\delta<1
and
0<\gamma<\delta
,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}(\Psi)\leq

\displaystyle 2\exp\left(-2\left(\frac{(n-k)^{2}-3\epsilon k^{2}}{(n-k)^{2}+(1-2\epsilon)k^{2}}\right)^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\epsilon)\frac{k^{2}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\gamma)^{2}(1-\beta)(1-\epsilon)\frac{k^{2}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}(1-\epsilon)k^{2}}{2n}\right)+2\exp\left(-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}k^{3}}{n^{2}}\right)

Proof.

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}(\Psi)

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{T^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{T^{\prime}},S^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{S^{\prime}}}\left[\mathbf{q}\notin B_{T^{\prime},S^{\prime}}^{\delta}\right]

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{T^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{T^{\prime}},S^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{S^{\prime}}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\right]

To relate
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}})
with
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})
, consider the following equivalent sampling strategy
\Psi^{\prime}
:

•

Sample two independent and uniform subsets of
[n]
each of size
k
. Let
s
denote their intersection size. Fix
s
, and discard the subsets themselves.
Sample a random subset
R
of
[n]
, of size
n-2(k-s)
.

•

Sample a uniformly random subset
S
of
R
, of size
s
. Sample a uniformly random subset -
S^{\prime}
, of
S
(among all possible subsets of
S
). Output
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})
.

In intuitive terms, compared to the original sampling strategy where
T,U\subseteq[n]
of size
k
each were sampled, in sampling strategy
\Psi
, set
T^{\prime}
corresponds to sampling
T\cup U
first, and then sampling
S=T\cap U
. And in the above sampling strategy,
\Psi^{\prime}
, sampling
R
corresponds to sampling
\overline{T\cup U}\cup(T\cap U)
, or in terms of sampling strategy
\Psi
it corresponds to sampling
\overline{T^{\prime}}\cup S
. Therefore,
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}})
in sampling strategy
\Psi
is equivalent to
\omega(\mathbf{q}_{R\setminus S})
in
\Psi^{\prime}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\delta}_{\mathsf{classical}}(\Psi)

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{T^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{T^{\prime}},S^{\prime}\leftarrow P_{S^{\prime}}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{\overline{T^{\prime}}})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{s,R,S,S^{\prime}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{R\setminus S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\right]

(9)

But note that given
s
, sampling
S
and
S^{\prime}
from
R
corresponds exactly the sampling analyzed in
Section

D.2
.
Therefore, using the same result, we get, for
0<\delta,\beta<1
and
0<\gamma<\delta
,

\displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{q}}\Pr_{S,S^{\prime}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{R\setminus S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\,\big|\,|S|=s\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{s}{n-2(k-s)}\right)^{2}\gamma^{2}s\right)

\displaystyle+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\gamma)^{2}(1-\beta){s}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}s}{2}\right).

We now factor in the distribution of
s
, which we analyze using Hoeffding’s inequality applied to sampling with replacement. Consider the following experiment - sample subset
T\subseteq[n]
size
k
uniformly at random. Now sample
k
elements from
[n]
with replacement and call that set
U
. Set
s=|T\cap U|
. Let for all
i\in[k]
,
X_{i}=1
iff the
i^{th}
drawn element for
U
is drawn from
T
. Then,
\Pr[X_{i}=1]=k/n
. Let
X=\sum_{i\in[k]}X_{i}
represent
s=T\cap U
.
\expectation[X]=k^{2}/n
. Since
X_{i}
are independently drawn binary random variables, applying Hoeffding’s inequality, for
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr[|X-\expectation[X]|\geq\epsilon^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(\frac{-2\epsilon'^2}{k})

\displaystyle\implies\Pr\left[\bigg|s-\frac{k^{2}}{n}\bigg|\geq\epsilon^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(\frac{-2\epsilon'^2}{k})

\displaystyle\implies\Pr\left[\bigg|s-\frac{k^{2}}{n}\bigg|\geq\frac{\epsilon k^{2}}{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(\frac{-2\epsilon^2k^3}{n^2})

where we substituted
\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\epsilon k^{2}}{n}
. Therefore,
s\in\left(\frac{(1-\epsilon)k^{2}}{n},\frac{(1+\epsilon)k^{2}}{n}\right)
except with probability
2\exp\left(\frac{-2\epsilon^{2}k^{3}}{n^{2}}\right)
for
0<\epsilon<1
.
We have then for
0<\epsilon,\beta,\delta<1
and
0<\gamma<\delta
,

\displaystyle\Pr_{s,R,S,S^{\prime}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{R\setminus S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{0}:\,|s_{0}-{k^{2}}/{n}|\geq\frac{\epsilon k^{2}}{n}}\Pr_{R,S,S^{\prime}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{R\setminus S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\,|\,s=s_{0}\right]\Pr_{s}\left[s=s_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{s_{0}:\,|s_{0}-{k^{2}}/{n}|<\frac{\epsilon k^{2}}{n}}\Pr_{R,S,S^{\prime}}\left[|\omega(\mathbf{q}_{R\setminus S})-\omega(\mathbf{q}_{S^{\prime}})|\geq\delta\,|\,s=s_{0}\right]\Pr_{s}\left[s=s_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s_{0}:\,|s_{0}-{k^{2}}/{n}|\geq\frac{\epsilon k^{2}}{n}}\Pr_{s}\left[s=s_{0}\right]+\sum_{s_{0}:\,|s_{0}-{k^{2}}/{n}|<\epsilon}2\exp\left(-2\left(1-\frac{s}{n-2(k-s)}\right)^{2}\gamma^{2}s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\gamma)^{2}(1-\beta){s}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}s}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-2\left(\frac{(n-k)^{2}-3\epsilon k^{2}}{(n-k)^{2}+(1-2\epsilon)k^{2}}\right)^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\epsilon)\frac{k^{2}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+2\exp\left(-(\delta-\gamma)^{2}(1-\beta)(1-\epsilon)\frac{k^{2}}{n}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\beta^{2}(1-\epsilon)k^{2}}{2n}\right)+2\exp\left(-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}k^{3}}{n^{2}}\right)

where we substituted the upper bound and lower bound of
s
to get the last inequality. Since this is true for any string
\mathbf{q}
, it is also true for
\max_{\mathbf{q}}
and hence using
Eq.

9
, we get
\epsilon_{\mathsf{classical}}^{\delta}
is bounded by the quantity above.
∎
